A Classification of Asymptotic Behaviors of Green Functions of Random Walks in the Quadrant
Irina Ignatiouk-Robert

Abstract
. This paper investigates the asymptotic behavior of Green functions associated to partially homogeneous random walks in the quadrant ℤ + 2 \mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2} . There are four possible distributions for the jumps of these processes, depending on the location of the starting point: in the interior, on the two positive axes of the boundary, and at the origin ( 0 , 0 ) (0,0) . With mild conditions on the positive jumps of the random walk, which can be unbounded, a complete analysis of the asymptotic behavior of the Green function of the random walk killed at ( 0 , 0 ) (0,0) is achieved. The main result is that eight regions of the set of parameters determine completely the possible limiting behaviors of Green functions of these Markov chains. These regions are defined by a set of relations for several characteristics of the distributions of the jumps. In the transient case, a description of the Martin boundary is obtained and in the positive recurrent case, our results give the exact limiting behavior of the invariant distribution of a state whose norm goes to infinity along some asymptotic direction in the quadrant. These limit theorems extend results of the literature obtained, up to now, essentially for random walks whose jump sizes are either 0 0 or 1 1 on each coordinate. Our approach relies on a combination of several methods: probabilistic representations of solutions of analytical equations, Lyapounov functions, convex analysis, methods of homogeneous random walks, and complex analysis arguments.



1.
Introduction

For a transient Markov chain
(Z(n))
on an infinite countable state space
{\mathcal{E}}
determining all possible limits of the associated Martin kernel,
the Martin boundary
, is an important and difficult problem in general. By the Poisson-Martin representation theorem, it gives the
Martin compactification
of the state space, an integral representation of all non-negative harmonic functions. For an introduction to the theory of Martin boundary for countable Markov chains, see the classical references Doob
[
5
]
and Dynkin
[
6
]
.

The characterization of the Martin boundary for homogeneous random walks in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
has been obtained in Ney and Spitzer
[
23
]
, via a set of technical estimates related to the local central limit theorem. The Martin boundary has been also identified for random walks on free groups, hyperbolic graphs and Cartesian products. See Woess
[
25
]
for a thorough presentation of boundary theory of random walks.

There are few results for more general Markov chains. For random walks on non-homogeneous trees, the Martin boundary has been obtained in Cartier
[
3
]
. Doney
[
4
]
identified the Martin boundary of homogeneous random walks
(Z(n))
on
\mathbb{Z}
killed on the negative half-line of
\mathbb{Z}
. For space-time random walks
(S(n)){=}(n,Z(n))
associated to a homogeneous random walk
Z(n)
on
\mathbb{Z}
killed on the negative half-line, the Martin boundary is obtained in Alili and Doney
[
1
]
. The proof of these results relies on the one-dimensional structure of these processes.

The Martin boundary for partially homogeneous random walks killed or reflected on a half-space or a cone of
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
has been identified with large deviation techniques, Choquet-Deny theory and ratio limit theorems of Markov-additive processes. See Ignatiouk
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
.

Random Walks on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}

In this paper we consider a partially homogeneous random walk
(Z(n))
on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
with the following characteristics: the distribution of its jumps is

a)
\mu
in the interior of
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
;    b)
\mu_{0}
at
0{=}(0,0)
;

c)
\mu_{1}
in
\{0\}{\times}(\mathbb{Z}_{+}{\setminus}\{0\})
;      d)
\mu_{2}
in
(\mathbb{Z}_{+}{\setminus}\{0\}){\times}\{0\}
;

The possible negative jumps are either
0
or
{-}1
on each coordinate for
\mu
,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
.

When it is transient, the Green function
G
of the Markov chain is, for
j
,
k{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
,

G(j,k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n){=}k).

The strict hitting time of
0{=}(0,0)
is denoted as

\tau_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\inf\{k{>}0:Z(k){=}(0,0)\}

and the Green function of the Markov chain killed at
0
is, for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
,
k{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}\{0\}
,

g(j,k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{n{\geq}0}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=k,n{<}\tau_{0}).

Exact Asymptotics of Green Functions

The Martin Kernel being the ratio of two Green functions, its limiting behavior can be obtained from the exact asymptotics of
G(j,k)
when
k
goes to infinity.
Ney and Spitzer
[
23
]
determines the Martin boundary of homogeneous random walks in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
in this way.

It is easily seen that, for
j
,
k{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}\{0\}
,

G(j,k)=g(j,k)+G(j,0)g(0,k)\text{ and }G(0,k)=G(0,0)g(0,k),

which gives a relation between the asymptotic behaviors of the Green functions
k{\to}G(j,k)
and
k{\to}g(j,k)
.

This type of asymptotic analysis can also be used to investigate positive recurrent Markov chains. The invariant distribution
\pi
is represented with the Green function of the Markov chain killed at
0
, for
k{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
,

(1.1)

\pi(k)=\pi(0)\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\left(Z(n){=}k,n{<}\tau_{0}\right)=\pi(0)g(0,k),

The limiting behavior of
k{\to}g(0,k)
gives therefore the asymptotic behavior of the invariant distribution of a state
k
going to infinity.

Convergence to Infinity

For
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
, we will investigate the asymptotic behavior of
g(j,k)
as
k{=}(k_{1},k_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
gets large in several ways.

(1)

With direction
u{\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}{=}\{x{=}(x_{1},x_{2}){\in}\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}:\|x\|{=}x_{1}^{2}{+}x_{2}^{2}{=}1\}
,

\min(k_{1},k_{2})\to{+}\infty,\quad\text{ and }\quad\frac{k}{\|k\|}\to u.

(2)

Along the axes.

The quantity
k_{2}{\in}\mathbb{Z}
is fixed and
k_{1}{\to}{+}\infty
, and the symmetrical case by exchanging the variables
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
.

The case
(1)
is the classical set of asymptotic behaviors considered in general. As we will see, the asymptotics of the Green function for the case
(2)
are different from the case
(1)
, they exhibit interesting behaviors. They have also been considered in Kobayashi and Miyazawa
[
16
]
for random walks with jumps of size
1
. Note that there is a slight abuse of terminology for
(2)
since, strictly speaking, the cases
u{=}(1,0)
and
u{=}(0,1)
of
(1)
are also “along the axes”.

A Functional Equation

A functional equation for generating functions of the Green function of the Markov chain killed at
0
plays a central role in our study. It is expressed as, for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
and
(x,y)
in a convenient subset of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

(1.2)

\bigl(1{-}P(x,y)\bigr)(H_{j}(x,y){-}H_{j}(x,0){-}H_{j}(0,y))\\
=L_{j}(x,y)+\bigl(\phi_{1}(x,y){-}1\bigr)H_{j}(x,0)+\bigl(\phi_{2}(x,y){-}1\bigr)H_{j}(0,y)

holds, where the quantity
(L_{j}(x,y))
is a known function and

(1)

(H_{j}(x,y))
is the generating function of
(g(j,k),k{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2})
;

(2)

P(x,y)
is the generating function of
\mu
at
(x,y)
, the distribution of the jumps in the interior of
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
. The quantity
Q(x,y){=}xy(1{-}P(x,y))
is in general referred to as the
kernel
;

(3)

For
i{=}1
,
2
,
\phi_{i}(x,y)
is the generating function of
\mu_{i}
at
(x,y)
.

In most of studies on the asymptotic behavior of Green functions or invariant measures of random walks in the quadrant, there is always an associated functional equation similar to equation (
1.2
).

Literature

We now give a brief presentation of the existing asymptotic results for non-homogeneous random walks in the quadrant.

(1)

Nearest neighbor random walks: Jumps of size
0
or
1
on each coordinate.

In this case, the kernel
(xy(1{-}P(x,y)))
is a polynomial of degree at most
2
in each variable. In the positive recurrent case, by using methods of complex analysis on elliptic curves, the asymptotic behavior of the invariant distribution along lines of
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
has been obtained in the early work Malyshev
[
21
]
in 1973.
Following these ideas, extensions of these results have been established in Kurkova and Malyshev
[
18
]
, Kurkova and Raschel
[
19
]
, and Li and Zhao
[
20
]
, but, as in the original paper
[
21
]
, only when positive jumps are of size
1
. See Fayolle et al.
[
8
]
for a general presentation and additional references therein.

In the positive recurrent case, Kobayashi and Miyazawa
[
16
]
determines the exact domain of convergence of the generating function of stationary distribution and obtains the asymptotic behavior of the stationary distribution.

In both cases,
[
16
]
and
[
21
]
, the analysis relies in an essential way on the explicit representation of the roots of the quadratic function
(xy(1{-}P(x,y)))
. This is the main limitation of this type of approach.

(2)

For positive recurrent random walks with unbounded positive jumps, exact asymptotics of the stationary measure have been investigated in Borovkov and Mogulskii
[
2
]
with large deviation techniques. The asymptotics are considered for interior directions of
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
. Some constants do not seem to be explicitly determined in the limit results of this reference. In particular, it is not clear how the limiting behavior of the invariant distribution of a state going to infinity depends on the asymptotic direction
u{\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
.

(3)

Positive recurrent random walks with unbounded positive jumps have also been analyzed in Kobayashi and Miyazawa
[
17
]
from the point of view of tail asymptotics. For these asymptotics, a line of
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
associated to a fixed vector is going to infinity in the sense that its distance to
(0,0)
is going to infinity. The quantity considered for the tail asymptotics is the invariant distribution of all states of
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
above this line. The exact domain of convergence of the generating function of the invariant distribution is obtained and, with methods of Markov-additive processes, exact tail asymptotics with explicit constants are derived.

(4)

Ignatiouk et al.
[
15
]
has investigated transient random walks in the quadrant
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with unbounded positive jumps, the size of negative jumps is not necessarily
1
but bounded. An additional assumption in
[
15
]
is that the random walk escapes to the infinity along the horizontal axis and the vertical axis. With methods of local Markov-additive processes and complex analysis arguments, the exact asymptotics of the Green function are obtained. They are expressed in terms of asymptotics of Green functions of random walks in half-plane, i.e. with one boundary removed. Our present paper shows that such a result is wrong in general, both boundaries play a role in fact in several of our convergence results.

A Quick Presentation

A significant part of our paper is devoted to the definition and the properties of the classification in eight regions of the space of parameters. It is defined as a set of relations for several characteristics associated to the distributions of the jumps
\mu
,
\mu_{i}
,
i{\in}\{1,2\}
. For each region of this classification, an investigation of the analyticity properties of the generating functions functions
H_{j}
,
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{+}
and the study of the nature of their dominant singularities are achieved. With these results, the exact asymptotics of the Green function
(g(j,k),j,k{\in}{\mathbb{N}}^{2})
are derived. They are stated in Theorems
3
and
4
of Section
2
.

We now give a sketch of the general method used to obtain these convergence results. Section
2
gives a much more detailed description of the contributions and also of the methods used.

A first step is of studying some solutions
(x,y)
of the equation
Q(x,y){=}xy(1{-}P(x,y)){=}0
. This is done by investigating the existence of a function
Y(x)
defined on a subset of
\mathbb{C}
such that
(x,Y(x))
is a zero of
Q
. By canceling the left-hand side of (
1.2
), it gives a relation between
(H_{j}(x,0))
and
(H_{j}(0,Y(x)))
. An analogue study is done, by exchanging the roles of
x
and
y
, with
(X(y),y)
. After this step, an analytic continuation of
(H_{j}(0,Y(x)))
is achieved and, with relation (
1.2
) it gives an analytic continuation of
(H_{j}(x,0))
. This is a difficult point and this is where a convenient representation of
(Y(x))
is crucial. In our approach, the key argument in this step is a probabilistic representation of the function
(Y(x))
. Under general conditions, an expression of the functions
(H_{j}(0,y))
and
(H_{j}(x,0))
is derived, and therefore an expression of
(H_{j}(x,y))
, the generating function of
(g(j,k))
. The last step uses this representation and, with complex analysis arguments, one can derive the asymptotic behavior of
(g(j,k))
when
k
goes to infinity.

2.
Overview of the Results

General Notations

Throughout the paper, the following notations are used.

(1)

For two points
(x_{1},y_{2})
,
(x_{2},y_{2}){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the
line segment
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the end-points
(x_{1},y_{2})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
is denoted by
[(x_{1},y_{2}),\,(x_{2},y_{2})]
.
The unit circle of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}
is

(2.1)

{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\{w{=}(u,v){\in}\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}:\|x\|=u^{2}{+}v^{2}=1\}.

(2)

For
a{\in}\mathbb{C}
and
r{>}0
, we denote by
B(a,r)
the open disk in
\mathbb{C}
with center
a
and radius
r
. A
poly-disc
of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the product of two open discs.

(3)

For
r_{2}{>}r_{1}{>}0
, we let

C(r_{1},r_{2})=\{x\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ r_{1}<|x|<r_{2}\}\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{C}(r_{1},r_{2})=\{x\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ r_{1}\leqslant|x|\leqslant r_{2}\}.

(4)

For a subset
B
of
[0,{+}x\infty[^{2}
, we denote

(2.2)

\Omega(B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\,(|x|,|y|)\in B\}.

A set
B\subset[0,{+}\infty[^{2}
is
logarithmically convex
(resp.
strictly logarithmically convex
) if, for any
x
,
y{\in}B
and
\lambda{\in}[0,1]
,
x^{\lambda}y^{1-\lambda}{\in}B
, resp.
x^{\lambda}y^{1-\lambda}{\in}\overset{\circ}{B}
when
x{\neq}y
. We denote by
\text{LogCH}(B)
the logarithmic convex hull of
B
in
[0,{+}\infty[^{2}
, i.e. the smallest logarithmically convex set of
[0,{+}\infty[^{2}
containing
B
.

To simplify some expressions, we will also use the notations, for a
{\mathcal{C}}_{2}
-function
f
on
\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{x}f(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(x,y),\quad\partial_{y}f(x,y)=\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}(x,y),

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{xx}f(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x^{2}}(x,y),\quad\partial^{2}_{xy}f(x,y)=\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial x\partial y}(x,y),\quad\partial^{2}_{yy}f(x,y)=\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial y^{2}}(x,y).

We now introduce the non-homogeneous random walks investigated in this paper.

2.1.
Non-Homogeneous Random walks in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
: Definitions and Assumptions

The process
Z(n){=}(Z_{1}(n),Z_{2}(n))
on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
is a Markov chain on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with transition probabilities given
for
j{=}(j_{1},j_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
by

(2.3)

{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(1)=j{+}k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}\mu(k)&\text{ if }j_{1}{>}0\text{ and }j_{2}{>}0,\\
\mu_{1}(k)&\text{ if }j_{1}{>}0\text{ and }j_{2}{=}0,\\
\mu_{2}(k)&\text{ if }j_{2}{>}0\text{ and }j_{1}{=}0,\\
\mu_{0}(k)&\text{ if }(j_{1},j_{2}){=}(0,0),\end{cases}

where
\mu
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and
\mu_{1}
,
\mu_{2}
and
\mu_{0}
are sub-probability measures (positive measures with total mass less or equal than
1
) on, respectively,
\mathbb{Z}{\times}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\mathbb{Z}_{+}{\times}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
.

Their generating functions, defined on their set of convergence in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, are denoted by

(2.4)

\displaystyle P(x,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mu(j)x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}},

(2.5)

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mu_{1}(j)x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}},\quad\quad\phi_{2}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mu_{2}(j)x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}},

(2.6)

\displaystyle\phi_{0}(x,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mu_{0}(j)x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}}.

The
level sets

D
,
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
of these generating functions are defined as

(2.7)

D\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\left\{(x,y)\in]0,+\infty[^{2}\penalty\ :\penalty\ P(x,y)\leqslant 1\right\}\\
\text{ and }D_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\left\{(x,y)\in]0,+\infty[^{2}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \phi_{i}(x,y)\leqslant 1\right\},\quad i{\in}\{1,2\}.

There are three main assumptions used in our results.

Assumption (A1)

(i)

The homogeneous random walk associated to the distribution
\mu
is irreducible on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
;

(ii)

The generating function
P
is finite in a neighborhood of the set
D
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
;

(iii)

The set
D
has a non-empty interior.

Assumption (A2)

(i)

For
j{=}(j_{1},j_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,
\mu(j_{1},j_{2}){=}0
if
j_{1}{<}{-}1
or
j_{2}{<}{-}1
.

Assumption (A3)

(i)

The random walk
Z(n){=}(Z_{1}(n),Z_{2}(n))
is irreducible on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
;

(ii)

The generating functions
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
and
\phi_{0}
are finite in a neighborhood of the set
D
;

(iii)

The sets
D{\cap}D_{1}
and
D{\cap}D_{2}
have a non-empty interior;

(iv)

For
j{=}(j_{1},j_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

–

\mu_{1}(j_{1},j_{2}){=}0
if
j_{1}{<}{-}1
;

–

\mu_{2}(j_{1},j_{2}){=}0
if
j_{2}{<}{-}1
;

(v)

There exists
j{=}(j_{1},j_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
j_{2}{>}0
such that
\mu_{1}(j_{1},j_{2}){>}0
;

(vi)

There exists
j{=}(j_{1},j_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
j_{1}{>}0
such that
\mu_{2}(j_{1},j_{2}){>}0
.

The Markov chain
(Z(n))
killed at
0
and its Green function are now introduced.

Definition 1

(Killed Markov Chain)
.

The return time of the process
(Z(n))
to the origin
0{=}(0,0)
is defined by

\tau_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\inf\{n\geqslant 1\penalty\ :Z(n)=(0,0)\}

and
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
denotes a process with the distribution of the Markov chain
(Z(n))
killed at
0
. Its Green function is defined by, for
j
,
k{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
,

(2.8)

g(j,k)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n)=k)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=k,\;\tau_{0}>n).

A non-negative function
\varkappa:\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\to}\mathbb{R}_{+}
is said to be harmonic for the Markov chain
(Z(n))
killed at
0
if, for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(\varkappa(Z(1));\;\tau_{0}>1)=\varkappa(j).

The next proposition introduces key quantities used to define the different regions which determine the asymptotic behavior of the Green function
(g(j,k))
. Its proof follows from Lemma
4.1
and Lemma
4.2
of Section
4
. Figures
1
,
2
,
3
and
5
in Section
C
of the appendix illustrate some of these definitions.

Proposition 2.1
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3),

(1)

the sets
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
are logarithmically convex and the set
D
is compact, strictly logarithmically convex and does not intersect with the axes
\{(x,y){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}{:}x{=}0\}
and
\{(x,y){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}{:}y{=}0\}
;

(2)

There exist
x^{*}_{P}
,
x^{**}_{P}{\in}]0,{+}\infty[
and
x^{*}
,
x^{**}\in]0,{+}\infty[
such that
x^{*}_{P}{<}x^{**}_{P}
,
x^{*}{<}x^{**}
, and

\displaystyle[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]

\displaystyle=\{x{\in}]0,+\infty[:\penalty\ \exists y{\in}]0,+\infty[,\,(x,y){\in}D\},

\displaystyle[x^{*},x^{**}]

\displaystyle=\{x{\in}]0,+\infty[:\penalty\ \exists y{\in}]0,+\infty[,\,(x,y){\in}D\cap D_{1}\}.

(3)

There exist functions
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}:[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]{\to}[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, such that, for
x{\in}[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
,
Y_{1}(x){\leqslant}Y_{2}(x)
and

[Y_{1}(x),Y_{2}(x)]=\{y{\in}]0,+\infty[\penalty\ :\penalty\ (x,y){\in}D\}.

For
x{\in}[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
,
Y_{1}(x)
,
Y_{2}(x)
are the unique positive solutions of the equation
P(x,y){=}1
, and
Y_{1}(x){=}Y_{2}(x)
holds if and only if
x{\in}\{x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}\}
.

(4)

There exist
y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}{\in}]0,+\infty[
and
y^{*},y^{**}{\in}]0,+\infty[
such that
y^{*}_{P}{<}y^{**}_{P}
,
y^{*}{<}y^{**}
, and

\displaystyle[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]

\displaystyle=\{y{\in}]0,+\infty[:\penalty\ \exists x{\in}]0,+\infty[,\,(x,y){\in}D\},

\displaystyle[y^{*},y^{**}]

\displaystyle=\{x{\in}]0,+\infty[:\penalty\ \exists x{\in}]0,+\infty[,\,(x,y){\in}D\cap D_{2}\}.

(5)

There exist functions
X_{1}
,
X_{2}{:}[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]{\to}[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
such that, for
y{\in}[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
,
X_{1}(y){\leqslant}X_{2}(y)
and

[X_{1}(y),X_{2}(y)]=\{x{\in}]0,+\infty[\penalty\ :\penalty\ (x,y){\in}D\}.

For
y{\in}[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
,
X_{1}(y)
,
X_{2}(y)
are the unique positive solutions of the equation
P(x,y){=}1
, and
X_{1}(y){=}X_{2}(y)
holds if and only if
y{\in}\{y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}\}
.

The relations
D{\cap}D_{1}{\subset}D
and
D{\cap}D_{2}{\subset}D
give the inequalities

(2.9)

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{*}<x^{**}\leqslant x_{P}^{**}\quad\text{and}\quad y^{*}_{P}\leqslant y^{*}<y^{**}\leqslant y_{P}^{**}.

Note that, since the point
(1,1)
is an element of
D{\cap}D_{1}
and
D{\cap}D_{2}
, one has also

(2.10)

x^{*}\leqslant 1\leqslant x^{**},\quad y^{*}\leqslant 1\leqslant y^{**}.

We now define four curves
{\mathcal{S}}_{11}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
,
{\mathcal{S}}_{21}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
on the boundary
\partial D
of the set
D
. They are also used in the definition of our classification.

(2.11)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{11}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\left\{(x,y)\in\partial D:\penalty\ \partial_{x}P(x,y)\leqslant 0,\;\partial_{y}P(x,y)\leqslant 0\right\},

(2.12)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{12}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\left\{(x,y)\in\partial D:\penalty\ \partial_{x}P(x,y)\leqslant 0,\;\partial_{y}P(x,y)\geqslant 0\right\},

(2.13)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{21}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\left\{(x,y)\in\partial D:\penalty\ \partial_{x}P(x,y)\geqslant 0,\>\partial_{y}P(x,y)\leqslant 0\right\},

(2.14)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{22}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\left\{(x,y)\in\partial D:\penalty\ \partial_{x}P(x,y)\geqslant 0,\;\partial_{y}P(x,y)\geqslant 0\right\}.

With Lemma
4.1
of Section
4
, these curves can be expressed in terms of the functions
X_{1}
,
X_{2}
and
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
of Proposition
2.1
as follows,

(2.15)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{11}

\displaystyle=\{(x,Y_{1}(x)):\penalty\ x\in[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{*}_{P})]\}=\{(X_{1}(y),y):\penalty\ y\in[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P})]\},

(2.16)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{12}

\displaystyle=\{(x,Y_{2}(x)):\penalty\ x\in[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})]\}=\{(X_{1}(y),y):\penalty\ y\in[Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]\},

(2.17)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{21}

\displaystyle=\{(x,Y_{1}(x)):\penalty\ x\in[X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]\}=\{(X_{2}(y),y):\penalty\ y\in[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})]\},

(2.18)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{22}

\displaystyle=\{(x,Y_{2}(x)):\penalty\ x\in[X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]\}=\{(X_{2}(y),y):\penalty\ y\in[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]\},

and the relations
X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}){=}X_{2}(y^{*}_{P})
,
X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}){=}X_{2}(y^{**}_{P})
,
Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}){=}Y_{2}(x^{*}_{P})
and
Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}){=}Y_{2}(x^{**}_{P})
hold.

2.2.
A Partition of the Space of Parameters

The next proposition shows that there is a partition of eight regions (B
a
),
a{\in}\{0,\ldots,7\}
for the possible locations of the points
x^{**}
,
Y_{1}(x^{**})
,
Y_{2}(x^{**})
,
y^{**}
,
X_{1}(y^{**})
,
X_{2}(y^{**})
. Its proof is given in Section
5
. As it will be seen, the asymptotic behavior of the Green functions of the Markov chain killed at
0
depends on the region associated to its parameters. Figures
6
,
7
,
8
and
9
in Section
C
of the appendix give an illustration of several situations.

Proposition 2.2

(Definition of the Classification)
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), one and only one of the following cases can occur,

(B0)

X_{1}(y^{**}){<}x^{**}{<}X_{2}(y^{**})
and
Y_{1}(x^{**}){<}y^{**}{<}Y_{2}(x^{**})
;

(B1)

X_{1}(y^{**}){<}X_{2}(y^{**})=x^{**}{<}x^{**}_{P}
,
Y_{1}(x^{**}){<}Y_{2}(x^{**})=y^{**}{<}y^{**}_{P}
and

(x^{**},y^{**})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
;

(B2)

X_{2}(y^{**}){<}x^{**}
,
Y_{2}(x^{**}){<}y^{**}
and
(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**})),\,(X_{2}(y^{**}),y^{**})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
;

(B3)

x^{**}=X_{1}(y^{**}){<}x^{**}_{P}
,
Y_{1}(x^{**}){<}Y_{2}(x^{**})=y^{**}
,
y^{*}{\leqslant}1{<}Y_{2}(x^{**})
and
(x^{**},y^{**})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
;

(B4)

x^{**}{<}X_{1}(y^{**}){<}x^{**}_{P}
,
Y_{1}(x^{**}){<}Y_{2}(x^{**}){<}y^{**}
,
y^{*}{\leqslant}1{<}Y_{2}(x^{**})
and

(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**})),\,(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12};

(B5)

y^{**}=Y_{1}(x^{**}){<}y^{**}_{P}
,
X_{1}(y^{**}){<}X_{2}(y^{**})=x^{**}
,
x^{*}{\leqslant}1{<}X_{2}(y^{**})
and
(x^{**},y^{**})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{21}
;

(B6)

y^{**}{<}Y_{1}(x^{**}){<}y^{**}_{P}
,
X_{1}(y^{**}){<}X_{2}(y^{**}){<}x^{**}
,
x^{*}{\leqslant}1{<}X_{2}(y^{**})
and

(X_{2}(y^{**}),y^{**}),\,(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**}))\in{\mathcal{S}}_{21};

(B7)

x^{**}=X_{1}(y^{**})=1
,
y^{**}=Y_{1}(x^{**})=1
,
\partial_{x}P(1,1){<}0
and
\partial_{y}P(1,1){<}0
.

The cases (B0)-(B7) have in fact a simple geometrical interpretation. They are determined by the location of the line segments
[(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**})),\,(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))]
and
[(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**}),\,(X_{2}(y^{**}),y^{**})]
. See Figures
5
of Section
C
of the appendix, where, see Section
5
,

–

the horizontal line segment represents
[(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**}),(X_{2}(y^{**}),y^{**})]
;

–

the vertical
line segment
"
represents

[(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**})),(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))]
.

The case (B7) corresponds to the case when the Markov chain
(Z(n))
is transient and escapes to the infinity along the each of the axes
\{0\}{\times}{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\mathbb{N}}{\times}\{0\}
. See Section
A
. In this case, the exact asymptotic of the Green function has been already obtained in the paper
[
15
]
by using methods of Markov-Additive processes. For this reason we will not consider this case.

In the literature, asymptotic results for nearest neighbor random walks in the quadrant are often formulated either under conditions of positive recurrence, see
[
8
,
20
,
21
,
22
]
, or under conditions of transience, see
[
18
]
. As it will be seen in Section
A
, the classification of Proposition
2.2
is not defined in such a way. Transience
and
recurrence properties may hold in each of the regions (B
a
),
a{\in}\{3,4,5,6\}
. See Proposition
A.1
. The recurrence/transience properties of
(Z(n))
have in fact a marginal impact in our investigation of the asymptotic behavior of the Green function
(g(j,k))
of the killed Markov chain.

2.3.
Convergence Domain and Functional Equation

For
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
, the generating function of
(g(j,k),k{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2})
, defined on its convergence domain in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, is denoted as

(2.19)

H_{j}(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}\{0\}}g(j,k)x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}.

This is a central set of functions in our analysis. A significant part of our work is devoted to the investigation of their convergence domain and also to determine the nature of the dominant singularities of the functions
x{\to}H_{j}(x,0)
and
y{\to}H_{j}(0,y)
. Once it is done, with Tauberian like theorems and complex analysis arguments, we will able to derive the asymptotic behavior of the Green function
(g(j,k),k{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2})
when
k
goes to infinity.

Our first important result is that if

(2.20)

{\Gamma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\{(x,y)\in[0,+\infty[^{2}:\penalty\ x{<}x^{\prime}\;\text{and}\;y{<}y^{\prime}\;\text{for some}\;(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in D\},

where
D
is defined by (
2.7
), and

(2.21)

\displaystyle x_{d}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}x^{**}&\text{if one of the conditions (B0){-}(B4) holds},\\
X_{2}(y^{**})&\text{if either (B5) or (B6) holds},\end{cases}

(2.22)

\displaystyle y_{d}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}y^{**}&\text{if one of the conditions (B0){-}(B2), (B5) or (B6) holds},\\
Y_{2}(x^{**})&\text{if either (B3) or (B4) holds}.\end{cases}

then, for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the generating function
(x,y){\to}H_{j}(x,y)
is analytic on

(2.23)

\Omega_{d}({\Gamma})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\penalty\ |x|{<}x_{d},\;|y|{<}y_{d}\},

In our next result, we will see that the point
x_{d}
(resp.
y_{d}
) is the dominant singularity of the functions
x{\to}H_{j}(x,0)
(resp. of the functions
y{\to}H_{j}(0,y)
) and that the set
\Omega_{d}({\Gamma})
is the maximal domain in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
where all generating functions
(x,y){\to}H_{j}(x,y)
,
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, converge.

For
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
, to formulate a key relation for the generating functions
(H_{j}(x,y))
, we define

(2.24)

Q(x,y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}xy(1-P(x,y))=xy-\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}x^{k_{1}+1}y^{k_{2}+1}\mu(k)

and

(2.25)

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(x,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}x(1-\phi_{1}(x,y))=x-\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}x^{k_{1}+1}y^{k_{2}}\mu_{1}(k),

(2.26)

\displaystyle\psi_{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}y(1-\phi_{2}(x,y))=y-\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}+1}\mu_{2}(k),

(2.27)

\displaystyle L_{j}(x,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}}-{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty)&\text{ if }(j_{1},j_{2}){\not=}(0,0),\\
\phi_{0}(x,y)-{\mathbb{P}}_{(0,0)}(\tau_{0}<+\infty)&\text{ if }(j_{1},j_{2}){=}(0,0),\end{cases}

(2.28)

\displaystyle h_{j}(x,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(j,(k_{1}+1,k_{2}+1))x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}},

(2.29)

\displaystyle h_{1j}(x)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{k_{1}=0}^{\infty}g(j,(k_{1}+1,0))x^{k_{1}},\quad\text{and}\quad h_{2j}(y)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}g(j,(0,k_{2}+1))y^{k_{2}}.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), the functions
(x,y){\to}L_{j}(x,y)
,
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
are clearly analytic in a neighborhood of the set
\Omega(\overline{\Gamma})
, see relation (
2.2
), and the functions
(x,y){\to}Q(x,y)
and
(x,y){\to}\psi_{i}(x,y)
,
i{=}1
,
2
, can be analytically continued to a neighborhood of
\Omega(\overline{\Gamma})
.

Theorem 1

(Convergence Domain and Functional Equation)
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A4), for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the following assertions hold

i)

The function
x{\to}h_{1j}(x)
, resp.
y{\to}h_{2j}(y)
, is analytic in
B(0,x_{d})
, resp. in
B(0,y_{d})
.

ii)

On the set
\Omega_{d}({\Gamma})
the function
(x,y){\to}h_{j}(x,y)
is analytic and the relation

(2.30)

Q(x,y)h_{j}(x,y)=L_{j}(x,y)+\psi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)+\psi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y)

holds.

When the random walk
(Z(n))
is positive recurrent, Theorem
1
has been established in Kobayashi and Miyazawa
[
17
]
. With Proposition
2.2
, the transience or recurrence properties do not play a role in our proof of this result.

Section
6
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
1
. We give a sketch of it.

(1)

We first prove that the series (
2.29
) and (
2.28
) converge in
\Omega(\Theta)
for a logarithmically convex set
\Theta{\subset}[0,+\infty[^{2}
whose boundary contains the line segments
[(x_{d},0),(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))]
and
[(0,y_{d}),(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})]
and such that
\overset{\circ}{\Theta}{\cap}\overset{\circ}{D}{\not=}\emptyset
and
\Theta{\cup}\overset{\circ}{D}{=}\{(x,y){\in}\Gamma:x{<}x_{d},y{<}y_{d}\}
. See Proposition
6.1
and Lemma
6.4
. An important ingredient of the proof of this step is the use of Lyapunov functions. See also Figure
10
of Section
C
of the appendix.

(2)

The functional equation (
2.30
) is established on the set
\Omega(\Theta)
.
From there, we get that, for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
, the functions
(x,y){\to}Q(x,y)h_{j}(x,y)
can be analytically continued to the set
\Omega_{d}({\Gamma})
.

(3)

Since the function
(x,y){\to}h_{j}(x,y)
is analytic in
\Omega(\Theta)
and the function
(x,y){\to}1/Q(x,y)
is analytic in
\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D})
, from these results, we will be able to deduce that the function
(x,y){\to}h_{j}(x,y)
can be continued as an analytic function to the set
\Omega_{d}({\Gamma})
.

Remark.

Clearly, for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
and
x{\neq}0
,
y{\neq}0
,

H_{j}(x,0)=xh_{1j}(x),\quad H_{j}(0,y)=yh_{2j}(y),\quad H_{j}(x,y){-}H_{j}(x,0){-}H_{j}(0,y)=xyh_{j}(x,y),

and the functional equation (
1.2
) of the introduction is equivalent to relation (
2.30
). The technical advantage of the formulation (
2.30
) is that the functions
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
and
Q
are also defined when
x{=}0
or
y{=}0
. In some cases, however, the expression (
1.2
) is more convenient.

2.4.
Singularity Analysis of
H_{j}

Since the set
\Omega_{d}({\Gamma})
can be represented as a union of the poly-discs centered at the origin of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, the above theorem proves that for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the generating function
H_{j}(x,y)
converge in
\Omega_{d}({\Gamma})
. The results of this section establish that the set
\Omega_{d}({\Gamma})
defined by (
2.23
) is the exact domain of convergence of the functions
H_{j}
,
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, i.e. the maximal domain in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
where the series (
2.19
) converge, and the dominant singularities of the functions
x{\to}H_{j}(x,0)
and
y{\to}H_{j}(0,y)
and their nature are identified.

Before stating these results, in order to have straight assertions, we have to fix a (small) technical problem related to irreducibility. It must be noted that, even under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), the killed Markov chain
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
, see Definition
1
, is not necessarily irreducible on the space
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{0\}
. One may have
g(j,k){=}0
for some
j
,
k{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{0\}
. The following lemma solves this problem.

Lemma 2.1
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), there exist
N_{0}{\geqslant}0
and a finite subset
E_{0}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}
such that for
j
,
k{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

(2.31)

\displaystyle g(j,k)=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{ if }j{\in}E_{0}\text{ and }k{\not\in}E_{0},

(2.32)

\displaystyle g(j,k)>0\quad

\displaystyle\text{ if }j{\not\in}E_{0}\text{ and }\|k\|{\geqslant}N_{0}.

Consequently, to investigate the asymptotics of
g(j,k)
as
k
goes to infinity, it is sufficient to consider a starting point
j
outside
E_{0}
. The proof of this lemma is given in Section
B
of the appendix. From now on, the integer
N_{0}{\geqslant}0
and the set
E_{0}
satisfying (
2.31
) and (
2.32
) are fixed. When the killed Markov chain
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
is irreducible on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus\{(0,0)\}}
, the set
E_{0}
is of course empty.

Proposition
2.2
and the definition of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
show that the relations,
x_{d}{\leqslant}x^{**}{\leqslant}x^{**}_{P}
and
y_{d}{\leqslant}y^{**}{\leqslant}y^{**}_{P}
hold. For
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
, we will prove that
x_{d}
, resp.
y_{d}
, is the dominant singularity of the function
x{\to}H_{j}(x,0)
, resp.
y\to H_{j}(0,y)
. We will see that the nature of the singularity
x_{d}
, resp.
y_{d}
, is determined by the cases (B0)-(B6) and also by several relations between the quantities
x_{d}
,
x^{**}
and
x^{**}_{P}
, resp.
y_{d}
,
y^{**}
and
y^{**}_{P}
.

By relation (
2.9
), the points
x^{**}
and
y^{**}
are respectively in
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, and from the definition of the functions
Y_{2}:[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]{\to}[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
and
X_{2}:[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]{\to}[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, we have

Y_{2}(x^{**})\leqslant y^{**}_{P}\quad\text{and}\quad X_{2}(y^{**})\leqslant x^{**}_{P}.

Proposition
2.2
gives that
y^{**}{<}y^{**}_{P}
if one of the cases (B0),(B1), (B5) or (B6) holds and, similarly,
x^{**}{<}x^{**}_{P}
if one of the cases (B0)-(B4) holds. This is summarized as follows.

(2.33)

x_{d}{=}\begin{cases}x^{**}{<}x^{**}_{P}&\text{ if one of the cases (B0),(B1), (B3) or (B4) holds},\\
x^{**}{\leqslant}x^{**}_{P}&\text{ with a possible equality }x^{**}{=}x^{**}_{P},\text{ if (B2) holds},\\
X_{2}(y^{**}){=}x^{**}{\leqslant}x^{**}_{P}&\phantom{withpossible}"\phantom{aaaequality}x^{**}{=}x^{**}_{P},\text{ if (B5) holds},\\
X_{2}(y^{**}){<}x^{**}{\leqslant}x^{**}_{P}&\text{ if (B6) holds},\end{cases}

and

(2.34)

y_{d}{=}\begin{cases}y^{**}<y^{**}_{P}&\text{ if one of the cases (B0),(B1), (B5) or (B6) holds},\\
y^{**}{\leqslant}y^{**}_{P}&\text{ with a possible equality }y^{**}{=}y^{**}_{P},\;\text{ if (B2) holds},\\
Y_{2}(x^{**}){=}y^{**}{\leqslant}y^{**}_{P}&\phantom{withpossible}"\phantom{aaaequality}y^{**}{=}y^{**}_{P},\;\text{ if (B3) holds},\\
Y_{2}(x^{**}){<}y^{**}{\leqslant}y^{**}_{P}&\text{ if (B4) holds}.\end{cases}

We now introduce several functions on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
which will be used to describe the dependence on the initial state
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
in the asymptotic behavior of
g(j,k)
when
k
goes to infinity. Under convenient conditions, as we will see, these functions are harmonic for the killed Markov chain at
0
.

Definition 2

(Functions for the Dependence on the Initial State)
.

For
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

(2.35)

\displaystyle\varkappa_{1}(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}L_{j}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))+\left(\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-1\right)H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x_{d})),

(2.36)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\left.\partial_{y}\left(\frac{L_{j}(x,y)+\left(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1\right)H_{j}(0,y)}{1-\phi_{1}(x,y)}\right)\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))},

(2.37)

\displaystyle\varkappa_{2}(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}L_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})+\left(\phi_{1}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})-1\right)H_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),0),

(2.38)

\displaystyle\tilde{\varkappa}_{2}(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\left.\partial_{x}\left(\frac{L_{j}(x,y)+\left(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1\right)H_{j}(x,0)}{1-\phi_{2}(x,y)}\right)\right|_{(x,y)=(X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P})},

(2.39)

\displaystyle\varkappa_{(x,y)}(j)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(x,0)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(0,x),

By Theorem
1
, and since the functions
\psi_{1}
,
\psi_{2}
,
Q
and
L_{j}
are analytic in a neighborhood of the set
\Omega(\overline{\Gamma})
, we have

–

\varkappa_{1}
is well defined on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
if
Y_{1}(x_{d}){<}x_{d}
, that is, if one of the cases (B0)-(B4) holds;

–

\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}

ifwell defined
"
on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}

if (B2),
x_{d}{=}x^{**}{=}x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})){<}1
hold;

–

\varkappa_{2}

ifwell defined
"

By symmetry (it is sufficient to to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
), the analogous results for the assertions i)-v) of Theorem
2
hold for the functions
y{\to}H_{j}(0,y)
,
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
.

The last assertion of Theorem
2
is obtained as a consequence of Theorem
1
. The proofs of the first five assertions are more demanding as it will be seen.

Remark.

In the context of the functional equation (
2.30
), a classical approach of the literature consists in finding a suitable analytic function
x{\to}Y(x)
(resp.
y{\to}X(y)
) satisfying the equation
Q(x,Y(x)){=}0
for any
x
, resp.
Q(X(y),y){=}0
for any
y
, in some domain large enough, in order to inject
y{=}Y(x)
(resp
x{=}X(y)
) in (
2.30
).

For nearest neighbor random walks, see Malyshev
[
21
]
, the equation
Q(x,y){=}0
is quadratic in
x
and
y
, its solutions have therefore an explicit form. In the general case, when the jumps of the random walk are unbounded, one clearly cannot find the functions
Y
and
X
in such a way. See Remark 2.3 of Kobayashi and Miyazawa
[
17
]
.

Probabilistic Representations of
{\mathbf{X}_{1}}
and
{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}

In our analysis, a part of the technicalities of the literature related to analytic continuation of the functions
(Y(x))
and
(X(y))
mentioned above is avoided via a probabilistic argument.

We show that the functions
Y_{1}{:}[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]{\to}[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
and
X_{1}{:}[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]{\to}[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, introduced in Proposition
2.1
, have a probabilistic representation. With this result, we are able to prove that there exists
{\varepsilon}{>}0
such that the function
X_{1}
, resp
Y_{1}
, can be analytically continued to the set

\{x{\in}\mathbb{C}{:}x^{*}_{P}{<}|x|{<}x^{**}_{P}{+}{\varepsilon},x{\not\in}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}{+}{\varepsilon}[\},\text{ resp. }\{y{\in}\mathbb{C}{:}y^{*}_{P}{<}|y|{<}y^{**}_{P}{+}{\varepsilon},\;y{\not\in}[y^{**}_{P},y^{**}_{P}{+}{\varepsilon}[\}.

From there, several important properties of the analytic continuation of
Y_{1}
and
X_{1}
are then derived. As a consequence one can inject
y{=}Y_{1}(x)
or
x{=}X_{1}(y)
in the equation (
2.30
), and then to establish Theorem
2
.

2.5.
Asymptotics of the Green function along the Axes

In this section, we investigate the asymptotics of the Green function
g(j,k)
when one of coordinate of
k{=}(k_{1},k_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
is fixed, i.e. as
k_{1}{\to}{+}\infty
with
k_{2}
fixed or
k_{2}{\to}{+}\infty
and
k_{1}
fixed. By symmetry it is enough to consider only the first convergence.

We define
\nu_{1}(0){=}1
and, for
n{\geq}1
,

(2.55)

\nu_{1}(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{(n{-}1)!}\left.\frac{\partial^{n-1}}{\partial y^{n-1}}\left(\frac{\psi_{1}(x_{d},y)}{Q(x_{d},y)}\right)\right|_{y=0},

where
Q
and
\psi_{1}
are defined by (
2.24
) and (
2.25
) respectively.
As the following theorem shows, the quantity
\nu_{1}(k_{2})
expresses the dependence on
k_{2}
in the limiting behavior of
k_{1}{\to}g(j,(k_{1},k_{2})))
when
k_{1}{\to}{+}\infty
.

In Section
8
, it is shown that
\nu_{1}
is, up to a multiplicative constant, the invariant distribution of a twisted version of a random walk on
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
obtained by removing the boundary
\{0\}{\times}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. It will show in particular that the coefficients
\nu_{1}(n)
,
n{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, are positive.

The case (B7), already analyzed, excepted, the following result gives a complete description of all possible cases for the asymptotic behavior of the Green function
g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))
as
k_{1}\to+\infty
for a fixed
k_{2}{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.

Theorem 3

(Asymptotics of Green Function with a Fixed Second Component)
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), the following assertions hold.

(1)

If either one of the cases (B0),(B1), (B3), (B4) holds or (B2) and
x_{d}{<}x^{**}_{P}
hold, then for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
k_{2}{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, as
k_{1}{\to}{+}\infty
,

(2.56)

g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ a_{1}\,\nu_{1}(k_{2})\varkappa_{1}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}-1}.

where
\varkappa_{1}(j)>0
and
a_{1}>0
are defined respectively by (
2.35
) and (
2.41
).

(2)

If (B2) and
x_{d}{=}x^{**}_{P}
hold, then for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
k_{2}{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, as
k_{1}{\to}{+}\infty
,

(2.57)

\displaystyle g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))

\displaystyle\sim a_{2}\,\nu_{1}(k_{2})\varkappa_{1}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}}\left(\sqrt{\pi k_{1}x_{d}}\right)^{-1}\quad\text{if}\quad\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d})){=}1,

(2.58)

\displaystyle g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))

\displaystyle\sim{a}_{3}\,\nu_{1}(k_{2})\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}}\left(k_{1}\sqrt{\pi k_{1}x_{d}}\right)^{-1}\quad\text{if}\quad\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d})){<}1,

where
\varkappa_{1}(j)>0
,
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)>0
,
a_{1}>0
and
{a}_{2}>0
are defined respectively by (
2.35
), (
2.36
), (
2.43
) and (
2.45
).

(3)

If (B5) holds and
x_{d}{<}x^{**}_{P}
, then for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
k_{2}{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, as
k_{1}{\to}{+}\infty
,

(2.59)

g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ a_{4}\nu_{1}(k_{2})\varkappa_{2}(j)k_{1}x_{d}^{-k_{1}-2},

where
\varkappa_{2}(j)>0
and
a_{4}>0
are defined by (
2.37
) and (
2.47
).

(4)

If (B5) holds and
x_{d}{=}x^{**}_{P}
, then for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
k_{2}{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, as
k_{1}{\to}{+}\infty
,

(2.60)

\displaystyle g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))

\displaystyle\sim\penalty\ a_{5}\,\nu_{1}(k_{2})\varkappa_{2}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}-1}\quad\text{if }\quad\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d})){=}1,

(2.61)

\displaystyle g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))

\displaystyle\sim\penalty\ {a}_{6}\,\nu_{1}(k_{2})\varkappa_{2}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}}\left(\sqrt{\pi k_{1}x_{d}}\right)^{-1}\quad\text{if }\quad\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d})){<}1,

where
\varkappa_{2}(j)>0
,
a_{5}>0
and
{a}_{6}>0
are defined respectively by (
2.37
), (
2.49
) and (
2.51
).

(5)

If (B6) holds, then for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
k_{2}{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, as
k_{1}{\to}{+}\infty
,

(2.62)

g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ a_{7}\nu_{1}(k_{2})\varkappa_{2}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}-1},

where
\varkappa_{2}(j)>0
and
a_{7}>0
are defined respectively by (
2.37
) and (
2.53
).

For
k_{2}=0
, this result is obtained as a straightforward consequence of Theorem
2
by using the Tauberian-like theorem due to Flajolet and Odlyzko
[
9
]
, see Corollary VI.1 of
[
10
]
). To get this result for
k_{2}>0
, we prove that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}\setminus E_{0}
and
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}g(j,(n,k_{2}))/g(0,(n,k_{2}))=\nu_{1}(k_{2}),\quad k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

by using a probabilistic representation of the coefficients
\nu_{1}(k_{2})
,
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. The proof of this result is given in Section
8
.

2.6.
Asymptotics of the Green function along Directions of
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}

In this section, we present the asymptotics of the Green function
g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))
as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|{\to}w
where
w
, the direction, is an element of
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}{=}\{(u_{1},u_{2}){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}:u_{1}^{2}{+}u_{2}^{2}{=}1\}
.

For each of the cases (B0)-(B6), we will introduce subsets
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
of
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
used to define the partition of the set of directions in
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
. This is achieved by the definitions
3
,
4
,
5
and
6
in Section
2.6.2
. A critical direction
w_{c}{=}(u_{c},v_{c})
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
will play a role in several cases. As it will be seen this partition will determine a structure of the asymptotics behavior of the Green function
k{\to}g(j,k)
.

2.6.1.
Diffeomorphism between
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}

The sets
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
and
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
are defined by (
2.1
) and (
2.14
).
Under Assumptions (A1), see for instance Ney and Spitzer
[
23
]
, the Laplace transform

(2.63)

(\alpha,\beta)\to\widetilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}P(e^{\alpha},e^{\beta})=\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\mu(k)e^{\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2}}.

is strictly convex, the level set
\widetilde{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\{(\alpha,\beta){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}:\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta){\leqslant}1\}

is strictly convex and compact, the gradient
\nabla_{\alpha,\beta}\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)
does not vanishes on the boundary
\partial\widetilde{D}
of
\widetilde{D}
, and the function

(2.64)

(\alpha,\beta)\to\nabla_{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)/\|\nabla_{\alpha,\beta}\widetilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)\|

determines a diffeomorphism from
\partial\widetilde{D}
to the unit circle
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}{=}\{(u_{1},u_{2}){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}:u_{1}^{2}{+}u_{2}^{2}{=}1\}
.

Since for
x{=}e^{\alpha}
and
y{=}e^{\beta}
, one has

(2.65)

\partial_{\alpha}\widetilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)=x\partial_{x}P(x,y)\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\beta}\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)=y\partial_{y}P(x,y),

for the set
D
defined by (
2.7
), it follows that the function

(2.66)

(x,y)\to w_{D}(x,y)=(u_{D}(x,y),v_{D}(x,y))\\
\penalty\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\penalty\ \frac{1}{\sqrt{x^{2}(\partial_{x}P(x,y))^{2}+y^{2}(\partial_{y}P(x,y))^{2}}}(x\partial_{x}P(x,y),\,y\partial_{y}P(x,y))

is a diffeomorphism from
\partial D
to
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
and, by the definition of
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
, from
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
to
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
.
We denote by
w{\to}(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
, resp.
w{\to}(\alpha_{D}(w),\beta_{D}(w))
, the inverse mapping of the function (
2.66
), resp. of (
2.64
).

Remark that, for
w_{1}{=}(u_{1},v_{1})
and
w_{2}{=}(u_{2},v_{2}){\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
, the inequalities
u_{1}{<}u_{2}
and
v_{1}{>}v_{2}
are equivalent and, since the set
\tilde{D}
is strictly convex,
\alpha_{D}(u_{1},v_{1}){<}\alpha_{D}(u_{2},v_{2})
, resp.
\beta_{D}(u_{1},v_{1}){<}\beta_{D}(u_{2},v_{2})
, if and only if
u_{1}{<}u_{2}
, resp.
v_{1}{>}v_{2}
. Hence, using again (
2.65
) for
x{=}e^{\alpha}
and
y{=}e^{\beta}
, one gets a similar property for the diffeomorphism
w{\to}(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
:

Lemma 2.2
.

Under Assumption (A1), for any
w_{1}{=}(u_{1},v_{1})
,
w_{2}{=}(u_{2},v_{2}){\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
, then

x_{D}(u_{1},v_{1})<x_{D}(u_{2},v_{2})\;\Leftrightarrow\;u_{1}<u_{2}\;\Leftrightarrow\;v_{1}>v_{2}\;\Leftrightarrow\;y_{D}(u_{1},v_{1})>y_{D}(u_{2},v_{2}).

This elementary property of the diffeomorphism
w{\to}(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
will be useful in the next section.

2.6.2.
Regions of directions

Definition 3
.

If (B0) holds, we introduce the vector
w_{c}{=}(u_{c},v_{c})\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
orthogonal to the vector

\left(\ln x^{**}{-}\ln X_{2}(y^{**}),\ln X_{2}(y^{**}){-}\ln y^{**}\right)

and we let

(2.67)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{w{=}(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:\,u>u_{c}\}

(2.68)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{w{=}(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:\,u<u_{c}\}

(2.69)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\emptyset.

Since in the case (B0), we have
X_{2}(y_{d}){>}x_{d}
and
Y_{2}(x_{d}){>}y_{d}
, such a vector
w_{c}{=}(u_{c},v_{c})\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
exists, is clearly unique and has positive coordinates. The sets
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
are therefore both non-empty:
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is a part of
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
included in the half-plane

\{w{=}(u,v){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}:v_{c}u{>}u_{c}v\}=\{w{=}(u,v){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}:(\ln(X_{2}(y_{d})){-}\ln(x_{d}))u>(\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d})){-}\ln(y_{d}))v\}

and contains the vector
(1,0)
, and
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
is a part of
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
included in the half-plane

\{w{=}(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:v_{c}u{<}u_{c}v\}=\{w{=}(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:(\ln(X_{2}(y_{d})){-}\ln(x_{d}))u<(\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d})){-}\ln(y_{d}))v\}

and contains the vector
(0,1)
.

Definition 4
.

If(B1) holds, we take
w_{c}{=}(u_{c},v_{c}){=}w_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
and define the regions
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
in the same way as in the case (B0).

In the case (B1), we have
x_{d}{<}x^{**}_{P}
,
y_{d}{<}y^{**}_{P}
and
(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})){=}(X_{2}(x_{d}),y_{d}){\in}{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
, hence, similarly to the case (B0), both coordinates of the vector
w_{c}
are positive and the sets of the regions

and

are non-empty:
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is a part of

.

Definition 5
.

If (B2) holds, we define

(2.70)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{w{=}(u,v){\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:u_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d}){<}u{<}u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\},

(2.71)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{w{=}(u,v){\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:u{>}u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\},

(2.72)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\{w{=}(u,v){\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:u{<}u_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})\}.

By Lemma
2.2
, for
w{=}(u,v){\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
, the inequalities

and

is equivalent to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{w_{D}(x,y):(x,y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22},\,x{<}x_{d},y{<}y_{d}\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\{w_{D}(x,y):(x,y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22},\,x{>}x_{d}\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{W}}_{0}

\displaystyle=\{w_{D}(x,y):(x,y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22},\,y{>}y_{d}\}.

In the case (B2), we have
X_{2}(y_{d}){<}x_{d}{\leqslant}x^{**}_{P}
and
Y_{2}(x_{d}){<}y_{d}{\leqslant}y^{**}_{P}
, hence the set
\{(x,y){\in}{\mathcal{S}}_{22}:x{<}x_{d},y{<}y_{d}\}
is non-empty, and consequently, the set of directions
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
is also non-empty: this a part of the set
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
included in the intersection of the two half-planes
\{w=(u,v){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}:v_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})u>u_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})v\}
and
\{w{=}(u,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:v_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))u<u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))v\}
. Similar arguments show that in this case,

–

the set of directions
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is empty if and only if
x_{d}{=}x^{**}_{P}
,

–

the set of
"
direction

{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
is
"
empty
if and only if
y_{d}{=}y^{**}_{P}
.

Definition 6
.

If either (B3) or (B4) holds we let

and we define

We will see that for any

, the asymptotic of the Green function

associated to the distribution

when
