Twisted Homogeneous Random Walks

To formulate convergence results when
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}{\to}{+}\infty
and
(k_{1},k_{2})/\|(k_{1},k_{2})\|{\to}w
, for
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
, some quantities, also used in the asymptotics of the Green function of the homogeneous random walk, are now introduced. See Ney and Spitzer
[
23
]
or Theorem 25.15 in Woess
[
25
]
.

Definition 7
.

For
w{\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
, we denote by
(S^{w}(n))
the homogeneous random walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with transition probabilities, for

m
,
k{=}(k_{1},k_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{m}(S^{w}(1){=}m{+}k)=(x_{D}(w))^{k_{1}}(y_{D}(w))^{k_{2}}\mu(k).

The vector of first moments and the matrix of second moments of
(S^{(w)}(n))
are denoted respectively by
{\mathfrak{m}}(w){=}({\mathfrak{m}}_{1}(w),{\mathfrak{m}}_{2}(w))
and
{\mathcal{Q}}(w){=}\bigl({\mathcal{Q}}_{i,j}(w)\bigr)_{i,j=1}^{2}
: for
i
,
j{\in}\{1,2\}
,

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{m}}_{i}(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}k_{i}\,(x_{D}(w))^{k_{1}}(y_{D}(w))^{k_{2}}\mu(k),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{Q}}_{i,j}(w)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}k_{i}\,k_{j}\,(x_{D}(w))^{k_{1}}(y_{D}(w))^{k_{2}}\mu(k).

The associated quadratic form at
z{\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}
is denoted by
z{\cdot}{\mathcal{Q}}(w){\cdot}z
. For
w{=}(u,v){\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
, we define
w^{\perp}{=}({-}v,u)
.

We can now state our second set of asymptotic results. The proof of this theorem is given in Section
9
. Recall that here and throughout the paper, for
k{=}(k_{1},k_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, we denote
w_{k}{=}k/\|k\|
.

Theorem 4

(Asymptotics of Green Function along Directions of
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
)
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
, the following assertions hold:

i)

If
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}{\to}{+}\infty
, then, uniformly with respect to
w_{k}
in any compact subset of
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,

(2.75)

g(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ b_{1}\varkappa_{1}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}-1}(Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2}-1},

where

(2.76)

b_{1}=(\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})){-}1)\left(\left.\partial_{y}P(x,y)\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))}\left.\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\right|_{x=x_{d}}\right)^{-1}>0;

ii)

If
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}{\to}{+}\infty
, then, uniformly with respect to
w_{k}
in any compact subset of
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
,

(2.77)

g(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ b_{2}\varkappa_{2}(j)(X_{2}(y_{d}))^{-k_{1}-1}y_{d}^{-k_{2}-1},

where

(2.78)

b_{2}=(\phi_{2}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d}){-}1)\left(\left.\partial_{x}P(x,y)\right|_{(x,y)=(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})}\left.\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\right|_{y=y_{d}}\right)^{-1}>0.

iii)

If (B0) holds then, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}{\to}{+}\infty
and
w_{k}{\to}w_{c}
,

(2.79)

g(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ C_{1}\varkappa_{1}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}-1}(Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2}-1}+C_{2}\varkappa_{2}(j)(X_{2}(y_{d}))^{-k_{1}-1}y_{d}^{-k_{2}-1},

where
b_{1}{>}0
and
b_{2}{>}0
are given respectively by (
2.76
) and (
2.78
).

iv)

If (B3) and
y_{d}{<}y^{**}_{P}
hold, then as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}{\to}{+}\infty
and
w_{k}{\to}(0,1)
,

(2.80)

g(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \varkappa_{1}(j)\left(b_{1}x_{d}^{-k_{1}-1}+b_{3}(X_{2}(y_{d}))^{-k_{1}-1}y_{d}^{-1}k_{2}\right)y_{d}^{-k_{2}-1},

where
b_{1}>0
is given by (
2.76
) and

(2.81)

b_{3}=x_{d}(\phi_{1}(x_{d},y_{d}){-}1)\left.\left(\partial_{y}P(x,y)\,\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\frac{d}{dy}X_{1}(y)\right)^{-1}\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{d},y_{d})}>0.

v)

If (B5) and
x_{d}{<}x^{**}_{P}
hold, then, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}{\to}{+}\infty
and
w_{k}{\to}(1,0)
,

(2.82)

g(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \varkappa_{2}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}-1}\left(b_{4}k_{1}x_{d}^{-1}(Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2}-2}+b_{2}y_{d}^{-k_{2}-1}\right)

where
b_{2}>0
is given by (
2.78
) and

(2.83)

b_{4}=y_{d}(\phi_{2}(x_{d},y_{d}){-}1)\left.\left(\partial_{x}P(x,y)\,\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\frac{d}{dx}Y_{1}(x)\right)^{-1}\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{d},y_{d})}>0.

vi)

if (B2) holds and
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}{\to}{+}\infty
, then, uniformly with respect to
w_{k}
in any compact subset of
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
,

(2.84)

g(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \varkappa_{(x_{D}(w_{k}),y_{D}(w_{k}))}(j)\frac{\|{\mathfrak{m}}(w_{k})\|\sqrt{w_{k}^{\perp}{\cdot}{\mathcal{Q}}(w_{k}){\cdot}w_{k}^{\perp}}}{\sqrt{2\pi\|k\|}\,(x_{D}(w_{k}))^{k_{1}}(y_{D}(w_{k}))^{k_{2}}}.

2.6.3.
Missing Asymptotics

With the definition of the regions of directions
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
, the above theorem provides the asymptotics of the Green function
g(j,k)
as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}{\to}{+}\infty
for all possible directions
w
except the following singular cases:

–

(B1) holds and
w{=}w_{c}{=}w_{D}(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))
;

–

(B2) holds and either
w{=}w_{D}(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))
or
w{=}w_{D}(X_{2}(y^{**}),y^{**})
;

–

(B3) and
y_{d}{=}y^{**}{=}y^{**}_{P}
hold and
w{=}(0,1)
;

–

(B5) and
x_{d}{=}x^{**}{=}x^{**}_{P}
hold and
w=(1,0)
.

Getting these missing asymptotics seems to require an additional significant technical effort. Note the asymptotics for these singular directions has not been derived even in the case of nearest neighbor jumps. In Kurkova and Malyshev
[
18
]
such asymptotic results are obtained but only along lines of
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
, with a rational direction in particular.

Remarks on Asymptotic Expressions.

To the best of our knowledge, the asymptotic behaviors
iv)
and
v)
have not been established in the literature, even in the case of nearest neighbor jumps. Note that the asymptotics of the cases
iii)
,
iv)
and
v)
of Theorem
4
are expressed as a sum of two terms. We now discuss the implications of these asymptotic results.

Case
iii)

The asymptotics (
2.79
) reflect in fact a competition between the geometric decay determined by the simple pole
x_{d}{=}x^{**}
of the function
x{\to}H_{j}(x,0)
and the geometric decay determined by the simple pole
y_{d}{=}y^{**}
of the function
y\to H_{j}(0,y)
. In the case when (B0) holds and if
(k_{n}){=}(k_{1,n},k_{2,n})
is a sequence of points of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
going to infinity such that
k_{n}/\|k_{n}\|{\to}w_{c}
, since
(x_{d}/X_{2}(y_{d}))^{u_{c}}{=}(y_{d}/Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{v_{c}}
and
X_{2}(y_{d}){>}x_{d}
, Theorem
4
shows in fact that for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
,

\displaystyle g(j,k_{n})

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ C_{1}\varkappa_{1}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1,n}-1}(Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2,n}-1}\quad\text{if }\lim_{n}\Bigl(k_{1,n}{-}k_{2,n}{u_{c}}/{v_{c}}\Bigr)={+}\infty

\displaystyle g(j,k_{n})

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ C_{2}\varkappa_{2}(j)(X_{2}(y_{d}))^{-k_{1,n}-1}y_{d}^{-k_{2,n}-1}\quad\text{if }\lim_{n}\Bigl(k_{2,n}{-}k_{2,n}{u_{c}}/{v_{c}}\Bigr)={-}\infty

(2.85)

\displaystyle g(j,k_{n})

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \left(C_{1}\varkappa_{1}(j){+}C_{2}\left(\frac{x_{d}}{X_{2}(y_{d})}\right)^{\sigma}\varkappa_{2}(j)\right)x_{d}^{-k_{1,n}-1}(Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2,n}-1}

\displaystyle\ \hskip 170.71652pt\text{ if }\lim_{n}\Bigl(k_{1,n}{-}k_{2,n}{u_{c}}/{v_{c}}\Bigr)=\sigma\in\mathbb{R}.

The convergence (
2.85
) exhibits an interesting phenomenon.

(1)

If
u_{c}/v_{c}{\in}\mathbb{R}{\setminus}\mathbb{Q}
, then for any
\sigma{\in}\mathbb{R}
, there is a sequence
(k_{n}){=}(k_{1,n},k_{2,n}){\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
going to infinity such that
\lim_{n}k_{n}/\|k_{n}\|{\to}w_{c}
, for which
\sigma{=}\lim_{n}(k_{1,n}{-}k_{2,n}{u_{c}}/{v_{c}})
and relation (
2.85
) holds.

(2)

The rational case, i.e; when
u_{c}/v_{c}{=}p/q{\in}\mathbb{Q}
for some
p
,
q{\in}{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
, relation (
2.85
) holds if and only if for
n
large enough,
qk_{1,n}{-}k_{2,n}p
is constant and, therefore,
\sigma{\in}\mathbb{Q}
is of the form
\sigma{=}\tilde{p}/q
for some
\tilde{p}{\in}\mathbb{Z}
.
An analogous result has been established in Ignatiouk et al.
[
15
]
.

Case
iv)

The asymptotics (
2.80
) reflect a competition between the geometric decay determined by the simple pole
x_{d}{=}x^{**}
of the function
x{\to}H_{j}(x,0)
and the geometric decay multiplied by a factor
k_{2}
determined by the pole
y_{d}{=}Y_{2}(x_{d}){=}y^{**}
of the second order of the function
y{\to}H_{j}(0,y)
. If (B3) and
y_{d}{<}y^{**}_{P}
hold and
(k_{n}){=}(k_{1,n},k_{2,n})
is a sequence of points of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
going to infinity such that
\|k_{n}\|{\to}{+}\infty
and
k_{n}/\|k_{n}\|{\to}(0,1)
, Theorem
3
shows that for
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
,

\displaystyle g(j,k)

\displaystyle\sim\varkappa_{1}(j)C_{1}x_{d}^{-k_{1,n}-1}y_{d}^{-k_{2,n}-1}

\displaystyle\text{ if }\lim_{n}k_{2,n}x_{d}^{k_{1,n}}/(X_{2}(y_{d})))^{k_{1,n}}{=}0;

\displaystyle g(j,k)

\displaystyle\sim\varkappa_{1}(j)C_{2}(X_{2}(y_{d}))^{-k_{1,n}-1}k_{2,n}y_{d}^{-k_{2,n}-2}

\displaystyle\text{ if }\lim_{n}k_{2,n}x_{d}^{k_{1,n}}/(X_{2}(y_{d})))^{k_{1,n}}{=}{+}\infty;

\displaystyle g(j,k)

\displaystyle\sim\varkappa_{1}(j)\left(C_{1}+\sigma C_{2}y_{d}^{-1}\right)x_{d}^{-k_{1,n}-1}y_{d}^{-k_{2,n}-1}

\displaystyle\text{ if }\lim_{n}k_{2,n}x_{d}^{k_{1,n}}/(X_{2}(y_{d})))^{k_{1,n}}{=}\sigma{>}0.

Case
v)

The asymptotic (
2.82
) reflects a competition between the geometric decay multiplied by a factor
k_{1}
determined by the pole
x_{d}{=}X_{2}(y_{d}){=}x^{**}
of the second order of the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
and the geometric decay determined by the simple pole
y_{d}{=}y^{**}
of the function
y{\to}H_{j}(0,y)
.

Similar asymptotics hold, by exchanging the roles of
x
and
y
in this case when
x_{d}{<}x^{**}_{P}
,
\|k_{n}\|{\to}{+}\infty
and
k_{n}/\|k_{n}\|{\to}(1,0)
.

Relations with Homogeneous Random Walks.

The asymptotic relation (
2.84
) is similar to the exact asymptotics of the Green function of the homogeneous random walk
(S(n))
in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, see Ney and Spitzer
[
23
]
and Theorem 25.15 of Woess
[
25
]
. The only difference in fact is that in the asymptotic obtained by Ney and Spitzer
[
23
]
, the function
j{=}(j_{1},j_{1}){\to}(x_{D}(w_{k}))^{j_{1}}(y_{D}(w_{k}))^{j_{1}}{=}\exp(\alpha_{D}(w_{k})j_{1}+\beta_{D}(w_{k})j_{2})
, which is harmonic for the homogeneous random walk, is replaced in our case by the function
j\to\varkappa_{(x_{D}(w_{k}),y_{D}(w_{k}))}(j)
, which is harmonic for the killed random walk
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
. Analogous results have been obtained in Ignatiouk
[
11
]
for the Green functions of a homogeneous random walk in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
killed outside of an open cone and for the asymptotics along the interior directions of the cone.

Outline of the paper

Our paper is organized as follows :

–

In Section
3
, our results are used to investigate the Martin compactification of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
of the killed Markov chain
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
and also of the original random walk
(Z(n))
when it is transient.

–

In Section
4
, we show that the points
x^{*}_{P}
,
x^{*}
,
x^{**}
,
x^{**}_{P}
,
y^{*}_{P}
,
y^{*}
,
y^{**}
,
y^{**}_{P}
and the functions
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}:[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]{\to}[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
and
X_{1}
,
X_{2}:[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]{\to}[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
are well defined, and we obtain their first properties;

–

Proposition
2.2
is proved in Section
5
;

–

Sections
6
and
9
are respectively devoted to the proofs of Theorems
1
and
4
.

–

In Section
A
, the conditions (B0)-(B7) are compared with the conditions of positive recurrence and transience for the random walk
(Z(n))
;

3.
Application to the Martin Boundary

When the Markov chain
(Z(n))
is transient, its Green function, for
j
,
k{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
,

G(j,k)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=k),

is related to the Green function
g(j,k)
of the killed Markov chain at
0
in the following way,

G(j,k)=g(j,k)+G(j,0)g(0,k),\quad\text{for $j{\neq}0$},

and

G(0,k)=G(0,0)g(0,k).

The Martin kernels have therefore a simple relation

(3.1)

\frac{G(j,k)}{G(0,k)}=\frac{1}{G(0,0)}\frac{g(j,k)}{g(0,k)}+\frac{G(j,0)}{G(0,0)}={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\tau_{0}=+\infty)\frac{g(j,k)}{g(0,k)}+P_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty),

and the Martin compactification of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
for the killed Markov chain
(Z(n))
at
0
is homeomorphic to the Martin compactification of the original random walk
(Z(n))
.

In this section, our asymptotic results, Theorems
3
and
4
, are used to obtain the asymptotics of the Martin kernel of the killed Markov chain
(Z(n))
at
0
. We do not assume that the original random walk
(Z(n))
is positive recurrent or transient. The Martin boundary of
(g(j,k))
is in fact almost completely described, the four asymptotic cases mentioned in Section
2.6.3
excepted. Throughout this section, we will assume that the conditions (A1)-(A3) are satisfied.

Since by Lemma
2.1
,
g(j,k)=0
, for any
j\in E_{0}
, and
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
, to investigate the Martin boundary of the killed random walk, it is sufficient to consider
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Suppose that either (B0) or (B1) holds, and let
the vector
w_{c}{=}(u_{c},v_{c}){\in}{\mathbb{S}}_{+}^{1}
be the critical direction defined in Section
2.6.2
and
(k_{n}){=}(k_{1,n},k_{2,n})
be a sequence of
Z_{+}^{2}
whose norm goes to infinity. Then for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
, the following assertions hold.

(1)

If
\liminf_{n}{k_{1,n}}/{\|k_{n}\|}{>}u_{c}
then

(3.2)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,k_{n})}{g(0,k_{n})}=\frac{\varkappa_{1}(j)}{\varkappa_{1}(0)},

where
\varkappa_{1}
is the function defined by relation (
2.35
).

(2)

If
\limsup_{n}k_{1,n}/\|k_{n}\|{<}u_{c}
then

(3.3)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,k_{n})}{g(0,k_{n})}=\frac{\varkappa_{2}(j)}{\varkappa_{2}(0)},

where
\varkappa_{2}
is the function defined by relation (
2.37
).

(3)

If (B0) holds and
\lim_{n}k_{n}/\|k_{n}\|{=}w_{c}
, then

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,k_{n})}{g(0,k_{n})}\\
=\frac{b_{1}\varkappa_{1}(j)\lambda_{1}^{-k_{1}-1}{+}b_{2}\varkappa_{2}(j)\lambda_{2}^{-k_{2}-1}}{b_{1}\varkappa_{1}(0)\lambda_{1}^{-k_{1}-1}{+}b_{2}\varkappa_{2}(0)\lambda_{2}^{-k_{2}-1}},

with
\lambda_{1}{=}x_{d}/X_{2}(y_{d})
and
\lambda_{2}{=}y_{d}/Y_{2}(x_{d})
and
b_{1}{>}0
, resp.
b_{2}{>}0
, is given by relation (
2.76
), resp. relation (
2.78
).

When either (B0) or (B1) holds, Proposition
3.1
implies that the minimal Martin boundary
\partial_{m}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, see Proposition 24.4 of
[
25
]
, contains two points
\xi_{1}
and
\xi_{2}
, and any sequence of points
(k_{n})
converging to infinity and such that
\liminf_{n}k_{1}^{(n)}/\|k^{(n)}\|{>}u_{c}
(resp.
\limsup_{n}k_{1}^{(n)}/\|k^{(n)}\|{<}u_{c}
) converges in the Martin compactification of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
to
\xi_{1}
(resp. to
\xi_{2}
).

When (B0) holds, with this result one gets that the minimal Martin boundary of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
relative for the killed Markov chain at
0
contains exactly two points, and with the same arguments as in Theorem 3 of
[
15
]
, we obtain that the full Martin boundary is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
, resp.
\mathbb{R}\cup\{\pm\infty\}
, if
u_{c}/v_{c}{\in}{\mathbb{Q}}
, resp.
u_{c}/v_{c}{\not\in}{\mathbb{Q}}
. By the Poisson-Martin representation theorem, in this case, any non-negative harmonic function for the killed Markov chain is therefore of the form
\theta_{1}\varkappa_{1}{+}\theta_{2}\varkappa_{2}
with for some
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}{\in}[0,{+}\infty[
.

Note that the full Martin boundary is not obtained in the case (B1) since the asymptotics of the Green function
g(j,k)
along the direction
w_{c}
are missing. See Section
2.6.3
.

For the region (B2) we have the following proposition.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that (B2) holds, and let
(k_{n}){=}(k_{1,n},k_{2,n})
be a sequence of
Z_{+}^{2}
whose norm goes to infinity. Then for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
, the following assertions hold.

(1)

If
\lim_{n}k_{n}/\|k_{n}\|=w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
, then

(3.4)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,k_{n})}{g(0,k_{n})}=\frac{\varkappa_{(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))}(j)}{\varkappa_{(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))}(0)}

where
\varkappa_{({\cdot},{\cdot})}
is the function defined by (
2.39
).

(2)

If
x_{d}{=}x^{**}_{P}
and the sequence
(k_{2,n})
is bounded, then

(3.5)

\lim_{n\to{+}\infty}\frac{g(j,k_{n})}{g(0,k_{n})}=\begin{cases}\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)/\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(0)&\text{if $\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1$},\\
\varkappa_{1}(j)/\varkappa_{1}(0)&\text{if $\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1$.}\end{cases}

where
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}
is the function defined by (
2.36
).

(3)

If
x^{**}{<}x^{**}_{P}
and
\liminf_{n}k_{1,n}/\|k_{n}\|{>}u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
, then

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,k_{n})}{g(0,k_{n})}=\frac{\varkappa_{1}(j)}{\varkappa_{1}(0)}.

(4)

if
y^{**}{=}y^{**}_{P}
and the sequence
(k_{1,n})
is bounded, then

(3.6)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,k_{n})}{g(0,k_{n})}=\begin{cases}\tilde{\varkappa}_{2}(j)/\tilde{\varkappa}_{2}(0)&\text{if $\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P})<1$},\\
\varkappa_{2}(j)/\varkappa_{2}(0)&\text{if $\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P})=1$}\end{cases}

where
\tilde{\varkappa}_{2}
is the function defined by (
2.38
).

(5)

If
y^{**}{<}y^{**}_{P}
and
\liminf_{n}k_{2,n}/\|k_{n}\|{>}v_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})
then,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,k_{n})}{g(0,k_{n})}=\frac{\varkappa_{2}(j)}{\varkappa_{2}(0)}.

When (B2) holds, this result proves that for any direction
w{\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
in the closure
\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
of
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
there is a point
\xi(w)
in the Martin boundary of the killed Markov chain, and that if
(k_{n})
is a sequence of points of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
whose norm converges to infinity then, for the convergence in the Martin compactification,

\lim_{n\to+\infty}k_{n}=\begin{cases}\xi(w)&\text{ for }w{\in}{\mathcal{W}}_{0}\text{ if }k_{n}/\|k_{n}\|{\to}w,\\
\xi(w_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})))&\text{ if }\liminf_{n}k_{1,n}/\|k_{n}\|{>}u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})),\\
\xi(w_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d}))&\text{ if }\limsup_{n}k_{1,n}/\|k_{n}\|{<}u_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d}).\end{cases}

As explained before, in the case (B2), also due to the missing asymptotics for the singular directions
w_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
and
w_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})
, the full Martin boundary is not completely determined. We conjecture that in this case it is homeomorphic to
\overline{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose that either (B3) and
y_{d}{<}y^{**}_{P}
hold or (B4) holds. Then for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
,

\lim_{\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,k)}{g(0,k)}=\frac{\varkappa_{1}(j)}{\varkappa_{1}(0)},

If either (B3) and
y_{d}{<}y^{**}_{P}
hold or (B4) holds, the full Martin boundary of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
of the killed Markov chain is therefore a single point, and, up to a multiplicative constant, the function
\varkappa_{1}
is the unique non-negative harmonic function.

When the original random walk
(Z(n))
is transient and the measures
\mu_{0}
,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are stochastic, since the Martin compactification of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
of the Markov chain
(Z(n))
is homeomorphic to the Martin compactification of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
of the killed Markov chain, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 3.4
.

If the measures
\mu_{0}
,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are stochastic and the Markov chain
(Z(n))
is transient, then under the hypotheses of Proposition
3.3
, for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

(3.7)

\lim_{\|k\|\to+\infty}\frac{G(j,k)}{G(0,k)}=1.

Proof.

Indeed, in this case, using (
3.1
), Proposition
3.3
and Lemma
2.1
, since the set
E_{0}
does not contain the origin, one gets

\lim_{\|k\|\to+\infty}\frac{G(j,k)}{G(0,k)}={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\tau_{0}=+\infty)\varkappa_{1}(j)+P_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+},

and consequently, by the Poisson-Martin representation theorem, up to a multiplicative constant, for any harmonic function
\varkappa
for the Markov chain
(Z(n))
, one has

\varkappa(j)/\varkappa(0)={\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\tau_{0}=+\infty)\varkappa_{1}(j)+P_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}.

Since in the case when the measures
\mu_{0}
,
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
are stochastic, the constants functions are harmonic for
(Z(n))
, from this it follows that

(3.8)

{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\tau_{0}=+\infty)\varkappa_{1}(j)+P_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty)=1,\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+},

and (
3.7
) holds.

Remark that the identity (
3.8
) can also be obtained in a straightforward way by using Proposition
A.3
of Section
A
.
∎

By symmetry, similar result can be obtained with
\varkappa_{2}
instead of
\varkappa_{1}
if either (B5) and
x_{d}=x^{**}_{P}
hold or (B6) holds.

We conclude with the case when either (B3) holds with
y_{d}{=}y^{**}_{P}
or (B5) holds with
x_{d}{=}x^{**}_{P}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Suppose that (B3) holds with
y_{d}{=}y^{**}_{P}
and let
(k_{n})
be a sequence of points of
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
whose norm converges to infinity. Then for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
, the convergence

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,k_{n})}{g(0,k_{n})}=\frac{\varkappa_{1}(j)}{\varkappa_{1}(0)}

holds in any of the two following cases:

–

when
\liminf_{n}k_{1,n}/\|k_{n}\|{>}0
;

–

when the sequence
(k_{1,n})
is bounded and
\lim_{n}k_{2,n}=+\infty
.

When (B3) holds with
y_{d}{=}y^{**}_{P}
there is therefore a point
\xi_{0}
in the Martin boundary of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
of the killed Markov chain, such that any sequence of points of
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
satisfying the conditions of Proposition
3.5
converges in the Martin compactification to
\xi_{0}
.

Similar result with the function
\varkappa_{2}
instead of
\varkappa_{1}
can be obtained when (B5) holds with
x_{d}{=}x^{**}_{P}
.

When (B3) holds with
y_{d}{=}y^{**}_{P}
, resp. (B5) holds with
x_{d}{=}x^{**}_{P}
, the asymptotics of the Green function
g(j,k)
when
\|k\|{\to}{+}\infty
and
k_{1}/\|k\|{\to}0
, resp. when
\|k\|{\to}{+}\infty
and
k_{2}/\|k\|{\to}0
are not known. See Section
2.6.3
. For this reason, the full Martin boundary is not determined in this case. We conjecture it is a single point.

4.
Preliminary results

In the following statement, we investigate the set
D
defined by (
2.7
): it is proved that the line segments
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
,
[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
and the functions
Y_{1},Y_{2}:[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]\to[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
and
X_{1},X_{2}:[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]\to[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
are well defined, and the first useful for our purpose properties of these functions are obtained.

Lemma 4.1
.

Under the hypotheses (A1), the line segments
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
and the functions
Y_{1},Y_{2}:[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]\to[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
and
X_{1},X_{2}:[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]\to[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
are well defined and the following assertions hold:

1)

For any
\hat{x}\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
,
Y_{1}(\hat{x})
and
Y_{2}(\hat{x})
are the only real positive solutions of the equation
P(\hat{x},y)=1
,

Y_{1}(\hat{x})<Y_{2}(\hat{x}),\quad\partial_{y}P(\hat{x},Y_{1}(\hat{x}))<0\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{y}P(\hat{x},Y_{2}(\hat{x}))<0\quad\text{if}\quad x^{*}_{P}<\hat{x}<x^{**}_{P},

and

Y_{1}(\hat{x})=Y_{2}(\hat{x})\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{y}P(\hat{x},Y_{1}(\hat{x}))=0\quad\text{if}\quad\hat{x}\in\{x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}\}.

2)

For any
\hat{y}\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
,
X_{1}(\hat{y})
and
X_{2}(\hat{y})
are the only real positive solutions of the equation
P(x,\hat{y})=1
,

X_{1}(\hat{y})<X_{2}(\hat{y}),\quad\partial_{x}P(X_{1}(\hat{y}),\hat{y})<0\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{x}P(X_{2}(\hat{y}),\hat{y})\quad\text{if}\quad y^{*}_{P}<\hat{y}<y^{**}_{P},

and

X_{1}(\hat{y})=X_{2}(\hat{y})\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{x}P(X_{1}(\hat{y}),\hat{y})=0\quad\text{if}\quad\hat{y}\in\{y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}\}.

3)

The four points
(x^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}))
,
(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))
,
(X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),y^{*}_{P})
and
(X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P})
are two by two distinct and moreover
Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}),Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})\in]y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}[
and
X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})\in]x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}[
.

4)

The function
X_{1}:[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P})]{\to}[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{*}_{P})]
is strictly decreasing and its inverse is

Y_{1}:[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{*}_{P})]{\to}[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P})]
.

5)

The function
X_{1}:[Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]{\to}[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})]
is strictly increasing and its inverse is

Y_{2}:[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})]{\to}[Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]
.

6)

The function
X_{2}:[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}]{\to}[X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]
is strictly increasing and its inverse is

Y_{1}:\penalty\ [X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]{\to}[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}]
.

7)

The function
X_{2}:[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]{\to}[X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]
strictly decreasing and its inverse is

Y_{2}:[X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]{\to}[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]
.

Proof.

To prove this lemma we notice that under the hypotheses (A1) the jump generating function
\tilde{P}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by (
2.63
)
is strictly convex and finite in a neighborhood of the set
\tilde{D}=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)\leqslant 1\}
. The set
\tilde{D}
is therefore strictly convex and compact (see for instance, Spitzer
[
24
]
) and because of the assumption (A1) (iii), it has a non-empty interior. This implies that

– each of the sets
\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}:\penalty\ \inf_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}}\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)\leqslant 1\}
and

\{\beta\in\mathbb{R}:\penalty\ \inf_{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}}\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)\leqslant 1\}

is a line segment with a non-zero length, we will denote them respectively by
[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]
and
[\beta^{*}_{P},\beta^{**}_{P}]
;

– for any
\hat{\alpha}\in[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]
, the set
[\beta_{1}(\hat{\alpha}),\beta_{2}(\hat{\alpha})]=\{\beta\in\mathbb{R}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \tilde{P}(\hat{\alpha},\beta)\leqslant 1\}
is a non-empty line segment ,
the points
\beta_{1}(\hat{\alpha})
and
\beta_{2}(\hat{\alpha})
are the only real solutions of the equation
\tilde{P}(\hat{\alpha},\beta)=1
,

\beta_{1}(\hat{\alpha})<\beta_{2}(\hat{\alpha}),\quad\partial_{\beta}\tilde{P}(\hat{\alpha},\beta_{1}(\hat{\alpha}))\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\beta}\tilde{P}(\hat{\alpha},\beta_{2}(\hat{\alpha}))>0\quad\text{if $\alpha^{*}_{P}<\hat{\alpha}<\alpha^{**}_{P}$},

and

\beta_{1}(\hat{\alpha})=\beta_{2}(\hat{\alpha})\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\beta}\tilde{P}(\hat{\alpha},\beta_{1}(\hat{\alpha}))=0\quad\text{if $\hat{\alpha}\in\{\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}\}$}:

– for any
\hat{\beta}\in[\beta^{*}_{P},\beta^{**}_{P}]
, the set
[\alpha_{1}(\hat{\beta}),\alpha_{2}(\hat{\beta})]=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\penalty\ :\penalty\ \tilde{P}(\alpha,\hat{\beta})\leqslant 1\}

is a non-empty line segment, the points
\alpha_{1}(\hat{\beta})
and
\alpha_{2}(\hat{\beta})
are the only real solutions of the equation
\tilde{P}(\alpha,\hat{\beta})=1
,

\alpha_{1}(\hat{\beta})<\alpha_{2}(\hat{\beta}),\quad\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{P}(\alpha_{1}(\hat{\beta}),\beta)<0\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{P}(\alpha_{2}(\hat{\beta}),\hat{\beta})>0\quad\text{if $\beta^{*}_{P}<\hat{\beta}<\beta^{**}_{P}$},

and

\alpha_{1}(\hat{\beta})=\alpha_{2}(\hat{\beta})\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{P}(\alpha_{1}(\hat{\beta}),\hat{\beta})=0\quad\text{if $\hat{\beta}\in\{\beta^{*}_{P},\beta^{**}_{P}\}$},

the four points
(\alpha^{*}_{P},\beta_{1}(\alpha^{*}_{P}))
,
(\alpha^{**}_{P},\beta_{1}(\alpha^{**}_{P}))
,
(\alpha_{1}(\beta^{*}_{P}),\beta^{*}_{P})
and
(\alpha_{1}(\beta^{**}_{P}),\beta^{**}_{P})
are two by two distinct and
\alpha_{1}(\beta^{*}_{P}),\alpha_{1}(\beta^{**}_{P})\in]\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}[
and
\beta_{1}(\alpha^{*}_{P}),\beta_{1}(\alpha^{**}_{P})\in]\beta^{*}_{P},\beta^{**}_{P}[
.

–

\beta_{1}{:}\penalty\ [\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]{\to}[\beta^{*}_{P},\beta^{**}_{P}]
is strictly convex with a minimum
\beta^{*}_{P}
at
\alpha_{1}(\beta^{*}_{P})=\alpha_{2}(\beta^{*}_{P})
;

–

\beta_{2}{:}\penalty\ [\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]{\to}[\beta^{*}_{P},\beta^{**}_{P}]
is strictly concave with a maximum
\beta^{**}_{P}
at
\alpha_{1}(\beta^{**}_{P}){=}\alpha_{2}(\beta^{**}_{P})
;

–

\alpha_{1}{:}\penalty\ [\beta^{*}_{P},\beta^{**}_{P}]{\to}[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]
is strictly convex with a minimum
\alpha^{*}_{P}
at
\beta_{1}(\alpha^{*}_{P}){=}\beta_{2}(\alpha^{*}_{P})
;

–

\alpha_{2}{:}\penalty\ [\beta^{*}_{P},\beta^{**}_{P}]{\to}[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]
is strictly concave with a maximum
\alpha^{**}_{P}
at
\beta_{1}(\alpha^{**}_{P}){=}\beta_{2}(\alpha^{**}_{P})
.

As a consequence of these properties, one gets that

–

\alpha_{1}{:}\penalty\ [\beta^{*}_{P},\beta_{1}(\alpha^{*}_{P})]{\to}[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha_{1}(\beta^{*}_{P})]
is strictly decreasing and its inverse function is
\beta_{1}
;

–

\alpha_{1}{:}\penalty\ [\beta_{1}(\alpha^{*}_{P}),\beta^{**}_{P}]{\to}[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha_{1}(\beta^{**}_{P})]
is strictly increasing
andts inverse
"
function is

\beta_{2}
;

–

\beta_{1}{:}\penalty\ [\alpha_{1}(\beta^{*}_{P}),\alpha^{**}_{P}]{\to}[\beta^{*}_{P},\beta_{1}(\alpha^{**}_{P}]
is strictly increasing
and its inverse
"
function is

\alpha_{2}
;

–

\alpha_{2}{:}[\beta_{1}(\alpha^{**}_{P}),\beta^{**}_{P}]{\to}[\alpha_{1}(\beta^{**}_{P}),\alpha^{**}_{P}]
is strictly decreasing
andts inverse
"
function is

\beta_{2}
.

Since the mapping
(\alpha,\beta){\to}(x,y){=}(e^{\alpha},e^{\beta})
determines a homeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
]0,+\infty[^{2}
and maps the set
\tilde{D}
to the set
D
, these assertions prove our lemma with
x^{*}_{P}{=}e^{\alpha^{*}_{P}}
,
x^{**}_{P}{=}e^{\alpha^{**}_{P}}
,
y^{*}_{P}{=}e^{\beta^{*}_{P}}
,
y^{**}_{P}{=}e^{\beta^{**}_{P}}
,

X_{i}(y)=e^{\alpha_{i}(\ln(y))},\quad\forall y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}],\;i\in\{1,2\},

and

Y_{i}(x)=e^{\beta_{i}(\ln(x))},\quad\forall x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}],\;i\in\{1,2\}.

∎

In the following lemma, we investigate the sets
D\cap D_{1}
and
D\cap D_{2}
. It is proved that the line segments
[x^{*},x^{**}]
and
[y^{*},y^{**}]
are well defined. With this result, we will be able to get the first useful for our purpose properties of the functions
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
and
y\to\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Under the hypotheses (A1) and (A3), there exist
x^{*},x^{**}\in]0,+\infty[
such that
x^{*}<x^{**}
and

[x^{*},x^{**}]=\{x\in]0,+\infty[:\penalty\ (x,y)\in D_{1}\cap D\;\text{for some}\;y>0\}.

Moreover, for any
x\in[x^{*},x^{**}]
, there exists
\tilde{Y}_{2}(x)\geqslant Y_{1}(x)
such that

\{y>0\penalty\ :\penalty\ (x,y)\in D_{1}\cap D\}=[{Y}_{1}(x),\tilde{Y}_{2}(x)],

the points
Y_{1}(x)
and
\tilde{Y}_{2}(x)
are the only real positive solutions of the equation

(4.1)

\max\{P(x,y),\phi_{1}(x,y)\}=1,

and
{Y}_{1}(x)<\tilde{Y}_{2}(x)
if and only if
x^{*}<x<x^{**}
.

Proof.

To prove this lemma, we consider the jump generating function
\tilde{P}
defined by (
2.63
), the jump generating function
\tilde{\phi}_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, defined for
(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, by

\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)=\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}\exp(\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2})\mu_{1}(k)

and the sets
\tilde{D}=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)\leqslant 1\}
and
\tilde{D}_{1}=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\penalty\ :\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)\leqslant 1\}
. Recall that for
x=e^{\alpha}
and
y=e^{\beta}
,

\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)=P(x,y),\quad\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)=\phi_{1}(x,y),

and that the mapping
(\alpha,\beta)\to(x,y)=(e^{\alpha},e^{\beta})
determines a homeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
]0,+\infty[^{2}
and maps the set
\tilde{D}\cap\tilde{D}_{1}
to the set
D\cap D_{1}
. Hence, to prove our lemma, it is sufficient to show that under our assumptions, the following assertions hold

–

there exist
\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\alpha^{*}<\alpha^{**}
and

[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}:(\alpha,\beta)\in\tilde{D}_{1}\cap\tilde{D}\;\text{for some}\;\beta\in\mathbb{R}\}.

–

for any
\alpha\in[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]
, there exists
\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)\geqslant\ln Y_{1}(e^{\alpha})
such that

\{\beta\in\mathbb{R}\penalty\ :\penalty\ (\alpha,\beta)\in\tilde{D}_{1}\cap\tilde{D}\}=[\ln{Y}_{1}(e^{\alpha}),\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)],

the points
\beta_{1}(\alpha)=\ln Y_{1}(e^{\alpha})
and
\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)
are the only real positive solutions of the equation

(4.2)

\max\{\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta),\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)\}=1,

and
\ln Y_{1}(e^{\alpha})<\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)
if and only if
\alpha^{*}<\alpha<\alpha^{**}
.

For this we remark that the set
\tilde{D}\cap\tilde{D}_{1}
is compact convex and has a non empty interior because

–

the function
\tilde{\phi}_{1}
is convex and consequently also the set
\tilde{D}_{1}
is also convex;

–

the set
\tilde{D}
is convex and compact (see the proof of Lemma
4.1
);

–

by Assumption (A3)(iii), the set
D\cap D_{1}
has a non-empty interior, and consequently, since the mapping
(\alpha,\beta)\to(x,y)=(e^{\alpha},e^{\beta})
determines a homeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
]0,+\infty[^{2}
and maps the set
\tilde{D}_{1}\cap\tilde{D}
to the set
D_{1}\cap D
, the set
\tilde{D}_{1}\cap\tilde{D}
has also a non-empty interior.

From this, it follows that the following assertions hold:

–

there exist
\alpha^{*}>0
and
\alpha^{**}>\alpha^{*}
such that

[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\penalty\ :(\alpha,\beta)\in\tilde{D}_{1}\cap\tilde{D}\;\text{for some}\;\beta\in\mathbb{R}\},

–

for any
\alpha\in[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]
, there exists
\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha),\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)\in]0,+\infty[
such that
\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha)\leqslant\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)
,

[\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha),\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)]=\{\beta\in\mathbb{R}:(\alpha,\beta)\in\tilde{D}\cap\tilde{D}_{1}\},

and

\max\{\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha)),\tilde{P}(\alpha,\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha))\}=\max\{\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)),\tilde{P}(\alpha,\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha))\}=1,

–

for any
\alpha\in]\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}[

\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha)<\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha).

Hence to complete our proof, it is sufficient to show that for any
\alpha\in[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]
, the following relations hold:

(4.3)

\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha)=\ln Y_{1}(e^{\alpha}),

(4.4)

\max\{\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta),\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)\}<1\quad\quad\text{if}\quad\quad\beta_{1}(\alpha)<\beta<\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha),

and

(4.5)

\alpha^{*}<\alpha<\alpha^{**}\quad\text{if}\quad\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha)<\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha).

Remark moreover that if (
4.4
) holds, then any
\beta
such that
\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha)<\beta<\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)
, the point
(\alpha,\beta)
belongs to the interior of the set
\tilde{D}\cap\tilde{D}_{1}
and consequently, (
4.5
) also holds. Hence, to complete our proof, it is sufficient to prove that for any
\alpha\in[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]
, (
4.3
) and (
4.4
) hold.
To get these relations, we remark that by Lemma
4.1
and since
\tilde{D}\cap\tilde{D}_{1}\subset\tilde{D}
, one has

[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]\subset\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}:(\alpha,\beta)\in\tilde{D}\quad\text{for some}\quad\beta\in\mathbb{R}\}=[\ln x^{*}_{P},\ln x^{**}_{P}]

and that for any
\alpha\in[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]
,

[\tilde{\beta}_{1}(\alpha),\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)]\subset\{\beta\in\mathbb{R}:(\alpha,\beta)\in\tilde{D}\}=[\ln Y_{1}(e^{\alpha}),\ln Y_{2}(e^{\alpha})].

Since because of Assumption (A3), the function
\beta\to\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)
is finite and strictly increasing in a neighborhood of the line segment
[0,\ln Y_{2}(e^{\alpha})]
, one gets therefore that for any
\alpha\in[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]
and
\beta<\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)
,

\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\beta)<\tilde{\phi}_{1}(\alpha,\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha))\leqslant 1.

This implies that for any
\alpha\in[\alpha^{*},\alpha^{**}]
, (
4.3
) holds. Moreover, since by Lemma
4.1
,
\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)<1
for any
\beta\in]\ln Y_{1}(e^{\alpha}),\ln Y_{2}(e^{\alpha})[
, from the last relation it follows that for any
\beta\in]\ln Y_{1}(e^{\alpha}),\tilde{\beta}_{2}(\alpha)[
, (
4.4
) also holds.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
4.2
we obtain

Corollary 4.1
.

Under the assumptions (A1) and (A3), for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
,

(i)

x\in[x^{*},x^{**}]
if and only if
\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\leqslant 1
;

(ii)

\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))<1
if
x^{*}<x<x^{**}
;

(iii)

\phi_{1}(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**}))=1
if
x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
;

(iv)

\phi_{1}(x^{*},Y_{1}(x^{*}))=1
if
x^{*}>x^{*}_{P}
.

Proof.

Indeed, since
P(x,Y_{1}(x))=1
for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, the first assertion of this corollary follows from Lemma
4.2
.

To get (ii), we recall that by Lemma
4.2
, for any
x^{*}<x<x^{**}
one has
Y_{1}(x)<\tilde{Y}_{2}(x)
and
\max\{\phi_{1}(x,\tilde{Y}_{2}(x)),P(x,\tilde{Y}_{2}(x))\}=1
. Since under the hypotheses (A3), for any
x\in[x^{*},P,x^{**}_{P}]
, the function
y\to\phi_{1}(x,y)
is finite and strictly increasing n a neighborhood of the line segment
[0,\ln Y_{2}(e^{\alpha})]
, from this it follows that
\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))<1
whenever
x^{*}<x<x^{**}
. The assertion (ii) is therefore also proved.

Suppose now that
x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
. Then by the first assertion of our corollary,

(4.6)

\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))>1\quad\text{if}\quad x^{**}<x\leqslant x^{**}_{P},

and by the second assertion,

(4.7)

\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))<1\quad\text{if}\quad x^{*}<x<x^{**}.

Since the function
\phi_{1}
is continuous in a neighborhood of the set
D
, the function
Y_{1}
is continuous on
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
(x,Y_{1}(x))\in D
for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is therefore continuous on
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, and consequently, relations (
4.6
) and (
4.7
) prove that
\phi_{1}(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**}))=1
. The assertion (iii) is therefore also proved. The proof of the assertion (iv) is quite similar.
∎

5.
The Classification in Eight Regions

This section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
2.2
establishing our classification into eight regions in the set of parameters of the random walk
(Z(n))
.

5.1.
The main idea of the proof

Remark first of all that the cases (B0)-(B7) have a simple geometrical interpretation : if we denote by
[(x,y),(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})]
the line segment in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the end-points at
(x,y)
and
(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
, then by Lemma
5.1
below, for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, one and only one of the following assertions holds:

–

the line segments
[(x,Y_{1}(x)),\,(x,Y_{2}(x))]
and
[(X_{1}(y),y),\,(X_{2}(y),y)]
have the common point
(x,y)\in D
,

–

these line segments are disjoint and
(x,y)\not\in D
.

The case (B0) occurs when the line segments

(5.1)

[(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**})),\,(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))]\quad\text{ and }\quad[(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**}),\,(X_{2}(y^{**}),y^{**})]

have the common point
(x^{**},y^{**})
in the interior of the set
D
. The cases (B1), (B3), (B5) and (B7) occur when these line segments have the common point
(x^{**},y^{**})
on the boundary of the set
D
:

(x^{**},y^{**})\in\begin{cases}{\mathcal{S}}_{22}&\text{in the case (B1)},\\
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}&\text{in the case (B3)},\\
{\mathcal{S}}_{21}&\text{in the case (B5)},\\
{\mathcal{S}}_{11}&\text{in the case (B7)}.\\
\end{cases}

The cases (B2), (B4) and (B6) occur when the line segments (
5.1
) are disjoint and
(x^{**},y^{**})\penalty\ \not\in\penalty\ D
. The nearest to
(x^{**},y^{**})
vertices of these line segments belong to
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
in the case (B2), to
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
in the case (B4) and to
{\mathcal{S}}_{21}
in the case (B6).

The main idea of our proof is the following :

First, we show that the point
(x^{**},y^{**})
either belongs to each of the line segments

(5.2)

[(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**}),\,(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**})]\quad\text{and}\quad[(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**}),\,(X_{2}(y^{**}),y^{**})]

or does not belong to any of them. With this result we describe all possible cases :

(5.3)

\begin{matrix}\text{(a)}&X_{1}(y^{**})<x^{**}<X_{2}(y^{**})\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{1}(x^{**})<y^{**}<Y_{2}(x^{**})\\
\text{(b)}&X_{1}(y^{**})<x^{**}=X_{2}(y^{**})\quad\text{ and }\quad Y_{1}(x^{**})<y^{**}=Y_{2}(x^{**})\\
\text{(c)}&x^{**}>X_{2}(y^{**})\quad\text{ and }\quad y^{**}>Y_{2}(x^{**})\\
\text{(d)}&x^{**}=X_{1}(y^{**})\quad\text{ and }\quad Y_{1}(x^{**})<y^{**}=Y_{2}(x^{**})\\
\text{(e)}&x^{**}<X_{1}(y^{**})\quad\text{ and }\quad y^{**}>Y_{2}(x^{**})\\
\text{(f)}&X_{1}(y^{**})<x^{**}=X_{2}(y^{**})\quad\text{ and }\quad y^{**}=Y_{1}(x^{**})\\
\text{(g)}&x^{**}>X_{2}(y^{**})\quad\text{ and }\quad y^{**}<Y_{1}(x^{**})\\
\text{(h)}&x^{**}=X_{1}(y^{**})\quad\text{ and }\quad y^{**}=Y_{1}(x^{**})\\
\text{(i)}&x^{**}<X_{1}(y^{**})\quad\text{ and }\quad y^{**}<Y_{1}(x^{**})\end{matrix}

Next, using (
2.10
), we prove that the cases (B0) - (B7) correspond respectively to the cases (a)-(h), and the case i) never holds.

5.2.
Preliminary results for the proof of Proposition
2.2

We begin our proof with the following preliminary results.

Lemma 5.1
.

Under the hypotheses (A1), for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, the line segments

[(x,Y_{1}(x)),(x,Y_{2}(x))]
and
[(X_{1}(y),y),(X_{2}(y),y)]

are disjoint if and only if
the point
(x,y)
does not belong to the set
D
.

Proof.

Indeed, by Lemma
4.1
, for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, the line segment
[(x,Y_{1}(x)),(x,Y_{2}(x))]
is the set of all points
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in D
with
x^{\prime}=x
, and similarly the line segment
[(X_{1}(y),y),(X_{2}(y),y)]
is the set of all points
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in D
with
y^{\prime}=y
. Hence, if
(x,y)\in D
then the both line segments
[(x,Y_{1}(x)),(x,Y_{2}(x))]
and
[(X_{1}(y),y),(X_{2}(y),y)]
contain the point
(x,y)
. Conversely, if these line segments are not disjoint, then the point
(x,y)
belongs to each of them, and consequently
(x,y)\in\penalty\ D
.
∎

Since for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, the point
(x,y)
is the only point that could belong to the both line segments
[(x,Y_{1}(x)),(x,Y_{2}(x))]
and
[(X_{1}(y),y),(X_{2}(y),y)]
, the above lemma implies the following statement.

Corollary 5.1
.

Under the hypotheses (A1), for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, if the point
(x,y)
does not belong to some of the line segments
[(x,Y_{1}(x)),(x,Y_{2}(x))]
or
[(X_{1}(y),y),(X_{2}(y),y)]
then
(x,y)
neither belongs to any of them.

By lemma
4.1
, for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, the points
Y_{1}(x)
and
(Y_{2}(x)
are the only real and positive solutions of the equation
P(x,y)=1
, and that
P(x,y)<1
for any
y\in]Y_{1}(x),Y_{2}(x)[
. Hence, for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, each of the points
(x,Y_{1}(x))
and
(x,Y_{2}(x))
belongs to the boundary of the set
D
, and for any
y\in]Y_{1}(x),Y_{2}(x)[
, the point
(x,y)
belongs to the interior
\overset{\circ}{D}
of
D
. Similarly, for any
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, each of the points
(X_{1}(y),y)
and
(X_{2}(y),y)
belongs to the boundary of
D
and for any
x\in]X_{1}(y),X_{2}(y)[
, the point
(x,y)
belongs to
\overset{\circ}{D}
. Using Corollary
5.1
it follows the following useful for our purpose property of the set
D
:

Corollary 5.2
.

Under the hypotheses (A1), for any
(x,y)\in D
, one and only one of the following assertions holds

–

the point
(x,y)
belongs to the interior of the set
D
and in this case,
X_{1}(y)<x<X_{2}(y)
and
Y_{1}(x)<y<Y_{2}(x)
;

–

the point
(x,y)
belongs to the boundary of
D
and in this case, the point
(x,y)
is an end point of each of the line segments
[(x,Y_{1}(x)),(x,Y_{2}(x))]
and
[(X_{1}(y),y),(X_{2}(y),y)]
.

By Lemma
4.1
, for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, we have

Y_{1}(x)=Y_{2}(x)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad x\in\{x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}\}

and, for any
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
,

X_{1}(y)=X_{2}(y)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad y\in\{y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}\},

and, the four points
(x^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}))
,
(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))
,
(X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),y^{*}_{P})
and
(X_{2}(y^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P})
are two by two distinct. Hence, the case with
x=X_{1}(y)=X_{2}(y)
and
y=Y_{1}(x)=Y_{2}(x)
is not possible, and as a straightforward consequence of Corollary
5.1
and Corollary
5.2
one gets

Corollary 5.3
.

Under the hypotheses (A1), for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, one and only one of the following cases holds:

(5.4)

\begin{matrix}\text{(a)}&X_{1}(y)<x<X_{2}(y)\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{1}(x)<y<Y_{2}(x)\\
\text{(b)}&x=X_{2}(y)>X_{1}(y)\quad\text{ and }\quad y=Y_{2}(x)>Y_{2}(x)\\
\text{(c)}&x>X_{2}(y)\quad\text{ and }\quad y>Y_{2}(x)\\
\text{(d)}&x=X_{1}(y)\quad\text{ and }\quad Y_{1}(x)<y=Y_{2}(x)\\
\text{(e)}&x<X_{1}(y)\quad\text{ and }\quad y>Y_{2}(x)\\
\text{(f)}&X_{1}(y)<x=X_{2}(y)\quad\text{ and }\quad y=Y_{1}(x)\\
\text{(g)}&x>X_{2}(y)\quad\text{ and }\quad y<Y_{1}(x)\\
\text{(h)}&x=X_{1}(y)\quad\text{ and }\quad y=Y_{1}(x)\\
\text{ (i) }&x<X_{1}(y)\quad\text{ and }\quad y<Y_{1}(x)\end{matrix}

In the case (a) of this statement, the point
(x,y)
belongs to the interior of the set
D
, in each of the cases (b), (d), (f) and (h), the point
(x,y)
belongs to the boundary of
D
, and in each of the cases (c), (e), (g) and (i), the point
(x,y)
does not belong to the set
D
.

When applied with
x=x^{**}
and
y=y^{**}
, this statement proves that under the hypotheses (A1), one of the assertions (a)-(i) of (
5.3
) holds.

The following statement will be used to investigate the position of the nearest to
(x^{**},y^{**})
vertices of the line segments (
5.2
) when the point
(x^{**},y^{**})
does not belong to the interior of
D
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Under the hypotheses (A1), for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, the following assertions hold :

1) If
x\leqslant X_{1}(y)
and
Y_{2}(x)\leqslant y
, then
(x,Y_{2}(x)),(X_{1}(y),y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
.

2) If
y\leqslant Y_{1}(x)
and
X_{2}(y)\leqslant x
, then
(X_{2}(y),y),\,(x,Y_{1}(x))\in{\mathcal{S}}_{21}
.

3) If
x\geqslant X_{2}(y)
and
y\geqslant X_{2}(x)
then
(x,Y_{2}(x)),(X_{2}(y),y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
.

4) If
x\leqslant X_{1}(y)
and
y\leqslant Y_{1}(x)
then
(x,Y_{1}(x)),\,(X_{1}(y),y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{11}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
and let
x\leqslant X_{1}(y)
and
Y_{2}(x)\leqslant y
. Then by Corollary
5.3
, either
x<X_{1}(y)
and
Y_{2}(x)<y
, or
x=X_{1}(y)
and
Y_{2}(x)=y
. In the second case, i.e when
x=X_{1}(y)
and
Y_{2}(x)=y
, we have
(x,Y_{2}(x))=(X_{1}(y),y)
and consequently, by the definition of
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
,

(x,Y_{2}(x))=(X_{1}(y),y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12}.

Consider now the case when
x<X_{1}(y)
and
Y_{2}(x)<y
. By the definition of the curves (
2.11
) - (
2.14
), we have

(x,Y_{2}(x))\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{22}\quad\text{and}\quad(X_{1}(y),y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{11}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{12}.

If we suppose that
(x,Y_{2}(x))\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
, then we will get
Y_{2}(x)\in[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]
and consequently, since
Y_{2}(x)<y\leqslant y^{**}_{P}
, we will have also
y\in[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]
. Since the function
Y_{2}
is strictly decreasing on
[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]
and since
y>Y_{2}(x)
, it follows that

(5.5)

X_{2}(x)<X_{2}\circ Y_{2}(x)=x

where the last relation holds because by Lemma
4.1
, the function
X_{2}:\penalty\ [Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]\to[X_{2}(y^{**}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]
is inverse to the function
Y_{2}:\penalty\ [X_{2}(y^{**}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]\to[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]
. Since
X_{1}(y)\leqslant X_{2}(y)
, (
5.5
) contradicts the inequality
x<X_{1}(y)
and consequently, when
x<X_{1}(y)
and
Y_{2}(x)<y
, the point
(x,Y_{2}(x))
belongs to
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
. Similar arguments show that in this case, the point
(X_{1}(y),y)
also belongs to
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
. The first assertion of Lemma
5.2
is therefore proved.
The proof of the assertions 2)-4) is quite similar.
∎

To investigate the cases (d) and (e) we will need the following preliminary result.

Lemma 5.3
.

If the conditions (A1) - (A3) are satisfied and
(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
, then

(5.6)

Y_{1}(x^{**})<Y_{2}(x^{**})\quad\text{and}\quad 1<Y_{2}(x^{**}).

Proof.

Indeed, under the hypotheses of this lemma, with the definition of
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
and using (
2.9
), one gets

(5.7)

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{*}<x^{**}\leqslant X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})<x^{**}_{P}.

Hence, in this case,
x^{**}\not=x^{*}_{P}
and
x^{**}\not=x^{**}_{P}
, and consequently, by Lemma
4.1
,
Y_{1}(x^{**})<Y_{2}(x^{**})
. The first relation of (
5.6
) is therefore proved. To get the second relation of (
5.6
), we recall that by Lemma
4.1
, the function
Y_{2}
is strictly increasing on the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})]
, and we remark that by(
5.7
) and (
2.10
) the following relations hold :

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant 1\leqslant x^{**}<X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}).

Hence,

Y_{2}(1)\leqslant Y_{2}(x^{**})

and moreover,

(5.8)

Y_{2}(1)<Y_{2}(x^{**})\quad\text{whenever}\quad 1<x^{**}.

Remark now that, with the definition of
Y_{1}(1)
and
Y_{2}(1)
and since
P(1,1)=1
, we have

(5.9)

\text{either }\quad Y_{1}(1)=1\leqslant Y_{2}(1)\quad\text{or}\quad Y_{1}(1)\leqslant 1=Y_{2}(1).

With these relations and using (
5.8
), one gets

1<Y_{2}(x^{**})\quad\text{whenever}\quad 1<x^{**}.

Now, to complete the proof of our lemma it is sufficient to show that

1<Y_{2}(1)\quad\text{whenever}\quad 1=x^{**}.

Suppose that
x^{**}=1
. Then from the first relation of (
5.6
) one gets

(5.10)

Y_{1}(1)<Y_{2}(1),

and by Corollary
4.1
and using (
5.7
), we obtain

\phi(1,Y_{1}(1))=1.

Since the function
y\to\phi(1,y)
is strictly increasing, from the last relation and (
5.10
) it follows that

\phi_{1}(1,Y_{2}(1))>1,

and since under our hypotheses,
\phi_{1}(1,1)\leqslant 1
, this proves that
Y_{2}(1)\not=1
. Hence, using again (
5.10
), we conclude that

Y_{2}(x^{**})=Y_{2}(1)>Y_{1}(1)=1.

Lemma
5.3
is therefore proved.
∎

To investigate the cases (h) and i) the following lemma will be used.

Lemma 5.4
.

Suppose that the condition (A1) is satisfied and let
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
,
y\in[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in D
be such that

(5.11)

x^{\prime}\leqslant x\leqslant X_{1}(y)\quad\text{ and }\quad y^{\prime}\leqslant y\leqslant Y_{1}(x).

Then

(5.12)

x^{\prime}=x=X_{1}(y)\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad y^{\prime}=y=Y_{1}(x).

Proof.

Indeed, suppose that the conditions of our lemma are satisfied and let (
5.11
) holds. Then according to the definition of the line segments
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
, we have
x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{\prime}\leqslant x^{**}_{P}
and
y^{*}_{P}\leqslant y^{\prime}\leqslant y^{**}_{P}
, and by Lemma
5.2
, the points
(x,Y_{1}(x))
and
(X_{1}(y),y)
belong to the set
{S}_{11}
. Using the definition of
{\mathcal{S}}_{11}
, it follows that

(5.13)

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{\prime}\leqslant x<X_{1}(y^{*}_{P})\quad\text{and}\quad y^{*}_{P}\leqslant y^{\prime}\leqslant y\leqslant Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}).

Since by Lemma
4.1
, the function
Y_{1}
is decreasing on the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{*}_{P})]
and the function
X_{1}
is decreasing on the line segment
[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P})]
, relations (
5.13
) imply that
Y_{1}(x^{\prime})\geqslant Y_{1}(x)
and
X_{1}(y^{\prime})\geqslant X_{1}(y)
,
and consequently, using (
5.11
) one gets

(5.14)

y^{\prime}\leqslant y\leqslant Y_{1}(x)\leqslant Y_{1}(x^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad x^{\prime}\leqslant x\leqslant X_{1}(y)\leqslant X_{1}(y^{\prime}).

Under the hypotheses of our lemma,
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in D
and by Corollary
5.1
and the definition of the line segments
[X_{1}(y^{\prime}),X_{2}(y^{\prime})]
and
[Y_{1}(x^{\prime}),Y_{2}(x^{\prime})]
, we have

X_{1}(y^{\prime})\leqslant x^{\prime}\leqslant X_{2}(y^{\prime})\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{1}(x^{\prime})\leqslant y^{\prime}\leqslant Y_{2}(x^{\prime}).

When combined with (
5.14
) these relations prove (
5.12
).
∎

5.3.
Proof of Proposition
2.2

Now we are ready to complete the proof of Proposition
2.2
. Under our hypotheses, by Corollary
5.3
applied with
x=x^{**}
and
y=y^{**}
, one and only one of the cases (a)- (i) of (
5.3
) holds, and remark that the case (a) is equivalent to the case (B0).

Remark furthermore that the case (B1) implies (b), and conversely, if the case (b) of (
5.3
) holds, then by Lemma
5.2
applied with
x=x^{**}
and
y=y^{**}
, the point
(x^{**},y^{**})
belongs to the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
, and by Lemma
4.1
,

x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad y^{**}<y^{**}_{P}

because in this case, we have
Y_{1}(x^{**})<Y_{2}(x^{**})
and
X_{1}(y^{**})<X_{2}(y^{**})
, The case (B1) is therefore equivalent to the case (b).

Similarly, the case (B2) implies the case (c) of (
5.3
) and conversely, in the case (c), by Lemma
5.2
applied with
x=x^{**}
and
y=y^{**}
, the points
(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))
and
(X_{2}(y^{**}),y^{**})
belong to the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
. The case (B2) is therefore equivalent to the case (c) of (
5.3
).

And similarly, the case (B3) (resp. (B4)) implies the case (d) (resp (e)), and conversely, if (d) (resp. (e)) holds, then by Lemma
5.2
applied with
x=x^{**}
and
y=y^{**}
the point
(x^{**},y^{**})=(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**})=(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))
belongs to the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
(resp. the points

(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))
and
(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**})
belong to the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
), and by Lemma
5.3
combined with Lemma
4.1
and (
2.10
), one gets

Y_{1}(x^{**})<Y_{2}(x^{**}),\quad x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}\quad\text{and}\quad y^{*}\leqslant 1<Y_{2}(x^{**}).

The case (d) is therefore equivalent to the case (B3), and the case (e) is equivalent to (B4).

Similar arguments (it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
) show that the case (f) is equivalent to (B5) and the case (g) is equivalent to (B6).

Now, to complete the proof of our proposition it is sufficient to show that the case (i) of (
5.3
) never holds, and the case (h) is equivalent to (B7). To get this result we apply Lemma
5.4
with
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(1,1)
and
(x,y)=(x^{**},y^{**})
.
By relations (
2.9
) and (
2.10
), we have

(5.15)

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{*}\leqslant 1\leqslant x^{**}\leqslant x^{**}_{P}\quad\text{and}\quad y^{*}_{P}\leqslant y^{*}\leqslant 1\leqslant y^{**}\leqslant y^{**}_{P}.

Hence, when either (h) or (i) holds, i.e. if

X_{1}(y^{**})\geqslant x^{**}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{1}(x^{**})\geqslant y^{**},

using Lemma
5.4
with
x=x^{**}
,
y=y^{**}
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(1,1)
we obtain

(5.16)

(x^{**},y^{**})=(1,1)\quad\text{and}\quad X_{1}(1)=Y_{1}(1)=1.

The case (i) is therefore impossible and the case (h) is equivalent to (
5.16
). Remark moreover that by Lemma
5.2
applied with
x=x^{**}=1
and
y=y^{**}=1
, from (
5.16
) it follows that the point
(1,1)
belongs to the curve
{\mathcal{S}}_{11}

and consequently, by Lemma
4.1
,

(5.17)

\partial_{x}P(1,1)\leqslant 0\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad\partial_{y}P(1,1)\leqslant 0,

and notice that by (
5.16
) and using (
2.9
) and (
2.10
),

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{*}<x^{**}=1\quad\;\text{and}\quad\;y^{*}_{P}\leqslant y^{*}<y^{**}=1.

Since the points
(x,y)=(x^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}))
and
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=(X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),y^{*}_{P})
are the only points in
{\mathcal{S}}_{11}
for which
\partial_{y}P(x,y)=0
and
\partial_{x}P(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=0
, the last relations show that
\partial_{x}P(1,1)\not=0
and
\partial_{y}P(1,1)\not=0
. Using (
5.17
) we conclude therefore that when (h) holds, we have also
\partial_{x}P(1,1)<0
and
\partial_{y}P(1,1)<0
and consequently the case (h) is equivalent to (B7).

6.
The Functional Equation and the Convergence Domain

6.1.
Sketch of the proof of Theorem
1

The main ideas of the proof of Theorem
1
are the following : By using the method of Lyapunov functions, we first show that the series

(6.1)

H_{j}(x,y)=\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(j,k)x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}},\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}

(and consequently, also the series (
2.28
) and (
2.29
)) converge on a suitable polycircular set
\Omega(\Theta)
closed to the points
(x_{d},0)
and
(0,y_{d})
. This is a subject of Proposition
6.1
below. With this preliminary result, we will be able to get the first assertion of Theorem
1
and to introduce on the set
\Omega(\Theta)
, the functional equation (
2.30
) (see Proposition
6.3
). Next, we show that the functions at the right hand side of (
2.30
) are analytic in the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\penalty\ |x|<x_{d},\;|y|<y_{d}\}
, and we extend in this way, first the function
(x,y)\to R_{j}(x,y)=Q(x,u)h_{j}(x,y)
and next the function
(x,y)\to h_{j}(x,y)
as analytic functions to the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\penalty\ |x|<x_{d},\;|y|<y_{d}\}
.

Definition 8
.

If one of the cases (B0)-(B2) holds, we define
\Theta
as the logarithmically convex hull of the union of the two rectangles
[0,x_{d}[\times[0,Y_{1}(x_{d})[
and
[0,X_{1}(y_{d})[\times[0,y_{d}[
(see Figure
10
) :

(6.2)

\Theta=\text{LogCH}\Bigl\{\bigl([0,x_{d}[\times[0,Y_{1}(x_{d})[\bigr)\cup\bigl([0,X_{1}(y_{d})[\times[0,y_{d}[\bigr)\Bigr\}.

In the case when one of the assertions (B3)-(B6) holds, we let

(6.3)

\Theta=[0,x_{d}[\times[0,y_{d}[.

Proposition 6.1
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A4), the series (
6.1
) converges on the set

(6.4)

\Omega(\Theta)=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}\penalty\ :\penalty\ (|x|,|y|)\in\Theta\}.

In the case when the random walk
(Z(n))
is positive recurrent, this statement follows from the results of Miyazawa
[
22
]
. In Section
6.2
, we give another proof of this statement by using the method of Lyapunov functions. Our proof is valid both for positive recurrent and for transient random walks.

Since for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, and
(x,y)\in]0,+\infty[^{2}
,

xyh_{j}(x,y)\leqslant H_{j}(x,y),\quad xh_{1j}(x)=H_{j}(x,0)\leqslant H_{j}(x,y)\quad\text{and}\quad yh_{2j}(y)=H_{j}(0,y)\leqslant H_{j}(x,y),

as a straightforward consequence of Proposition
6.1
, one gets

Corollary 6.2
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A4), for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

i) the series (
2.29
) converge (and consequently the functions
h_{1j}
and
h_{2j}
are analytic) respectively in
B(0,x_{d})
and in
B(0,y_{d})
, with
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
defined respectively by (
2.21
) and (
2.22
);

ii) the series (
2.28
) converge on the set
\Omega(\Theta)
and consequently, the function
h_{j}
is analytic in
\Omega(\Theta)
.

With this results, using classical arguments (see Section
6.3
) we obtain

Proposition 6.3
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A4), for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
(x,y)\in\Omega(\Theta)
, the functional equation (
2.30
) holds.

By the definition of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
, see relations (
2.21
) and (
2.22
), and of the sets
\Theta
and
{\Gamma}
, when either (B3) or (B4) holds, we have the relations

x_{d}=x^{**},\quad y_{d}=Y_{2}(x_{d})\quad\text{and}\quad\Theta=[0,x_{d}[\times[0,Y_{2}(x_{d})[=\{(x,y)\in{\Gamma}:\,x<x_{d},\;y<y_{d}\},

and when either (B5) or (B6) holds,

y_{d}=y^{**},\quad x_{d}=X_{2}(y_{d})\quad\text{and}\quad\Theta=[0,X_{2}(y_{d})[\times[0,x_{d}[=\{(x,y)\in{\Gamma}:\,x<x_{d},\;y<y_{d}\}.

Hence, when one of the case (B3)-(B6) occurs, Theorem
1
follows from Corollary
6.2
and Proposition
6.3
.

When one of the cases (B0)-(B2) occurs, the first assertion of Theorem
1
follows from Corollary
6.2
as well, and
to prove the second assertion of Theorem
1
the following proposition will be used.

Proposition 6.4
.

If the conditions (A1)-(A3) are satisfied and one of the assertions (B0)-(B2) holds, then

(6.5)

\{(x,y)\in{\Gamma}:\,x<x_{d},\;y<y_{d}\}\subset\Theta\cup\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:\,x<x_{d},\;y<y_{d}\}.

With this lemma and using our previous results we will be able to show first that the function
(x,y){\to}h_{j}(x,y)
can be continued to the set
\Omega_{d}(\gamma)
as a meromorphic function, and next to show that it is in fact analytic in this set.

The proof of Theorem
1
is organized as follows:

–

Proposition
6.1
is proved in Section
6.2
;

–

Section
6.3
is devoted to the proof of Proposition
6.3
;

–

the proof of Proposition
6.4
is given in Section
6.4
;

–

the proof of Theorem
1
is completed in Section
6.5
.

6.2.
Proof of Proposition
6.1

6.2.1.
The main idea of the proof.

To main tool of our proof of this proposition is the method of Lyapunov functions. We show that for any
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
such that

(6.6)

x_{1}>x_{2}\quad\text{and}\quad y_{1}<y_{2},

the function
f:\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}\to[0,+\infty[
defined by

(6.7)

f(k_{1},k_{2})=x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}},\quad(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}.

satisfies

(6.8)

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}\bigl(f(Z(1))\bigr)\leqslant\theta f(j),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E,

with some
0<\theta<1
and for some finite subset
E\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
. This is a subject of lemma
6.1
below. With this result we are able to prove that for any two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfying (
6.6
), the series

(6.9)

\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}}g(j,k)(x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}})

converges, this is a subject of Lemma
6.2
. Next, to prove that the series (
6.1
) converge for any
(x,y)\in\Theta
, we show that for any
(x,y)\in\Theta
, there are two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfying (
6.6
) and such that

(6.10)

x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}\leqslant x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}},\quad\forall k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}.

6.2.2.
Preliminary results for the proof of Proposition
6.1
.

We begin our analysis with the following statements.

Lemma 6.1
.

Suppose that the conditions (A1)-(A3) are satisfied and let two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfy (
6.6
). Then for some finite set
E\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the function

f:\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}\to]0,+\infty[
defined by (
6.7
) satisfies (
6.8
) with some
0<\theta<1
.

Proof.

Consider two functions
f_{1},f_{2}:\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

f_{1}(j_{1},j_{2})=x_{1}^{j_{1}}y_{1}^{j_{2}},\quad\text{and}\quad f_{2}(j_{1},j_{2})=x_{2}^{j_{1}}y_{2}^{j_{2}},\quad\forall(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2},

and let

\tilde{\theta}=\max\{\phi_{1}(x_{1},y_{1}),\;P(x_{1},y_{1}),\;\phi_{2}(x_{2},y_{2}),\;P(x_{2},y_{2})\}.

Then
0<\tilde{\theta}<1
because
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
, and moreover, for any
(j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}\{0\}
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(f_{1}(Z(1))\leqslant\begin{cases}\tilde{\theta}f_{1}(j_{1},j_{2}),&\text{if}\;j_{1}\not=0,\\
\phi_{2}(x_{1},y_{1})y_{1}^{j_{2}},&\text{if}\;j_{1}=0,\end{cases}

and

{\mathbb{E}}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(f_{2}(Z(1))\leqslant\begin{cases}\tilde{\theta}f_{2}(j_{1},j_{2}),&\text{if}\;j_{2}\not=0,\\
\phi_{1}(x_{2},y_{2})x_{2}^{j_{1}}&\text{if}\;j_{2}=0.\end{cases}

For the function
f=f_{1}+f_{2}
, we obtain therefore that for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}\{0\}
,

(6.11)

{\mathbb{E}}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}(f(Z(1))\leqslant\begin{cases}\tilde{\theta}f(j_{1},j_{2})&\text{if $j_{1}>0$ and $j_{2}>0$}\\
\tilde{\theta}y_{2}^{j_{2}}+\phi_{2}(x_{1},y_{1})y_{1}^{j_{2}}&\text{if $j_{1}=0$ }\\
\tilde{\theta}x_{1}^{j_{1}}+\phi_{1}(x_{2},y_{2})x_{2}^{j_{1}}&\text{if $j_{2}=0$}.\end{cases}

Since under hypotheses of our lemma,
x_{1}>x_{2}>0
and
0<y_{1}<y_{2}
, we have moreover

\lim_{j_{1}}(x_{2}/x_{1})^{j_{1}}=\lim_{j_{2}\to\infty}(y_{1}/y_{2})^{j_{2}}=0,

and consequently, for any
{\varepsilon}>0
there is
N_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any
(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
j_{2}>N_{\varepsilon}
and
j_{1}=0
,

\tilde{\theta}y_{2}^{j_{2}}+\phi_{2}(x_{1},y_{1})y_{1}^{j_{2}}\leqslant(\tilde{\theta}+{\varepsilon})y_{2}^{j_{2}}=(\tilde{\theta}+{\varepsilon})f_{2}(j_{1},j_{2})\leqslant(\tilde{\theta}+{\varepsilon})f(j_{1},j_{2})

and similarly, for any
(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
j_{1}>N_{\varepsilon}
and
j_{2}=0
,

\tilde{\theta}x_{1}^{j_{1}}+\phi_{1}(x_{2},y_{2})x_{2}^{j_{1}}\leqslant(\tilde{\theta}+{\varepsilon})f(j_{1},j_{2}).

Hence, for
{\varepsilon}>0
such that
\tilde{\theta}+{\varepsilon}<1
, letting

E=\{(j_{1},0)\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}\penalty\ :\penalty\ j_{1}\leqslant N_{\varepsilon}\}\cup\{(0,j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}\penalty\ :\;j_{2}\leqslant N_{\varepsilon}\}

one gets (
6.8
) with
\theta=\tilde{\theta}+{\varepsilon}<1
.
∎

Lemma 6.2
.

Under the hypotheses of Lemma
6.1
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
, the series (
6.9
) converges.

Proof.

Indeed, consider two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfying (
6.6
) and let the function

f:\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}\to]0,+\infty[
be defined by (
6.7
). Then by Lemma
6.1
, for some finite set
E\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
and some positive number
\theta<1
, (
6.8
) holds. Without any restriction of generality, we will suppose that
E
contains the origin
(0,0)
. Denote by
\tau_{E}
the first time when the process
(Z(n))
hits the set
E
:

\tau_{E}=\inf\{n\geqslant 1:\penalty\ Z(n)\in E\},

and let

g_{E}(j,k)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=k,\;\tau_{E}\geqslant n),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}E,\;k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}.

Then by (
6.8
), for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(f(Z(n));\;\tau_{E}\geqslant n)\leqslant\theta^{n}f(j),\quad\forall n\in{\mathbb{N}},

and consequently,

(6.12)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g_{E}(j,k)\!\!\left(x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}}\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g_{E}(j,k)f(k)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\mathbb{E}}_{j}\bigl(f(Z(n));\;\tau_{E}\geqslant n\bigr)\leqslant f(j)(1-\theta)^{-1}.

Using the identity

\displaystyle g(j,k)=g_{E}(j,k)+\sum_{\ell\in E{\setminus}\{0\}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}E}g(j,\ell){\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}(Z(1)=m)g_{E}(m,k)

from the above relation it follows that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(j,k)\bigl(x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\theta}\Bigl(f(j)+\sum_{\ell\in E{\setminus}\{0\}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}E}g(j,\ell){\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}(Z(1)=m)f(m)\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\theta}\Bigl(f(j)+\sum_{\ell\in E{\setminus}\{0\}}g(j,\ell){\mathbb{E}}_{\ell}(f(Z(1)))\Bigr).

Since the set
E
is finite and under the hypotheses (A1) and (A3),
{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell}(f(Z(1)))<+\infty
for any
\ell\in E
, this proves that the series (
6.9
) converges for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E
. To prove that this series converges for
j\in E
, it is sufficient now to notice that for
j\in E
,

g(j,k)=\sum_{\ell\in E{\setminus}\{0\}}g(j,\ell)\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}E}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}(Z(1)=m)g_{E}(m,k),

and consequently,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(j,k)\left(x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant\sum_{\ell\in E{\setminus}\{0\}}g(j,\ell)\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}E}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}(Z(1)=m)\!\!\!\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g_{E}(m,k)\left(x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}}\right)<+\infty.

∎

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose that the conditions (A1)-(A3) are satisfied and let
\tilde{x}\in]x^{*},x^{**}[
and
\tilde{y}\in]y^{*},y^{**}[
be such that

(6.13)

\tilde{x}>X_{1}(\tilde{y})\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{y}>Y_{1}(\tilde{x}).

Then for any
y_{1}>Y_{1}(\tilde{x})
closed enough to
Y_{1}(\tilde{x})
and any
x_{2}>X_{1}(\tilde{y})
closed enough to
X_{1}(\tilde{y})
, the points
(x_{1},y_{1})=(\tilde{x},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})=(x_{2},\tilde{y})
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
6.1
.

Proof.

Indeed, by Lemma
4.2
, for
\tilde{x}\in]x^{*},x^{**}[
and
\tilde{y}\in]y^{*},y^{**}[
, each of the line segments
[Y_{1}(\tilde{x}),\tilde{Y}_{2}(\tilde{x})]=\{y>0:\penalty\ (\tilde{x},y)\in D\cap D_{1}\}
and
[X_{1}(\tilde{y}),\tilde{X}_{2}(\tilde{y})]=\{x>0:\penalty\ (x,\tilde{y})\in D\cap D_{2}\}
has a non-zero length, and for any
y_{1}
and
x_{2}
such that
Y_{1}(\tilde{x})<y_{1}<\tilde{Y}_{2}(\tilde{x})
and
X_{1}(\tilde{y})<x_{2}<\tilde{X}_{2}(\tilde{y})
, one has

(\tilde{x},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}},\quad\text{and}\quad(x_{2},\tilde{y})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}.

Using (
6.13
), this proves that for any
y_{1}
and
x_{2}
such that
Y_{1}(\tilde{x})<y_{1}<\min\{\tilde{Y}_{2}(\tilde{x}),\tilde{y}\}
and
X_{1}(\tilde{y})<x_{2}<\min\{\tilde{x},\tilde{X}_{2}(\tilde{y})\}
, the points
(x_{1},y_{1})=(\tilde{x},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})=(x_{2},\tilde{y})
satisfy the conditions of Lemma
6.1
.
∎

Now we are ready to get

Lemma 6.4
.

Suppose that the conditions (A1)-(A4) are satisfied and let

(6.14)

\Theta_{0}=\begin{cases}\bigl([0,x_{d}[\times[0,Y_{1}(x_{d})[\bigr)\cup\bigl([0,X_{1}(y_{d})[\times[0,y_{d}[\bigr)&\text{if either (B0), or (B1) or (B2) holds}\\
[0,x_{d}[\times[0,Y_{2}(x_{d})[&\text{if either (B3) or (B4) holds}\\
[0,X_{1}(y_{d})[\times[0,y_{d}[&\text{if either (B5) or (B6) holds}.\\
\end{cases}

Then for any
(x,y)\in\Theta_{0}
, there are two points
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
satisfying the conditions of Lemma
6.1
and relations (
6.10
).

Proof.

Suppose first that one of the cases (B0), (B1) or (B2) occurs and let
(x,y)\in[0,x_{d}[\times[0,Y_{1}(x_{d})[
. The definitions of the cases (B0), (B1), (B2) and of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see Proposition
2.2
and relations (
2.33
), (
2.34
)), give

\max\{X_{1}(y_{d}),x\}<x_{d}=x^{**}\quad\text{and}\quad y<Y_{1}(x_{d})<y_{d}=y^{**},

and by (
2.9
),

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{*}<x^{**}=x_{d}\quad\text{and}\quad y^{*}_{P}\leqslant y^{*}<y^{**}=y_{d}.

Since the functions
X_{1}:[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]\to[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
Y_{1}:[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]\to[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
are continuous, it follows that for any
\tilde{x}\in]\max\{x^{*},x\},x_{d}[
and
\tilde{y}\in]y^{*},y_{d}[
closed enough respectively to
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
one has

(6.15)

0\leqslant x<\tilde{x},\quad\quad 0\leqslant y<Y_{1}(\tilde{x}),

(6.16)

X_{1}(\tilde{y})<\tilde{x}\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{1}(\tilde{x})<\tilde{y}.

By Lemma
6.3
, from (
6.16
) it follows that for
y_{1}>Y_{1}(\tilde{x})
and
x_{2}>X_{1}(\tilde{y})
closed enough respectively to
Y_{1}(\tilde{x})
and
X_{1}(\tilde{y})
, the points
(x_{1},y_{1})=(\tilde{x},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})=(x_{2},\tilde{y})

satisfy the conditions of Lemma
6.1
, and using (
6.15
) one gets (
6.10
).

When one of the cases (B0), (B1) or (B2) holds and
(x,y)\in[0,x_{d}[\times[0,Y_{1}(x_{d})[
, our lemma is therefore proved. For
(x,y)\in[0,X_{1}(y_{d})[\times[0,y_{d}[
, the proof is quite similar.

Suppose now that either (B3) or (B4) holds. The definitions of the cases (B3) and (B4) and of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see Proposition
2.2
and relations (
2.33
), (
2.34
)), and by (
2.9
), give

(6.17)

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{*}<x^{**}=x_{d}\leqslant X_{1}(y^{**}),

(6.18)

y^{*}\leqslant 1<y_{d}=Y_{2}(x_{d})\leqslant y^{**},

(6.19)

(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**}),\,(x^{**},Y_{2}(x^{**}))=(x_{d},y_{d})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12},

and

(6.20)

Y_{1}(x_{d})<Y_{2}(x_{d}).

From the definition of
{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
and relation (
6.19
), it follows that

X_{1}(y^{**})\leqslant X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})

and consequently, by (
6.17
),

(6.21)

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{*}<x_{d}\leqslant X_{1}(y^{**})\leqslant X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}).

Consider now a point
(x,y)\in\Theta_{0}
. Then by (
6.14
) and using (
6.21
), (
6.18
) and (
6.20
),

(6.22)

0\leqslant\max\{x^{*},x\}<x_{d}\leqslant X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})\quad\text{and}\quad 0\leqslant\max\{Y_{1}(x_{d}),y^{*},y\}<y_{d}=Y_{2}(x_{d})\leqslant y^{**}

Since the function
Y_{2}
is strictly increasing on the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})]
and the functions
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
are continuous on
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, it follows that for any
\hat{x}
and
\tilde{x}
closed enough to
x_{d}
and such that

(6.23)

\max\{x^{*},x\}<\hat{x}<\tilde{x}<x^{**}=x_{d},

one has

\max\{Y_{1}(\tilde{x}),y^{*},y\}<Y_{2}(\hat{x})<Y_{2}(\tilde{x})<Y_{2}(x_{d})=y_{d}\leqslant y^{**},

Remark that because of (
6.21
) and (

), the points
\hat{x}
and
\tilde{x}
belong to the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})]
.
Since the functions

and
Y_{2}:[x^{*}_{P},X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})]\to[Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]
are inverse to each other, letting
\tilde{y}=Y_{2}(\hat{x})
we get therefore
X_{1}(\tilde{y})=\hat{x}
and using the above relations we obtain

(6.24)

y^{*}<\tilde{y}=Y_{2}(\hat{x})<y^{**},\quad Y_{1}(\tilde{x})<Y_{2}(\hat{x})=\tilde{y},\quad X_{1}(\tilde{y})=\hat{x}<\tilde{x},

(6.25)

x<\hat{x}=X_{1}(\tilde{y})\quad\text{and }\quad y<Y_{2}(\hat{x})=\tilde{y}.

By Lemma
6.3
, from (
6.23
) and (

) it follows that for any
y_{1}>Y_{1}(\tilde{x})
closed enough to
Y_{1}(\tilde{x})
and
x_{2}>X_{1}(\tilde{y})
closed enough to
X_{1}(\tilde{y})
, the points

(x_{1},y_{1})=(\tilde{x},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})=(x_{2},\tilde{y})

satisfy the conditions of Lemma
6.1
, and using moreover (
6.25
), we get that for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}}\leqslant(X_{1}(\tilde{y}))^{j_{1}}\tilde{y}^{j_{2}}\leqslant x_{2}^{j_{1}}y_{2}^{j_{2}}\leqslant x_{1}^{j_{1}}y_{1}^{j_{2}}+x_{2}^{j_{1}}y_{2}^{j_{2}}.

Hence, in the case when either (B3) or (B4) holds, Lemma
6.3
is also proved. To prove this lemma the case when either (B5) or (B6) holds, it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
.
∎

6.2.3.
Proof of Proposition
6.1

This proposition is a consequence of Lemma
6.2
and Lemma
6.3
: by Lemma
6.3
, for any
(x,y)\in\Theta_{0}
, there are two points
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
for which the conditions of Lemma
6.1
are satisfied and relations (
6.10
) hold. By Lemma Lemma
6.2
and using (
6.10
), this proves that

\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(j,k)x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}\leqslant\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(j,k)\left(x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}}\right)<+\infty.

Hence, for any
(x,y)\in\Theta_{0}
, the series (
6.1
) converge. Since the set
\Theta
is the logarithmically convex hull of the set
\Theta_{0}
, and the domain of convergence of power series with center
0
is always logarithmically convex, this proves that the series (
6.1
) converge in
\Omega(\Theta)
.

6.3.
Proof of Proposition
6.3

Consider first the case when
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\not=(0,0)
.
By Proposition
6.1
, the series (
6.1
)
converge on the set
\Omega(\Theta)
. Hence, for any
(x,y)\in\Omega(\Theta)
with non-zero
x
and
y
, by the Fubini theorem and using the Markov property, one gets

\displaystyle H_{j}(x,y)

\displaystyle=x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}}+\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=k,\;\tau_{0}>n)x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}

(6.26)

\displaystyle=x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}}+\sum_{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}}g(j,\ell){\mathbb{E}}_{\ell}\left(x^{Z_{1}(1)}y^{Z_{2}(1)},\;\tau_{0}>1\right)

Because of Assumptions (A1)-(A3), for
(x,y)\in\Omega(\Theta)
with
x\not=0
and
y\not=0
, we have

{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell}\left(x^{Z_{1}(1)}y^{Z_{2}(1)},\;\tau_{0}>1\right)=\begin{cases}x^{\ell_{1}}y^{\ell_{2}}P(x,y)-\mu(-1,-1)\mathbb{1}_{\{(1,1)\}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})&\text{if $\ell_{1}>0$ and $\ell_{2}>0$,}\\
x^{\ell_{1}}\,\phi_{1}(x,y)-\mu_{1}(-1,0)\mathbb{1}_{\{(1,0)\}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})&\text{if $\ell_{1}>0$ and $\ell_{2}=0$,}\\
y^{\ell_{2}}\,\phi_{2}(x,y)-\mu_{2}(0,-1)\mathbb{1}_{\{(0,1)\}}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})&\text{if $\ell_{1}=0$ and $\ell_{2}>0$.}\end{cases}

Using these relations in (
6.26
), for
(x,y)\in\Omega(\Theta)
with non-zero
x
and
y
, one gets therefore

\displaystyle H_{j}(x,y)

\displaystyle=x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}}-\mu(-1,-1)g(j,(1,1))-\mu_{1}(-1,0)g(j,(1,0))-\mu_{2}(0,-1)g(j,(0,1))

\displaystyle+P(x,y)\hskip-17.07164pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}:\\
\ell_{1}>0,\;\ell_{2}>0\end{subarray}}g(j,\ell)x^{\ell_{1}}y^{\ell_{2}}+\phi_{1}(x,y)\!\!\sum_{\ell_{1}=1}^{\infty}g(j,(\ell_{1},0))x^{\ell_{1}}+\;\phi_{2}(x,y)\!\!\sum_{\ell_{2}=1}^{\infty}g(j,(0,\ell_{2}))y^{\ell_{2}}

or equivalently,

H_{j}(x,y)=L_{j}(x,y)+xyP(x,y)h_{j}(x,y)+x\phi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)+y\phi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y)\\

with

\displaystyle L_{j}(x,y)

\displaystyle=x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}}-\mu(-1,-1)g(j,(1,1))-\mu_{1}(-1,0)g(j,(1,0))-\mu_{2}(0,-1)g(j,(0,1))

\displaystyle=x^{j_{1}}y^{j_{2}}-{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty).

Since clearly
H_{j}(x,y)=xyh_{j}(x,y)+xh_{1j}(x)+yh_{2j}(y)
, the last relation proves (
2.30
) for any
(x,y)\in\Omega(\Theta)
and

j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{{\setminus}}\{(0,0)\}
. To get (
2.30
) for
(j_{1},j_{2})=(0,0)
, it is sufficient now to notice that for any
(x,y)\in\Omega(\Theta)
,

H_{(0,0)}(x,y)=\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}}{\mathbb{P}}_{(0,0)}\bigl(Z(1)=(j_{1},j_{2})\bigr)H_{j}(x,y)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}}\hskip-17.07182pt{\mathbb{P}}_{(0,0)}\bigl(Z(1)=(j_{1},j_{2})\bigr)L_{j}(x,y)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{(0,0)}\left(x^{Z_{1}(1)}y^{Z_{2}(1)};\;\tau_{0}>1\right)-{\mathbb{P}}_{(0,0)}(1<\tau_{0}<+\infty)

\displaystyle=\phi_{0}(x,y)-{\mathbb{P}}_{(0,0)}(\tau_{0}<+\infty).

6.4.
Proof of Proposition
6.4

Suppose that one of the cases (B0)-(B2) holds. Then by Proposition
2.2
and the definition of the points
x_{d}
,
y_{d}
, the following relations hold

(6.27)

x_{d}=x^{**}>X_{1}(y_{d}),\quad\quad y_{d}=y^{**}>Y_{1}(x_{d}),

and the set
\Theta
is defined as a logarithmically convex hull of the set

([0,x_{d}[\times[0,Y_{1}(x_{d})[)\cup([0,X_{1}(y_{d})[\times[0,y_{d}[).

The points
(x_{1},y_{2})=(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))
and
(x_{2},y_{2})=(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})
are therefore on the boundary of the set
\Theta
and also on the boundary of the set
D
. Since the set
\Theta
is logarithmically convex and the set
D
is strictly logarithmically convex, it follows that for any
0<\theta<1
, the point
(x_{\theta},y_{\theta})
, with
x_{\theta}=x_{1}^{\theta}x_{2}^{1-\theta}
and
y_{\theta}=y_{1}^{\theta}y_{2}^{1-\theta}
, belongs to the set
\overline{\Theta}\cap\overset{\circ}{D}
, and consequently,

Y_{1}(x_{\theta})<y_{\theta}<Y_{2}(x_{\theta}).

By the definition of the set
\Theta
, for any
0<\theta<1
and
y\in[0,y_{\theta}[
, the point
(x_{\theta},y)
is in
\Theta
, and for any
y\in]Y_{1}(x_{\theta}),Y_{2}(x_{\theta})[
and the point
(x_{\theta},y)
is in
\overset{\circ}{D}
. Consequently, for any
0<\theta<1
and
y\in[0,Y_{2}(x_{\theta})]
, the point
(x_{\theta},y)
belongs to the set
\overline{\Theta}\cup\overset{\circ}{D}
, or equivalently, that for any
y\geqslant 0
,

(6.28)

y\geqslant Y_{2}(x_{\theta})\quad\text{whenever}\quad(x_{\theta},y)\not\in\overline{\Theta}\cup\overset{\circ}{D}.

Consider now a point
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in[0,+\infty[^{2}
with
\hat{x}<x_{d}
and
\hat{y}<y_{d}
and such that
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\not\in(\Theta\cup\overset{\circ}{D})
. Then
\hat{x}\geqslant X_{1}(y_{d})
(because otherwise
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in[0,X_{1}(y_{d})[\times[0,y_{d})[\subset\Theta
), and consequently for some
0<\theta\leqslant 1
,
\hat{x}=x_{\theta}=x_{d}^{\theta}(X_{1}(y_{d}))^{1-\theta}
. Hence, by (
6.28
), we get
\hat{y}\geqslant Y_{2}(\hat{x})
, and with similar arguments (it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
) we obtain
\hat{x}\geqslant X_{2}(\hat{y})
. By Lemma
5.2
, these inequalities show that
(\hat{x},Y_{2}(\hat{x})),(X_{2}(\hat{y}),\hat{y})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
. Hence, if we suppose that for some
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in D
, the inequalities
\hat{x}\leqslant x^{\prime}
and
\hat{y}\leqslant y^{\prime}
hold, then using the similar arguments as in the proof of Lemma
5.4
, we will obtain that
\hat{x}=x^{\prime}=X_{2}(\hat{y})
and
\hat{y}=y^{\prime}=Y_{2}(\hat{x})
. With the definition of the set
{\Gamma}
, this proves that
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\not\in{\Gamma}
.

6.5.
Proof of Theorem
1

Now, we are ready to complete the proof of Theorem
1
.
In the cases (B3)-(B6), Theorem
1
