follows from Corollary
6.2
and Proposition
6.3
, and in the cases (B0)-(B2), the first assertion of Theorem
1
follows from Corollary
6.2
as well.To complete the proof of Theorem
1
we have therefore to prove its second assertion in the cases (B0)-(B2).

We know that, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the function
(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,y)
is analytic in
\Omega({\Gamma})
, the functions
(x,y)\to Q(x,y)=xy(1-P(x,y)
,
(x,y)\to\psi_{1}(x,y)=x(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)
and
(x,y)\to\psi_{2}(x,y)=y(1-\phi_{2}(x,y))
can be analytically continued to the set
\Omega({\Gamma})
, and by Corollary
6.2
, the functions
h_{1j}
and
h_{2j}
are analytic respectively in the discs
B(0,x_{d})
and
B(0,y_{d})
. Hence for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the function

(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,y)+\psi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)+\psi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y)
is analytic in the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\penalty\ |x|<x_{d},\;|y|<y_{d}\}
. Since by Proposition
6.3
, on the set
\Omega(\Theta)
,

Q(x,y)h_{j}(x,y)=L_{j}(x,y)+\psi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)+\psi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y),

and since clearly
\Theta\subset\{(x,y)\in{\Gamma}:\penalty\ x<x_{d},y<y_{d}\}
, we conclude therefore that the functions
(x,y)\to Q(x,y)h_{j}(x,y)
can be analytically continued to the set

\Omega_{d}(\Gamma)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\penalty\ |x|<x_{d},\;|y|<y_{d}\}

and the function
(x,y)\to h_{j}(x,y)
can be continued as a meromorphic function

h_{j}(x,y)=\frac{L_{j}(x,y)+\psi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)+\psi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y)}{Q(x,y)}.

to the set
\Omega_{d}(\Gamma)
. Since

–

by Proposition
6.1
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the function
h_{j}
is analytic in
\Omega(\Theta)
;

–

for any
(x,y)\in\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D})
, by the definition of the set
D
, we have
|P(x,y)|\leqslant P(|x|,|y|)<1
and, consequently,
Q(x,y)\not=0
;

–

and by Proposition
6.4
, the set
\Omega_{d}(\Gamma)
is included to the union of the open sets
\Omega(\Theta)
and
\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D})
,

we conclude therefore that the function
h_{j}
can be analytically continued to the set
\Omega_{d}(\Gamma)
.

7.
Singularity Analysis of Generating Functions

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2
. Recall that the case (B7) will not be considered. See Section
2.2
. Throughout this section we will assume therefore that

Assumption (A4)
one of the cases (B0)-(B6) holds.

7.1.
The main ideas and the sketch of the proof

The main steps of our proof are the following. First, we prove the light version of the assertion (i)-(v) of this theorem, i.e. we get, in each of the corresponding cases, relations (
2.40
), (
2.44
) (
2.42
), (
2.46
), (
2.48
), (
2.50
), (
2.52
), and (
2.54
) with positive constants given by (
2.41
), (
2.43
), (
2.45
), (
2.47
), (
2.49
), (
2.51
), (
2.53
). This is a subject of Propositions
7.10
and
7.12
and Lemma
7.6
below.

With these results we will be able to show that in each of the assertions (i)-(vi) of our theorem, the corresponding function
\varkappa_{i}
,
\tilde{\varkappa}_{i}
or
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}
is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
.

Second, in Proposition
7.13
below, we prove that in each of the assertions (i)-(vi), the corresponding function
\varkappa_{i}
,
\tilde{\varkappa}_{i}
or
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}
is harmonic for the random walk
({Z}_{\tau_{0}}(n))
and positive everywhere in the set
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
. With this statement, the proof of our theorem will be completed.

The first step of the proof of the last assertion of Theorem
2
is given by Lemma
7.6
. This result is obtained as a traightforward consequence of Theorem
1
.

The first step of the proof of the assertions (i)-(v) of Theorem
2
proof is the most difficult. Its main idea is the following: we extend the function
Y_{1}
as an analytic function to some domain of
\mathbb{C}
large enough and we inject next
y=Y_{1}(x)
to the functional equation (
2.30
) in order to get the identity

(7.1)

L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))=0.

Using this result we will be able to extend beyond the point
x_{d}
first the function
x\to\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)=(\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)-1)H_{j}(x,0)
and next the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
.

Remark that in a difference with nearest neighbor random walks, we have no explicit form of the function
Y_{1}
. Moreover, by the implicit function theorem, this function can be extended only to some neighborhood of the interval
]x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}[
in
\mathbb{C}
which is clearly not sufficient for our purpose. The first difficulty of our proof is therefore to extend the function
Y_{1}
to some sufficient for our analysis domain of
\mathbb{C}
. We perform this first step of our proof by using the probabilistic representation of the function
Y_{1}:[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]\to[y^{*}_{P},y^{**}_{P}]
obtained in
[
13
]
. This is a subject of Proposition
7.1
below. In this statement, for some
{\varepsilon}>0
, we extend the function
Y_{1}
as an analytic function to the set

\overset{\circ}{U}_{{\varepsilon}}=C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}[,

and a continuous function on the set

(7.2)

U_{{\varepsilon}}\penalty\ =\penalty\ C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}]x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}[\penalty\ =\penalty\ \overset{\circ}{U}_{{\varepsilon}}\cup\{x^{**}_{P}\}.

It is proved moreover that the extended function
Y_{1}
satisfies the identity

(7.3)

Q(x,Y_{1}(x))=0,

on the set
U_{\varepsilon}
, and that, on the closed annulus
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
, the following relations holds

(7.4)

|Y_{1}(x)|<Y_{1}(|x|)\quad\text{whenever}\quad x\not=|x|.

Another difficulty is that by Theorem
1
, we know only that the function
y\to h_{2j}(y)
is analytic in
B(0,y_{d})
, and the function
(x,y)\to h_{j}(x,y)
is analytic in
\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\penalty\ |x|<x_{d},\;|y|<y_{d}\}
. Hence, we can inject
y=Y_{1}(x)
to the functional equation (
2.30
) only for those
x\in B(0,x_{d})
for which
|Y_{1}(x)|<y_{d}
. To overcome this difficulty, we use the inequality (
7.4
).
With this inequality, we are able to show that for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the annulus
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d})=\{x\in\mathbb{C}:x_{d}-\delta<|x|<x_{d}\}
to the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\penalty\ |x|<x_{D},\,|y|<y_{d}\}
where the functions
(x,y)\to H_{j}(x,y)
,
(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,y)+\psi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y)
and
(x,y)\to\psi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)
are analytic. This is a subject of Proposition
7.4
below.

In this way, on the annulus
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d})
, by letting in the functional equation (
2.30
)
y=Y_{1}(x)
and using (
7.3
) we obtain the identity (
7.1
) with analytic in
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d})
functions
x\to\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)
and
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))
and
x\to\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
. Moreover, when one of the cases (B0)-(B4) holds, using again the inequality (
7.4
), we will be able to show that for some
\delta>0
, the function
(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
and continuous on
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
. Using this result together with the identity (
7.1
), we will extend the function
x\to-\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))H_{j}(x,0)
as an analytic function to the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
and a continuous function to the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
. This is a subject of Corollary
7.6
below.

Next, in Proposition
7.7
, we investigate the function
x\to 1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))
, and finally, in Proposition
7.10
and Proposition
7.11
below, when one of the cases (B0)-(B4) holds, we extend the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
beyond the point
x_{d}
.

In the case when either (B5) or (B6) holds, we begin our analysis by investigating the function
h_{2j}
. With the same arguments as in the previous cases (it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
) we obtain that the function
h_{2j}
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,y_{d}+\delta_{0}){\setminus}\{y_{d}\}
. Next we show that for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap U_{\varepsilon}{\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
. And finally, using again the identity (
7.1
) we extend first the function
x\to\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)
and next the function
x\to h_{1j}(x)
beyond the point
x_{d}
. This is a subject of Proposition
7.12
below.

7.2.
Analytic continuation and properties of the function
x\to Y_{1}(x)

We begin our analysis with the following result.

Proposition 7.1
.

Analytic continuation of the function
Y_{1}
. Suppose the conditions (A1) are satisfied and let
\mu(j)=0
for all
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
j_{2}<-1
(remark that we do not need the whole condition (A2) to be satisfied). Then

i)

the function
Y_{1}
is strictly convex on the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and for some
{\varepsilon}>0
, it can be extended to the set
U_{\varepsilon}
as an analytic function in the set
\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
and a continuous function on the set
U_{\varepsilon}
satisfying there the identity (
7.3
).

ii)

relation (
7.4
) holds on the set
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
;

iii)

for any
\hat{x}\in]X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}[
, the function
x\to(Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(\hat{x})
does not vanish in the set
C(\hat{x}-\delta,\hat{x}+\delta){\setminus}\{\hat{x}\}
for some
\delta>0
, and has at the point
\hat{x}
a simple zero with

(7.5)

\frac{d}{dx}Y_{1}(\hat{x})=-\left.\partial_{x}P(x,y)/\partial_{y}P(x,y)\right|_{(x,y)=(\hat{x},Y_{1}(\hat{x}))}>0;

iv)

the function
x\to Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})
does not vanish in the set
C(x^{**}_{P}-\delta,x^{**}_{P}+\delta){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+\delta[
for some
\delta>0
, and as
x\to x^{**}_{P}
,

(7.6)

Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})\sim-c\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}\quad\;\text{and }\;\quad\frac{d}{dx}Y_{1}(x)\sim\frac{c}{2\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}}

with

c=\left.\sqrt{\partial_{x}P(x,y)/\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)}\,\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}>0.

7.2.1.
The main ideas and the sketch of the proof of Proposition
7.1

To prove this result, we first get a probabilistic representation of the function
Y_{1}
. As a consequence, we obtain that the function
Y_{1}
is strictly convex on the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and can be continued as an analytic function to the open annulus
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
and as a continuous function to the closed annulus
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
satisfying there the inequality (
7.4
) and the identity (
7.3
). This is a subject of the results of Subsection
7.2.2
.

Next, in Subsection
7.2.3
, we extend the functions
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
to a neighborhood of
x^{**}_{P}
as two branches of a two-valued analytic function having a branching point
x^{**}_{P}
and we get (
7.6
). This result is obtained by using the implicit function theorem and the Morse lemma.

Finally, in Subsection
7.2.4
, the proof of Proposition
7.1
is completed: by using the implicit function theorem and relations (
7.4
) and (
7.3
) on the circle
\{x\in\mathbb{C}:|x|=x^{**}_{P}\}
, we extend the function
Y_{1}
and the identity (
7.3
) to the whole set
U_{\varepsilon}=C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}]x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}[
for some
{\varepsilon}>0
, and we prove the two last assertions of Proposition
7.1
.

7.2.2.
Probabilistic representation of the functions
Y_{1}
its consequences

Consider the homogeneous random walk
(S(n)=(S_{1}(n),S_{2}(n))
on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with transition probabilities

{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(S(1)=k)=\mu(k-j),\quad\forall k,j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},

and the first time
\tau_{1}
when the random walk
(S(n))
hits the set
\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}
:

\tau_{1}=\inf\{n\geqslant 1\penalty\ :\penalty\ S(n)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}\},

By Lemma 2.2 of
[
13
]

Lemma 7.1
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
7.1
, for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
and
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}\left(x^{S_{1}(\tau_{1})};\;\tau_{1}<+\infty\right)=x^{j_{1}}Y_{1}(x)^{j_{2}}.

As a straightforward consequence of this statement one gets the following probabilistic representation of the function
Y_{1}
:

Corollary 7.2
.

Under the hypotheses (A1) and (A2), for any
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
,

(7.7)

Y_{1}(x)={\mathbb{E}}_{(0,1)}\left(x^{S_{1}(\tau_{1})};\;\tau_{1}<+\infty\right)

With this result we get

Lemma 7.2
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
7.1
, the function
Y_{1}
is strictly convex on
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and can be extended to the set
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
as a function

(7.8)

Y_{1}(x)=\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{P}}_{(0,1)}(S(\tau_{1})=(k_{1},0),\;\tau_{1}<+\infty)x^{k_{1}}

which is continuous on
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
, analytic in
{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
and, on the set
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
, satisfies the inequality (
7.4
) and the identity (
7.3
).

Proof.

Indeed, by Corollary
7.2
, the series

(7.9)

\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}{\mathbb{P}}_{0,1)}(S(\tau_{1})=(k_{1},0),\;\tau_{1}<+\infty)x^{k_{1}}={\mathbb{E}}_{(0,1)}\left(x^{S_{1}(\tau_{1})};\;\tau_{1}<+\infty\right)

converges and the identity (
7.8
) holds for any real
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
. Since the coefficients of the series (
7.9
) are real and non-negative, it follows that this series converges also on
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
. Hence, by letting (
7.8
) for
x\in\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
, the function
Y_{1}
can be extended to the set
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
as a function which is continuous on
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
and analytic in
{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
.

To get (
7.4
) and to show that the function
Y_{1}
is strictly convex on
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, we show that all coefficients of the series (
7.9
) are strictly positive. For this we recall that under our hypotheses, the random walk
(S(n))
is irreducible on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and hence, for any
k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
, there are
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a sequence of points
\ell_{0},\ldots,\ell_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
with
\ell_{0}=(0,1)
and
\sum_{i=0}^{n}\ell_{i}=(k_{1},0)
such that

\mu(\ell_{i})>0\quad\text{for any $i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$}.

Moreover, without any restriction of generality, one can assume that for some
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, the second coordinate of each of the points
\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{j}
is either zero or positive, and the second coordinate of each of the points
\ell_{j+1},\ldots,\ell_{n}
strictly negative. Then for any
j\in\{0,\ldots,n-1\}
, the second coordinate of the point
\ell_{0}+\cdots+\ell_{n}

is strictly positive and consequently,

{\mathbb{P}}_{0,1}(S(\tau_{1})=(k_{1},0),\;\tau_{1}<+\infty)\geqslant\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mu(\ell_{i})>0.

All coefficients of f the series (
7.9
) are therefore strictly positive.

Since for any
k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
, the function
x\to{\mathbb{P}}_{(2,0)}(S(\tau_{1})=(k_{1},0),\;\tau_{1}<+\infty)x^{k_{1}}
is convex on
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and the function
x\to{\mathbb{P}}_{(2,0)}(S(\tau_{1})=(2,0),\;\tau_{1}<+\infty)x^{2}
is strictly convex on
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, this proves that the function
Y_{1}
is strictly convex on
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
. And using moreover Proposition P7.5 of
[
24
]
we get (
7.4
).

Remark finally that by relation (
7.4
), the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the closed annulus
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
to the set

\overline{\Gamma}_{1}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ x\in\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}),\;|y|\leqslant Y_{1}(|x|)\}.

Since under the hypotheses of Proposition
7.1
, the function

Q(x,y)=xy-\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in{\mathbb{N}}^{2}}\mu(k_{1}-1,k_{2}-1)x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}

is analytic in a neighborhood of the set
\overline{\Gamma}_{1}
, and the function
Y_{1}
is already extended as an analytic function to the open annulus
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
and as a continuous function to the closed annulus
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
, it follows that the function
x\to Q(x,Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
and continuous on
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
. Since moreover the identity (
7.3
) holds for any real
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
, by the uniqueness of the analytic continuation to the set
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
and by continuity of the function
x\to Q(x,Y_{1}(x))
on
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
, this proves that the identity (
7.3
) holds also on the whole set
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
.
∎

7.2.3.
Analytic continuation of the functions
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
to a neighborhood of the branching point
x^{**}_{P}

Now, we extend the functions
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
to a neighborhood of
x^{**}_{P}
as two branches of a two-valued analytic function having a branching point
x^{**}_{P}
and we get (
7.6
).

Lemma 7.3
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
7.1
, for some
{\varepsilon}>0
small enough, the functions
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
can be continued to the disk
B(x^{**}_{P},{\varepsilon})
as two branches of two-valued analytic in
B(x^{**}_{P},{\varepsilon}){\setminus}\{x^{**}_{P}\}
function such that for any
x\in B(x^{**}_{P},{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}]
,

(7.10)

P(x,Y_{1}(x))=P(x,Y_{2}(x))=1,

and there is an analytic in
B(0,\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
function function
F_{Y}
such that

(7.11)

Y_{1}(x)=F_{Y}(-\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}),\quad\quad\quad Y_{2}(x)=F_{Y}(\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x})

and

(7.12)

\left.\frac{d}{du}F_{Y}(u)\right|_{u=0}=\left.\sqrt{\frac{\partial_{x}P(x,y)}{\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)}}\,\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}>0.

Proof.

By the definition of the point
x^{**}_{P}
and the functions
Y_{1}
,
Y_{2}
, we have

Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=Y_{2}(x^{**}_{P}),\quad\quad P(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1,

and by Lemma
4.1
,

(7.13)

\left.\partial_{y}P(x,y)\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}=0,\quad\text{and}\quad\left.\partial_{x}P(x,y)\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}>0.

Hence, by the implicit function theorem, there are a neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of the point
x^{**}_{P}
and an analytic in a neighborhood
U
of
Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})
function
y\to\psi(y)

such that for any
(x,y)\in U\times V
,

P(x,y)=1\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad x=\psi(y),

\psi(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=x^{**}_{P},\quad\quad\quad\left.\frac{d}{dy}\psi(y)\right|_{y=Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})}=0,

and

\left.\frac{d^{2}}{dy^{2}}\psi(y)\right|_{y=Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})}=-\left.\frac{\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)}{\partial_{x}P(x,y)}\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}<0,

where the last relation follows from the second relation of (
7.13
) because under the hypotheses (A1), the real valued function
y\to P(x^{**}_{P},y)
is strictly convex in a neighborhood of
Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})
. Using the Morse lemma we conclude therefore that for some neighborhood
\tilde{U}\subset U
of
Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})
, there is a
\mathbb{C}
-diffeomorphism
\omega
from
\tilde{U}
to a neighborhood
\tilde{V}
of
0
, with

(7.14)

\omega(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=0\quad\text{and}\quad\left.\frac{d}{dy}\omega(y)\right|_{y=Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})}=\left.\sqrt{\frac{\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}P(x,y)}{\partial_{x}P(x,y)}}\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}

such that
\tilde{V}+x^{**}_{P}\subset V
and for any
(x,y)\in(\tilde{V}+x^{**}_{P})\times\tilde{U}
,

P(x,y)=1\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad x=x^{**}_{P}-\omega^{2}(y).

Without any restriction of generality, one can assume that
\omega(\tilde{U})=\tilde{V}=B(0,\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
with
{\varepsilon}>0
small enough. Then for
x\in B(x^{**}_{P},{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}]
, and
y\in\tilde{U}
, from the above relation it follows that

\displaystyle P(x,y)=1

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\bigl(\omega(y)=\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}\quad\text{or}\quad\omega(y)=-\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}\bigr)

\displaystyle\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\bigl(y=\omega^{-1}(\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x})\quad\text{or}\quad y=\omega^{-1}(-\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x})\bigr)

with the square root function
\sqrt{\;}
analytic in
\mathbb{C}{\setminus}]-\infty,0]
. Since the inverse to
\omega
function
\omega^{-1}
is analytic in
B(0,\sqrt{{\varepsilon}})
, and since for real
x\in[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and
y>0
,

P(x,y)=1\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad\bigl(y=Y_{1}(x)\quad\text{or}\quad y=Y_{2}(x)\bigr)

we conclude therefore that the functions
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
can be extended to the set
B(x^{**}_{P},{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}]
as analytic functions such that either

Y_{1}(x)=\omega^{-1}(\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x})\;\text{ and }\;Y_{2}(x)=\omega^{-1}(-\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}),\quad\forall x\in B(x^{**}_{P},{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}],

or

Y_{1}(x)=\omega^{-1}(-\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x})\;\text{and}\;Y_{2}(x)=\omega^{-1}(\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}),\quad\quad\forall x\in B(x^{**}_{P},{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}].

These relations show that the function
\omega^{-1}
is real valued on the interval
]-\sqrt{{\varepsilon}},\sqrt{{\varepsilon}}[
, and hence, by the second relation of (
7.14
), it is strictly increasing in a neighborhood of
0
. Since the function
Y_{1}
is increasing on the interval
[X_{1}(y^{*}),x^{**}_{P}]
and the function
Y_{2}
is decreasing on the interval
[X_{1}(y^{*}),x^{**}_{P}]
, it follows that (
7.10
) and (
7.11
) hold with
F_{Y}=\omega^{-1}
. Relation (
7.12
) follows from (
7.14
).
∎

7.2.4.
Analytic continuation of the function
Y_{1}
to the set
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}]

Now we extend the function
Y_{1}
and the identity (
7.3
) to the set
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}]
for some small
{\varepsilon}>0
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Under the hypotheses of Proposition
7.1
, for some
{\varepsilon}>0
small enough, the function
x\to Y_{1}(x)
can be analytically continued to the set
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}[
, and satisfies there the identity (
7.3
).

Proof.

By Lemma
7.2
, the function
Y_{1}
is already extended to the closed annulus
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
as an analytic function in
{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
and a continuous function on
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
satisfying there the identity (
7.3
), and that
by Lemma
7.3
, for some
{\varepsilon}>0
, the function
Y_{1}
is also already analytically continued to the set
B(x^{**}_{P},{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}+{\varepsilon}]
. Hence, to prove this lemma, it is sufficient to show that the function
Y_{1}
can be analytically continued to a neighborhood of the set
\{x\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ |x|=x^{**}_{P},\;|x|\not=x^{**}_{P}\}
. In order to get this result, we use the implicit function theorem.

By Lemma
7.2
,
Q(\tilde{x},Y_{1}(\tilde{x}))=0
for any point
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{C}
with
|x|=x^{**}_{P}
and remark that under the hypotheses of Proposition
7.1
, the function
Q
is analytic in a neighborhood of
(\tilde{x},Y_{1}(\tilde{x}))
. Hence, by the implicit function theorem, it is sufficient to show that for any such a point
\tilde{x}
,

(7.15)

\partial_{y}Q(\tilde{x},Y_{1}(\tilde{x}))\not=0\quad\text{whenever}\quad\tilde{x}\not=x^{**}_{P}.

To get this relation, we let us remark that according to the definition (
2.24
) of the function
Q
and using (
7.4
), for any point
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\tilde{x}|=x^{**}_{P}
and such that
\tilde{x}\not=x^{**}_{P}
, one has

(7.16)

\left|\tilde{x}-\partial_{y}Q(\tilde{x},Y_{1}(\tilde{x}))\right|\leqslant\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}(k_{2}+1)\mu(k_{1},k_{2})(x^{**}_{P})^{k_{1}+1}|Y_{1}(\tilde{x})|^{k_{2}}\\
\\
<\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{k_{2}=0}^{\infty}(k_{2}+1)\mu(k_{1},k_{2})(x^{**}_{P})^{k_{1}+1}Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})^{k_{2}}=x^{**}_{P}-\partial_{y}Q(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))

where

\partial_{y}Q(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=x^{**}_{P}-x^{**}_{P}P(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))-x^{**}_{P}Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})\partial_{y}Px^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=0

because
P(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
and by Lemma
4.1
,
\partial_{y}P(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=0
.
Hence, for any
\tilde{x}\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\tilde{x}|=x^{**}_{P}
and such that
\tilde{x}\not=x^{**}_{P}
,

\left|\partial_{y}Q(\tilde{x},Y_{1}(\tilde{x}))\right|\geqslant|\tilde{x}|-\left|\tilde{x}-\partial_{y}Q(\tilde{x},Y_{1}(\tilde{x}))\right|=x^{**}_{P}-\left|\tilde{x}-\partial_{y}Q(\tilde{x},Y_{1}(\tilde{x}))\right|>0

and consequently, (
7.15
) holds.
∎

7.2.5.
Proof of Proposition
7.1

The first two assertions of Proposition
7.1
are proved by Lemma
7.2
, Lemma
7.3
and Lemma
7.4
.

To prove the third assertion of Proposition
7.1
, recall that by Lemma
7.2
, the function
Y_{1}
is analytic in the annulus
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
and satisfies there the inequality (
7.4
) and remark that any point
\hat{x}\in]X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}[
belongs to the annulus

C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
because
x^{*}_{P}<X_{1}(y^{*}_{P})<x^{**}_{P}
. Hence, for any
\hat{x}\in]X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}[
, the function
x\to(Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(\hat{x})
is analytic in a neighborhood of the circle
\{x\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ |x|=\hat{x}
, and by (
7.4
), the point
\hat{x}
is an only zero of this function in the circle
\{x\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ |x|=\hat{x}
. Since the zeros of analytic functions are always isolated, this proves that for any
\hat{x}\in]X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}[
, there is
\delta>0
for which the function
x\to(Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(\hat{x})
does not vanish in the set
C(\hat{x}-\delta,\hat{x}+\delta){\setminus}\{\hat{x}\}
.

Moreover, we have

–

by Lemma
4.1
, the real valued function
Y_{1}
is strictly increasing on the interval
]X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}[
,

–

by Lemma
7.2
, it is also strictly convex on
]X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}[
,

–

and by Lemma
4.1
,
P(\hat{x},Y_{1}(\hat{x}))=1
and
\partial_{y}P(\hat{x},Y_{1}(\hat{x}))<0
.

Hence, for any
\hat{x}\in]X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}[
, using the implicit function theorem, one gets (
7.5
). From this relation, it follows moreover that for any
\hat{x}\in]X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}[
,
the point the point
\hat{x}
is a simple zero of the function
x\to Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(\hat{x})
. The third assertion of Proposition
7.1
is therefore also proved.

To get the last assertion of Proposition
7.1
, recall that by Lemma
7.3
and Lemma
7.4
, the function
Y_{1}
was already extended to the set
U_{\varepsilon}=C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}]x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}[
as an analytic function in
\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}=C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+{\varepsilon}[
and a continuous function on
U_{\varepsilon}
satisfying there the inequality (
7.4
). The function
x\to Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})
is therefore analytic in
\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
and continuous on
U_{\varepsilon}
, and moreover by (
7.4
), the point
x^{**}_{P}
is its only zero in the circle
\{x\in\mathbb{C}:|x|=x^{**}_{P}\}
. To complete the proof of the fourth assertion of Proposition
7.1
, it is therefore sufficient to get (
7.6
) and to show that the point
x^{**}_{P}
is an isolated zero of the function
x\to Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})
. For this, we remark that by Lemma
7.3
, as
x\to x^{**}_{P}
,

(7.17)

Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})=F_{Y}(-\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x})-F_{Y}(0)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ -c\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}

and

\frac{d}{dx}Y_{1}(x)\sim\frac{c}{2\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}}

with

c=\left.\frac{d}{du}F_{Y}(0)\right|_{u=0}=\left.\sqrt{\partial_{x}P(x,y)/\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)}\,\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}>0.

Relations (
7.6
) are therefore proved, and since the constant
c
is strictly positive, from (
7.17
) it follows that the point
x^{**}_{P}
is an isolated zero of the function
x\to Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})
.

7.3.
The properties of the mapping
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
and their consequences

Remark that by (
7.4
), the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the closed annulus to the set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ \;x\in C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}),\;|y|\leqslant Y_{1}(|x|)\}
, and under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3), for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the functions
(x,y)\to\phi_{1}(x,y)
,
(x,y)\to\psi_{2}(x,y)
and
(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,y)
are analytic in a neighborhood of this set. Hence, as a straightforward consequence of Proposition
7.1
one gets

Corollary 7.3
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3), for some
\delta>0
, the functions
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
,
x\to\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))
and
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))
for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, are analytic in the set
\overset{\circ}{U}_{\delta}
and continuous on the set
U_{\delta}
.

Another consequence of Proposition
7.1
is the following property of the mapping
x{\to}(x,Y_{1}(x))
.

Proposition 7.4
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A4), for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the annulus
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d})
to the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\,|x|<x_{d},\,|y|<y_{d}\}
. Moreover, for any neighborhood
V
of the set
\Omega(\overline{\Gamma})
in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
and
\hat{y}>Y_{1}(x_{d})
, there is
\delta>0
, for which the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap U_{\varepsilon}
to the set
\{(x,y)\in V:\,|y|<\hat{y}\}
.

Proof.

Remark that by (
7.4
), for any
x\in]x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}[
,

|Y_{1}(x)|\leqslant Y_{1}(|x|)<Y_{2}(|x|).

Hence, the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the set
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
to the set

{\Gamma}_{1}=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ \;x\in C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}),\;|y|<Y_{2}(|x|)\}.

For any
x\in]x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}[
,
Y_{1}(x)
and
Y_{2}(x)
are the only real and positive solution of the equation
P(x,y)=1
,
Y_{1}(x)<Y_{2}(x)
and for any
Y_{1}(x)<y<Y_{2}(x)
, the point
(x,y)
belongs to the interior of the set
D=\{(x,y)\in[0,+\infty[^{2}:P(x,y)\leqslant 1\}
(see Lemma
4.1
for more details). Hence for any
(x,y)\in{\Gamma}_{1})
, there is a point
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\in D
such that
|x|<x^{\prime}
and
|x|<y^{\prime}
and consequently, the set
{\Gamma}_{1}
is included to the set
{\Gamma}
. The mapping
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps therefore the set
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
to the set
{\Gamma}
.

Consider now the case when one of the cases (B0)-(B4) holds. In this case, (see Proposition
2.2
and relations (
2.33
), (
2.34
)), we have
Y_{1}(x_{d})<y_{d}
. Since the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the annulus
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
to the set
{\Gamma}
and
by Proposition
7.1
, the function
Y_{1}
is continuous on
U_{\varepsilon}
, it follows that for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the annulus
C(x_{d}-\delta,x)
to the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\,|x|<x_{d},\,|y|<y_{d}\}
.

When one of the cases (B0)-(B4) holds, the first assertion of Proposition
7.4
is therefore proved.

Consider now the case when (B5) or (B6) holds. In this case (see Proposition
2.2
and relations (
2.33
), (
2.34
)), the point
(x_{d},y_{d})=(X_{2}(y^{**}),y^{**})
belongs to the curve
{\mathcal{S}}_{21}=\{(x,Y_{1}(x)):\penalty\ x\in[X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]\}=\{(X_{2}(y),y):\penalty\ y\in[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})]\}
and
y^{*}_{P}\leqslant y^{*}<y_{d}=y^{**}<y^{**}_{P}
. Since by lemma
4.1
, the functions
X_{2}:[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})]\to[X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]
and
Y_{1}:[X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]\to[y^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})]
are strictly increasing and inverse to each other, it follows that in this case, we have
x^{*}_{P}<X_{2}(y_{d})=x_{d}
. Hence, for any
x\in C(x^{*}_{P},x_{d})
, using (
7.4
) one gets

|Y_{1}(x)|\leqslant Y_{1}(|x|)<Y_{1}(x_{d}).

Since we have already proved that the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the annulus
C(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
to the set
{\Gamma}
, the last relations prove that for
\delta=x_{d}-x^{*}_{P}>0
, this function maps the annulus
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d})
to the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega({\Gamma}):\,|x|<x_{d},\,|y|<y_{d}\}
.

In the case when one of the cases (B5) or (B6) holds, the first assertion of Proposition
7.4
is therefore also proved.

The second assertion of Proposition
7.4
holds because the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
is continuous in the set
U_{\varepsilon}
including the closed annulus
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
and maps
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
to the closure of the set
{\Gamma}
.

∎

Remark that under the hypotheses (A1)(ii), (A2) and (A3) (ii), (iv), the functions
(x,y)\to Q(x,y)=xy(1-P(x,y)
,
(x,y)\to\psi_{1}(x,y)=x(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)
and
(x,y)\to\psi_{2}(x,y)=y(1-\phi_{2}(x,y))
can be continued as analytic functions to some neighborhood of the set

\Omega(\overline{\Gamma})
. Hence, from Theorem
1
it follows

Corollary 7.5
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A4), there is a neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{2}
of the set
\Omega(\overline{\Gamma})
such that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
,

–

the function
(x,y)\to\psi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)
is analytic in the set
\{(x,y)\in{\mathcal{V}}:\penalty\ |x|<x_{d}\}
;

–

the function
(x,y)\to\psi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y)
is analytic in the set
\{(x,y)\in{\mathcal{V}}:\penalty\ |y|<y_{d}\}

–

the function
(x,y)\to R_{j}(x,y)=Q(x,y)h_{j}(x,y)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
\{(x,y)\in{\mathcal{V}}:\penalty\ |x|<x_{d},\;|y|<y_{d}\}
by letting

(7.18)

R_{j}(x,y)=L_{j}(x,y)+\psi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)+\psi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y).

When combined together, Proposition
7.4
, Corollary
7.3
and Corollary
7.5
imply the following statement.

Corollary 7.6
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A4) for some
\delta>0
, and any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

i) the functions
x\to-\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)
and
x\to\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
are analytic in the annulus
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d})
and satisfy there the identity

(7.19)

(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)=L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x));

ii) moreover, if one of the cases (B0)-(B4) holds with
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
(i.e. if either one of the cases (B0), (B1), (B3) or (B4) holds or (B2) and
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
hold), then for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to L_{J}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in the annulus
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)
and by (
7.19
), the function
x\to\eta_{j}(x)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)
.

iii) if (B2) holds with
x_{d}=x^{**}_{P}
, then for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
and continuous on the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
, and by (
7.19
), the function
x\to\eta_{j}(x)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended to the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
as an analytic function in
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
and a continuous function on
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
.

With this result, when one of the cases (B0)-(B4) holds, the function
x\to(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)
is therefore already extended beyond the point
x_{d}
. To extend the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
beyond the point
x_{d}
we need to investigate the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
. This is a subject of the next section.

7.4.
Analytic continuation and properties of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))

By Corollary
7.3
, for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is already extended to the set
U_{\delta}
as an analytic function in the set
\overset{\circ}{U}_{\delta}
and a continuous function on the set
U_{\delta}
. We will need moreover the following properties of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
.

Proposition 7.7
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)- (A3), the following assertions hold:

i) the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is strictly convex on the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
.

ii) for any
x\in C(x^{*},x^{**})
,

(7.20)

|\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))|\leqslant\phi(|x|,Y_{1}(|x|))<1;

iii) if
x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
, then for some
\hat{\delta}>0
, the point
x^{**}
is an only and simple zero of the function
x\to 1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
in the annulus
C(x^{*},x^{**}+\hat{\delta})
and

(7.21)

\left.\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\right|_{x=x^{**}}>0;

iv) if
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
, then
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))\leqslant 1
;

v) if
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
, then for some
\hat{\delta}>0
, the function
x\to 1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
has no zeros in
U_{\hat{\delta}}=C(x^{*},x^{**}_{P}+\hat{\delta}){\setminus}]x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+\hat{\delta}[
;

vi) if
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
, then for some
\hat{\delta}>0
, the point
x^{**}_{P}
is an only zero of the function
x\to 1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
in
U_{\hat{\delta}}
and as
x\to x^{**}_{P}
,

(7.22)

1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\sim c\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}\quad\;\text{with}\;\quad c=\left.\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x,y)\sqrt{\frac{\partial_{x}P(x,y)}{\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)}}\,\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}>0.

7.4.1.
Outline of the proof of Proposition
7.7

To prove this proposition, we first get a probabilistic representation of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
similar to those of the function
x\to Y_{1}(x)
. This is a subject of Corollary
7.8
. In Section
7.4.3
, as a straightforward consequence of this result, we get the first and the second assertion of Proposition
7.7
. The proof of the third assertion of this statement is given in Section
7.4.4
and the proofs of the last tree assertions are completed in Section
7.4.5
.

7.4.2.
Probabilistic representation of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))

The following probabilistic representation of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is another useful for our purpose consequence of Lemma
7.1
:

Corollary 7.8
.

Let
(\hat{S}(n))
be a random walk on the half-plane
\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}
with transition probabilities

(7.23)

{\mathbb{P}}_{k}(\hat{S}(1)=j)\penalty\ =\penalty\ \begin{cases}\mu(j-k)&\text{for all $j,k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}$ with $k_{2}>0$}\\
\mu_{1}(j-k)&\text{for all $j,k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}$ with $k_{2}=0$}\\
\end{cases}

and let
T_{1}
be the first time when the random walk
\bigl(\hat{S}(n)=(\hat{S}_{1}(n),\hat{S}_{2}(n))\bigr)
hits the boundary
\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}
:

T_{1}=\inf\{n>0:\penalty\ \hat{S}(n)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}\}.

Then under the hypotheses (A1) - (A3), for any
x\in\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})

(7.24)

\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))={\mathbb{E}}_{(0,0)}\left(x^{\hat{S}_{1}(T_{1})};\;T_{1}<+\infty\right).

Proof.

To get (
7.24
) from Lemma
7.1
it is sufficient to notice that by the Markov property, and according to the definition of the random walks
(S(n))
and
(\hat{S}(n))
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{(0,0)}\left(x^{\hat{S}_{1}(\tau_{1})};\;\tau_{1}<+\infty\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}}\mu_{1}(k)x^{k_{1}}+\hskip-8.53581pt\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}:\penalty\ k_{2}\not=0}\mu_{1}(k){\mathbb{E}}_{k}(x^{S_{1}(T_{1})},\;T_{1}<+\infty)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}\times\{0\}}\mu_{1}(k)x^{k_{1}}+\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}:\penalty\ k_{2}\not=0}\mu_{1}(k)x^{j_{1}}Y_{1}(x)^{j_{2}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)).

∎

7.4.3.
Proof of the first and the second assertions of Proposition
7.7

With Corollary
7.8
, by using the same arguments as in the proof of Lemma
7.2
we get

Corollary 7.9
.

Under the hypotheses (A1) - (A3), the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is strictly convex on
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and satisfies on the set
\overline{C}(x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P})
the following relation

(7.25)

|\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))|<\phi_{1}(|x|,Y_{1}(|x|)),\quad\forall x\not=|x|.

Remark that this statement proves the first assertion of Proposition
7.7
. Moreover, since by Corollary
4.1
,

(7.26)

\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))<1\quad\text{for any}\quad x\in]x^{*},x^{**}[,

using (
7.25
) one gets (
7.20
), and consequently, the second assertion of Proposition
7.7
also holds.

7.4.4.
Proof of the third assertion of Proposition
7.7

Suppose now that
x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
. In this case, by Corollary
4.1
,

(7.27)

\phi_{1}(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**}))=1\quad\text{and }\quad\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))>1\quad\text{for all}\quad x\in]x^{**},x^{**}_{P}].

Using this relation together with (
7.20
) one get that the point
x^{**}
is an only zero of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1
in the set
\{x\in\mathbb{C}\penalty\ :\penalty\ x^{*}<|x|\leqslant x^{**}\}
, and since the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1
is analytic in the neighborhood
\overset{\circ}{U}_{\delta}
of this set, it follows that for some
\hat{\delta}>0
, the point
x^{**}
is an only zero of this function in the annulus
C(x^{*},x^{**}+\hat{\delta})
. Moreover, since the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is strictly convex on the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}]
and by (
2.9
), in the case when
x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
, the point
x^{**}
belongs to the interior of this line segment, from (
7.26
) and (
7.27
) it follows that

\left.\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\right|_{x=x^{**}}>0,

and consequently, the point
x^{**}
is in this case a simple zero of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1
. The third assertion of Proposition
7.7
is therefore also proved.

7.4.5.
Proof of the last three assertions of Proposition
7.7

Suppose now that
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
. Then by Corollary
4.1
,
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))\leqslant 1
, and consequently, the fourth assertion of Proposition
7.7
holds.

Moreover, if
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
, using (
7.25
) we get

|\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))]\leqslant\phi_{1}(|x|,Y_{1}(|x|))<1,\quad\text{for any $x\in\mathbb{C}$ with $x^{*}<|x|\leqslant x^{**}_{P}$},

and consequently, the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1
has no zeros in the set
\{x\in\mathbb{C}\penalty\ :\penalty\ x^{*}<|x|\leqslant x^{**}_{P}\}
. Since the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is continuous on the set
U_{\delta}
, it follows that, for some
\hat{\delta}>0
, it has no zeros in the set
U_{\hat{\delta}}
.

An finally, if
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
, using again (
7.25
) one gets that the point
x^{**}_{P}
is an only zero of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1
in the set
\{x\in\mathbb{C}\penalty\ :\penalty\ x^{*}<|x|\leqslant x^{**}_{P}\}
. Moreover, since under our hypotheses, the function
(x,y)\to\phi_{1}(x,y)
is analytic in a neighborhood of the point
(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))
and since because of Assumption (A3)(iv),

\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))>0,

using (
7.6
) one gets that as
x\to x^{**}_{P}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ (x-x^{**}_{P})\partial_{x}\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))-c\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))

\displaystyle\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ -c\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))

with

c=\left.\sqrt{\partial_{x}P(x,y)/\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)}\,\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}>0.

This proves that the point
x^{**}_{P}
is in this case an isolated zero of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1
in
U_{\delta}
. Since this function is continuous in a neighborhood
U_{\delta}
of the set
\{x\in\mathbb{C}\penalty\ :\penalty\ x^{*}<|x|\leqslant x^{**}_{P}\}
, and has no zeros in
\{x\in\mathbb{C}\penalty\ :\penalty\ x^{*}<|x|\leqslant x^{**}_{P}\}
aside of the point
x^{**}_{P}
, we conclude therefore that for some
\hat{\delta}>0
, the point
x^{**}_{P}
is an only zero of the function
x\to\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1
in
U_{\hat{\delta}}
. The last assertion of
Proposition
7.7
is therefore also proved.

7.5.
Analytic continuation of the function
x{\to}H_{j}(x,0)
, cases (B0)-(B4)

In this section, we extend the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
beyond the point
x_{d}
when one of the cases (B0)-(B4) holds. The cases (B0), (B1), (B3), (B4), and the case (B2) with
x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
are considered in Proposition
7.10
. The case (B2) with
x_{d}=x^{**}_{P}
is considered in Proposition
7.11
below.

Proposition 7.10
.

Suppose that the conditions (A1)-(A3) are satisfied and let either one of the cases (B0), (B1), (B3), (B4) holds or (B2) and
x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
hold. Then for some
\delta>0
and any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,x_{d}+\delta){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
, and (
2.40
) holds with
\varkappa_{1}(j)
defined by (
2.35
) and
a_{1}>0
defined by (
2.41
).

Proof.

Under the hypotheses of this proposition, by Corollary
7.6
, for some
\delta_{1}>0
, the function
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in the annulus

C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d}+\delta_{1})
, and using the identities

(7.28)

\displaystyle(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)

\displaystyle=-\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)

(7.29)

\displaystyle=L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))

the function
x\to(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)

can be analytically continued to the open annulus
C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d}+\delta_{1})
. Since by Proposition
7.7
, for some
\delta_{2}>0
, the function
x\to(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))^{-1}
is analytic in the set
C(x_{d}-\delta_{2},x_{d}+\delta_{2}){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
and has at the point
x_{d}
a simple pole with

\lim_{x\to x_{d}}\frac{x_{d}-x}{1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))}=\left.\left(\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\right)^{-1}\right|_{x=x_{d}}>0,

we conclude therefore that the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,x_{d}+\delta){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
, and that (
2.40
) holds with
\varkappa_{1}(j)
given by (
2.35
) , and
a_{1}>0
given by (
2.41
).
∎

Proposition 7.11
.

Suppose that the conditions (A1)-(A3) are satisfied and let (B2) and
x_{d}=x^{**}_{P}
hold. Then the following assertions holds.

–

If
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
, then for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,x^{**}_{P}+\delta){\setminus}[x^{**},x^{**}_{P}+\delta[
satisfying (
2.42
) with
\varkappa_{1}(j)
defined by (
2.35
) with
x_{d}=x^{**}_{P}
and
a_{2}>0
defined by (
2.43
).

–

If
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
, then for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended to the set
B(0,x^{**}_{P}+\delta){\setminus}]x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+\delta[
as an analytic function in
B(0,x^{**}_{P}+\delta){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+\delta[
and a continuous function on
B(0,x^{**}_{P}+\delta){\setminus}]x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+\delta[
, satisfying (
2.44
) with
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)
defined by (
2.36
)
and
\tilde{a}_{2}>0
defined by (
2.45
).

Proof.

Indeed, in this case, by Corollary
7.6
, for some
0<\delta_{1}\leqslant{\varepsilon}
, the function
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in
C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d}+\delta_{1})\cap\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
and continuous on
C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d}+\delta_{1})\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
, and using the identities (
7.28
) end (
7.29
), the function
x\to\eta_{j}(x)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended to the set
C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d}+\delta_{1})\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
as an analytic function in
C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d}+\delta_{1})\cap\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
and a continuous function on
C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d}+\delta_{1})\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
. Since by Proposition
7.7
, for some
0<\delta\leqslant\delta_{1}
, the function
x\to 1/(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))
is analytic in the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
, we get therefore that the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
by letting

\displaystyle H_{j}(x,0)

\displaystyle=\eta_{j}(x)\bigl(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\bigr)^{-1}

(7.30)

\displaystyle=\left(L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))\right)\bigl(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\bigr)^{-1}.

Recall moreover that by Proposition
7.7
, when
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
, the function
x\to 1/(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))
is continuous on
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
, and when
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
,

(7.31)

\lim_{x\to x^{**}_{P}}\frac{\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}}{1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))}=\left.\left(\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x,y)\sqrt{\partial_{x}P(x,y)/\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)}\right)^{-1}\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}>0.

Hence, in the case when
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as a continuous function to the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap{U}_{\varepsilon}
, and in the case when
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
, using (
7.30
) (
7.31
), one gets (
2.42
) with
\varkappa_{1}(j)
defined by (
2.35
), and
a_{2}>0
given by (
2.43
).

Remark finally that when
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
, using (
7.30
) , for
x\in B(0,x^{**}_{P})
closed enough to
x^{**}_{P}
, one gets

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dx}

\displaystyle H_{j}(x,0)=\frac{d}{dx}\left(r_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dx}\left((L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+(\phi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1)H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x)))(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\left.\left((L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(0,y))(1-\phi_{1}(x,y))^{-1}\right)\right|_{y=Y_{1}(x)}

\displaystyle\;+\partial_{y}\left.\left((L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+(\phi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1)H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x)))(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))^{-1}\right)\right|_{y=Y_{1}(x)}\times\frac{d}{dx}Y_{1}(x)

where by (
7.6
), as
x\to x^{**}_{P}
,

\frac{d}{dx}Y_{1}(x)\sim\frac{c}{2\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}}\quad\;\text{with}\;\quad c=\left.\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\partial_{x}P(x,y)/\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)}\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}>0.

Since in the case when
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
, the function

(x,y)\to(L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(0,y))(1-\phi_{1}(x,y))^{-1}

is analytic in a neighborhood of the point
(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))
, it follows (
2.44
) with
\tilde{a}_{2}>0
given by (
2.45
).
∎

7.6.
Analytic continuation of the function
x{\to}H_{j}(x,0)
, cases (B5) and (B6)

Now we are ready to extend the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
beyond the point
x_{d}
when either (B5) or (B6) holds. This is a subject of Proposition
7.12
below.

Proposition 7.12
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3), there is
\delta>0
such that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the following assertions hold

i) If (B6) holds, then the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,x_{d}+\delta){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
and (
2.52
) holds with
a_{5}>0
given by (
2.53
).

ii) If (B5) holds and
x_{d}=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
, then the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,x_{d}+\delta){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
and (
2.46
) holds with
a_{3}>0
given by (
2.47
).

iii) If (B5),
x_{d}=x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
hold, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,x_{d}+\delta){\setminus}[x_{d},x_{d}+\delta[
and (
2.48
) holds with
a_{4}>0
given by (
2.49
).

iv) If (B5),
x_{d}=x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
hold, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,x_{d}+\tilde{\delta}){\setminus}[x_{d},x_{d}+\tilde{\delta}[
and (
2.50
) holds with
\tilde{a}_{4}
given by (
2.51
).

Recall that under our hypotheses, all functions
(x,y){\to}L_{j}(x,y)
,
(x,y){\to}\psi_{1}(x,y)
and
(x,y){\to}\psi_{2}(x,y)
are analytic in some neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of the set
\Omega(\overline{\Gamma})
. Throughout this section, the set
{\mathcal{V}}
will be given.

7.6.1.
Preliminary result

We begin the proof of this proposition with the following lemma.

Lemma 7.5
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3) and if one of the cases (B5) or (B6) holds, there is
\delta>0
such that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the function
x\to(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x))
can be analytically continued to the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta)\cap U_{\varepsilon}\setminus\{x_{d}\}
.

Proof.

Suppose that either (B5) or (B6) holds. Then by Proposition
2.2
and using relations (
2.33
), (
2.34
)), one gets

(7.32)

X_{1}(y_{d})<x_{d}=X_{2}(y_{d})\leqslant x^{**},\quad y^{*}<y_{d}=y^{**}=Y_{1}(x_{d})<y^{**}_{P}\quad\text{and}\quad(x_{d},y_{d})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{21},

with
x_{d}=x^{**}
in the case
(B5)
, and
x_{d}<x^{**}
in the case (B6). Hence, with the same arguments as in the proof of proposition
7.10
, (it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
), one gets that there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the functions
h_{1j}
and
y\to yh_{2j}(y)=H_{j}(0,y)
and can be analytically continued to the set
B(0,y_{d}+\delta_{0}){\setminus}\{y_{d}\}
, and

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to y_{d}}(y_{d}-y)yh_{2j}(y)

\displaystyle=\lim_{y\to y_{d}}(y_{d}-y)H_{j}(0,y)

(7.33)

\displaystyle=c_{1}\Bigl(L_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})+(\phi_{1}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d}))-1)H_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),0)\Bigr)

with

(7.34)

c_{1}=\left.\left(\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\right)^{-1}\right|_{y=y_{d}}>0.

Since by (
7.32
),
y_{d}<y^{**}_{P}
, without any restriction of generality, we will suppose throughout our proof that

(7.35)

y_{d}+\delta_{0}<y^{**}_{P}.

Moreover, by using Proposition
7.1
and the first assertion of Corollary
7.6
we can inject
y=Y_{1}(x)
to the functional equation (
2.30
):

–

by Proposition
7.1
, for some
{\varepsilon}>0
, the function
Y_{1}
is already analytically continued to
\overset{\circ}{U}_{\varepsilon}
and extended as a continuous function to the set
U_{\varepsilon}
,

–

by Corollary
7.6
, there is
\delta_{1}{\in}]0,{\varepsilon}[
, such that any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,
the functions
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
and
x\to-\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)
are analytic in the set
C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d})
and for any
x\in C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d})
the following relation holds

(7.36)

(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)=L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x)).

To complete the proof of Lemma
7.5
, the following steps will be performed:

step 1
:

First we will show that for some
\delta_{2}\in]0,\delta_{1}[
, the function
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in the set

(7.37)

\{x\in C(x_{d}-\delta_{2},x_{d}+\delta_{2})\cap U_{\varepsilon}:\penalty\ Y_{1}(x)\not=Y_{1}(x_{d})\}.

step 2
:

Next we will prove that for some
\delta_{3}\in]0,\delta_{2}[
, the point
x_{d}
is an only zero of the function
x\to Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(x_{d})
in
C(x_{d}-\delta_{3},x_{d}+\delta_{3})\cap U_{\varepsilon}
, and we will deduce from our previous result that the function
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in the set

(7.38)

C(x_{d}-\delta_{3},x_{d}+\delta_{3})\cap U_{\varepsilon}\setminus\{x_{d}\}.

With this result and using the identity (
7.36
) we will be able to complete our proof.

Step 1
: Since because of (
7.32
),
Y_{1}(x_{d})=y_{d}<y_{d}+\delta_{0}
, by Proposition
7.4
applied with
\hat{y}=y_{d}+\delta_{0}
, we obtain that for some
0<\delta_{2}<\delta_{1}
, the function
x\to(x,Y_{1}(x))
maps the set
C(x_{d}-\delta_{2},x_{d}+\delta_{2})\cap U_{\varepsilon}
to the set
\{(x,y)\in{\mathcal{V}}:\,|y|<y_{d}+\delta_{0}\}
. Since the function
y\to H_{j}(0,y)
is already analytically continued to the set
B(0,y_{d}+\delta_{0})\setminus\{y_{d}\}
and the functions
(x,y){\to}\psi_{2}(x,y)
and
(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,y)
are analytic in
{\mathcal{V}}
, it follows that the function
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in the set (
7.37
).

Step 2
: by (
7.32
) and (
2.9
), we have

y^{*}_{P}\leqslant y^{*}<y_{d}=y^{**}=Y_{1}(x_{d}),

and consequently,
(x_{d},y_{d})\not=(X_{1}(y^{*}_{P}),y^{*}_{P})
. Since by (
7.32
),
(x_{d},y_{d})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{21}
, using the definition of the curve
{\mathcal{S}}_{21}
(see (
2.17
)) one gets therefore

X_{1}(y^{*}_{P})<x_{d}\leqslant x^{**}_{P},

and consequently, using the assertions (ii) and (iv) of Proposition
7.1
, we conclude that for some
0<\delta_{3}<\delta_{2}
, the point
x_{d}
is an only zero of the function
x\to Y_{1}(x)-Y_{1}(x_{d})
in
C(x_{d}-\delta_{3},x_{d}+\delta_{3})\cap U_{\varepsilon}
. Since with our previous result, we have already proved that the function
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in the set (
7.37
), this proves that the function
x\to L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in the set (
7.38
). Finally, since on the annulus
C(x_{d}-\delta_{1},x_{d})
, the identity (
7.36
) holds, this proves that the function
x{\to}-\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)
can be analytically continued to the set
(C(x_{d}-\delta_{3},x_{d}+\delta_{3})\cap U_{\varepsilon}){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
.
∎

7.6.2.
Proof of Proposition
7.12

To complete the proof of Proposition
7.12
, we consider separately all possible cases :

–

when (B6) holds;

–

when (B5) holds with
x_{d}=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
;

–

when (B5) holds with
x_{d}=x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
;

–

when (B5) holds with
x_{d}=x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
.

Suppose first that (B6) holds. Then by Proposition
2.2
and the definition of
x_{d}
(see (
2.33
)), the following relations hold

(7.39)

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{*}<X_{1}(x^{**})<x_{d}=X_{2}(y^{**})<x^{**}\leqslant x^{**}_{P}.

Hence, by Lemma
7.5
and according to the definition of the set
U_{\varepsilon}
(see (
7.2
)), for some
\hat{\delta}>0
, the function
x\to-\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)
is already analytically continued to the set
C(x_{d}-\hat{\delta},x_{d}+\hat{\delta}){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
. By Proposition
7.7
, for any
x\in C(x^{*},x^{**})
,

|\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))|\leqslant\phi_{1}(|x|,Y_{1}(|x|))<1.

Since by (
7.39
),
x^{*}<x_{d}<x^{**}
, it follows that for
0<\delta^{\prime}<\min\{x^{**}-x_{d},x_{d}-x^{*}\}
,
the function
x\to 1/(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is analytic in
C(x_{d}-\delta^{\prime},x_{d}+\delta^{\prime})
and

(7.40)

1-\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))>0.

Since the function
x\to\eta_{j}(x)=-\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))H_{j}(x,0)
is already extended as an analytic function to the set
C(x_{d}-\hat{\delta},x_{d}+\hat{\delta}){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
, it follows that for
\delta=\min\{\delta^{\prime},\hat{\delta}\}
, the function the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be analytically continued to
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
by letting

(7.41)

H_{j}(x,0)=(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))^{-1}\bigl(L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))\bigr).

Remark finally that by (
7.32
),
y_{d}=y^{**}<y^{**}_{P}
. Hence, using exactly the same arguments as in the proof of Proposition
7.7
(it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
), one gets
\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})=\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**})=1
. Since by (
7.32
),
X_{1}(y_{d})<X_{2}(y_{d})=x_{d}
and
Y_{1}(x_{d})=y_{d}
, and since under our hypotheses (see Assumption (A3)(vi)) the real valued function
x\to\phi_{2}(x,y_{d})
is strictly increasing, it follows that

(7.42)

\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))=\phi_{2}(x_{d},y_{d})=\phi_{2}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})>\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})=1.

Using these relations together with (
7.41
), (
7.40
), (
7.33
) and (
7.5
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to x_{d}}(x_{d}-x)

\displaystyle H_{j}(x,0)=\lim_{x\to x_{d}}(x_{d}-x)(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))^{-1}\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to x_{d}}(x_{d}-x)(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))^{-1}(\phi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1)H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x))

\displaystyle=a_{5}\Bigl(L_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})+(\phi_{1}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})-1)H_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),0)\Bigr)=a_{5}\varkappa_{2}(j)

with

a_{5}=\left.(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)\left((1-\phi_{1}(x,y))\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\frac{d}{dx}Y_{1}(x)\right)^{-1}\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{d},y_{d})}>0.

The first assertion of Proposition
7.12
is therefore proved.

Suppose now that (B5) holds and let
x_{d}=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
. Then by Proposition
2.2
and the definition of
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see (
2.33
) and (
2.34
)), one has

y_{d}=y^{**}=Y_{1}(x^{**})=Y_{1}(x_{d})\quad\text{and}\quad x^{*}<X_{1}(y_{d})<x_{d}=X_{2}(y_{d})=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}.

and by Proposition
7.7
, for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to 1/(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))
is meromorphic in the annulus
C(x^{*},x_{d}+\delta)
, and has there a unique and simple pole at the point
x_{d}=x^{**}
with

(7.43)

\lim_{x\to x_{d}}\frac{x_{d}-x}{1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))}=\left.\left(\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\right)^{-1}\right|_{x=x_{d}}>0;

Here, the only difference with the previous case is that the function
x\to 1/(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
has a simple pole at
x_{d}=x^{**}
. Using therefore exactly the same arguments as in the previous case we obtain that for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
C(x_{d}-\delta,x_{d}+\delta){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
. And using finally (
7.41
) together with (
7.33
), (
7.42
), (
7.5
) and (
7.43
) we get

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to x_{d}}

\displaystyle(x_{d}-x)^{2}H_{j}(x,0)=a_{3}\Bigl(L_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})+(\phi_{1}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})-1)H_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),0)\bigr)=a_{3}\varkappa_{2}(j)

with

\displaystyle a_{3}

\displaystyle=\left.(\phi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1)\left(\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\frac{d}{dx}Y_{1}(x)\right)^{-1}\right|_{x=x_{d},y=y_{d}}

\displaystyle=\left.(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)\left(\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\frac{d}{dx}Y_{1}(x)\right)^{-1}\right|_{x=x_{d},y=y_{d}}>0.

The second assertion of Proposition
7.12
is therefore also proved.

Suppose now that (B5),
x_{d}=x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
hold. Then by Lemma
7.5
and according to the definition of the set
U_{\varepsilon}
(see (
7.2
)), by using the identity (
7.36
), the function
x\to-\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{1j}(x)
was already analytically continued to the set
C(x^{**}_{P}-\hat{\delta},x^{**}_{P}+\hat{\delta}){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+\hat{\delta}[
, and by Proposition
7.7
, for some
0<\delta<\hat{\delta}
, the function
x\to(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))^{-1}
is analytic in
C(x^{**}_{P}-\delta,x^{**}_{P}+\delta){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}+\delta[

and

H_{j}(x,0)=xh_{1j}(x)=x\eta_{j}(x)(\psi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)))^{-1}=\eta_{j}(x)(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))^{-1},\quad\forall x\in C(x^{**}_{P}-\delta,x^{**}_{P}).

Hence, by using the identity (
7.41
), the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be analytically continued to the set
C(x^{**}_{P}-\delta,x^{**}_{P}+\delta){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}[
, and using finally (
7.41
) together with (
7.33
), (
7.6
) and (
7.32
) one gets

\displaystyle\lim_{x\to x^{**}_{P}}

\displaystyle\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}H_{j}(x,0)

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to x^{**}_{P}}\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))^{-1}\bigl(L_{j}(x,Y_{1}(x))+\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))\bigr)

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to x^{**}_{P}}\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))^{-1}\psi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))h_{2j}(Y_{1}(x))

\displaystyle=\lim_{x\to x^{**}_{P}}\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}(1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))^{-1}(\phi_{2}(x,Y_{1}(x))-1)H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x))

\displaystyle=\tilde{a}_{4}\Bigl(L_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})+(\phi_{1}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})-1)H_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),0)\bigr)=\tilde{a}_{4}\varkappa_{2}(j)

with

\tilde{a}_{4}=(\phi_{2}(x_{d},y_{d})-1)\sqrt{\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x_{d},y_{d})/\partial_{x}P(x_{d},y_{d})}\left((1-\phi_{1}(x_{d},y_{d}))\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})\right)^{-1}>0,

Consider now the case when (B5),
x_{d}=x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
hold. In this case, with exactly the same arguments as above one gets that for some
\delta>0
, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be continued as an analytic function to the set
C(x^{**}_{P}-\delta,x^{**}_{P}+\delta){\setminus}[x^{**}_{P},x^{**}_{P}[
by using the identity (
7.41
). The only difference is here that now, by Proposition
7.7
,

\frac{1}{1-\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))}\sim\frac{c_{1}}{\sqrt{x^{**}_{P}-x}}

with

c_{1}=\left.\left(\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x,y))\sqrt{\partial_{x}P(x,y)/\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)}\right)^{-1}\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))}>0

and consequently, since in this case
x_{d}=x^{**}_{P}
and
y_{d}=Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})
, using (
7.41
) together with (
7.33
) and (
7.32
), one gets

\lim_{x\to x_{d}}(x_{d}-x)H_{j}(x,0)=a_{4}\Bigl(L_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})+(\phi_{1}(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})-1)H_{j}(X_{1}(y_{d}),0)\bigr)

with

a_{4}=\left.(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)\partial^{2}_{yy}P(x,y)\left(\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x,y)\partial_{x}P(x,y)\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\right)^{-1}\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{d},y_{d})}>0.

Proposition
7.12
is therefore proved.

7.7.
Analytic continuation of the function
(x,y)\to(1-P(x,y))H_{j}(x,y)

The following Lemma is the first step in the proof of the last assertion of Theorem
2
.

Lemma 7.6
.

If the conditions (A1)-(A3) are satisfied and (B2) holds, then

–

the set
\{(x,y)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}:\;x<x_{d},\;y<y_{d}\}
is non-empty;

–

there exists a neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of the set
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
such that, for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

the function
x{\to}(1{-}P(x,y))H_{j}(x,y)
can be analytically continued to the set
\{(x,y){\in}\Omega({\mathcal{V}}):|x|{<}x_{d},|y|{<}y_{d}\}
;

–

for any
(\hat{x},\hat{y}){\in}\{(x,y){\in}{\mathcal{S}}_{22}:x{<}x_{d},y{<}y_{d}\}
, the function
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}
is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
and for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
,

(7.44)

\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}(x,y)\to(\hat{x},\hat{y})\\
(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}\end{subarray}}(1-P(x,y))(H_{j}(x,y)-H_{j}(x,0)-H_{j}(0,y))=\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}

Proof.

Indeed, by Theorem
1
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
and
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
with
\hat{x}<x_{d}
and
\hat{y}<y_{d}
, there is a neighborhood
V(\hat{x},\hat{y})
of the point
(\hat{x},\hat{y})
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, such that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the function

\displaystyle(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)

\displaystyle H_{j}(x,0)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(0,y)

\displaystyle=L_{j}(x,y)+\psi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)+\psi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y)

is analytic in the polycircular set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ (|x|,|y|)\in V(\hat{x},\hat{y})\}
, and the function
(x,y)\to(1-P(x,y))(H_{j}(x,y)-H_{j}(x,0)-H_{j}(0,y))=Q(x,y)h_{j}(x,y)
can be continued as an analytic function to
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ (|x|,|y|)\in V(\hat{x},\hat{y})\}
by letting

(1-P(x,y))(H_{j}(x,y)-H_{j}(x,0)-H_{j}(0,y))=L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(x,0)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(0,y).

Hence, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
the quantity

\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}=L_{j}(\hat{x},\hat{y})+(\phi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y})-1)H_{j}(\hat{x},0)+(\phi_{2}(\hat{x},\hat{y})-1)H_{j}(0,\hat{y})

is well defined and as
(x,y)\to(\hat{x},\hat{y})
for
(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}
, (
7.44
) holds. Moreover, since by Theorem
1
, the function
(x,y)\to H_{j}(x,y)
is analytic in the set
\Omega_{d}(\Gamma)=\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:(|x|,|y|)\in\Gamma,\;|x|<x_{d},\;|y|<y_{d}\}
, and since the set
\Omega_{d}(\Gamma)
is a union of the poly-discs centered at the origin in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
, the power series

H_{j}(x,y)-H_{j}(x,0)-H_{j}(0,y)=\sum_{k_{1}=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k_{2}=1}^{\infty}g(j,k)x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}

converge on the set
\Omega_{d}(\Gamma)
, and consequently, for
(x,y)\in\Gamma
such that
x<x_{d}
and
y<y_{d}
,

H_{j}(x,y)-H_{j}(x,0)-H_{j}(0,y)\geqslant 0.

Since for
(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}
,
P(x,y)<1
, it follows that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the left hand side of (
7.44
) is non-negative and consequently, the function
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}
is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
.
∎

7.8.
Harmonic functions
\varkappa_{i}
,
\tilde{\varkappa}_{i}
and
\varkappa_{(x,y)}
and their properties

In this section, the second step of the proof of Theorem
2
is performed (see Section
7.1
). This is a subject of the following statement.

Proposition 7.13
.

Under the hypotheses (A1) - (A3), the following assertions hold:

i) The function
\varkappa_{1}
defined by (
2.35
)
is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, positive on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}E_{0}
and harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
in each of the following cases :

–

if one of the assertions (B0), (B1), (B3) or (B4) is valid;

–

if (B2) holds and
x^{*}<x^{**}_{P}
;

–

if (B2) holds,
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
.

ii) The function
\varkappa_{2}
defined by (
2.37
)
is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, positive on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}E_{0}
and harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
in each of the following cases :

–

if one of the assertions (B0), (B1), (B5) or (B6) is valid;

–

if (B2) holds and
y^{**}<y^{**}_{P}
;

–

if (B2) holds,
y^{**}=y^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P})=1
.

iii) If (B2) is valid with
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
, then the function
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}
defined by (
2.36
)
is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, positive on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}E_{0}
and harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
.

iv) If (B2) is valid, then for any
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
with
\tilde{x}<x_{d}
and
\hat{y}<y_{d}
, the function
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}
defined by (
2.39
) is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, positive on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}{\setminus}E_{0}
and harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
.

To get this result, we first show there that the left hand sides of (
2.40
), (
2.42
), (
2.44
), (
2.46
), (
2.48
), (
2.50
), (
2.52
) and (
2.54
) are non-negative. With these arguments we conclude that each of the functions defined by (
2.35
), (
2.36
), (
2.37
), (
2.39
) is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
. Next we show that each of these functions (in the corresponding cases i)-iv). of our proposition) is harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
, and by using suitable Lyapunov functions, we prove that each of them is not identically zero on the set
\{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+};\;\|j\|\geqslant N_{0}\}
. With this results and using Lemma
2.1
we will be able to show that each of the functions defined by (
2.35
), (
2.36
), (
2.37
), (
2.39
) (in the corresponding cases i)-iv). of our proposition) is positive throughout the set
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
.

7.8.1.
Preliminary estimates.

We begin the proof of this proposition with the following preliminary result

Lemma 7.7
.

Suppose that the conditions (A1)-(A3) are satisfied and let two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfy (
6.6
). Then for some constant
C>0
(depending on the points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
but do not depending on
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
),

(7.45)

{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty)\leqslant C\left(x_{1}^{j_{1}}y_{1}^{j_{2}}+x_{2}^{j_{1}}y_{2}^{j_{2}}\right),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}

and

(7.46)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(j,k)\left(x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}}\right)\leqslant C\left(x_{1}^{j_{1}}y_{1}^{j_{2}}+x_{2}^{j_{1}}y_{2}^{j_{2}}\right),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}.

Proof.

Indeed, if two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfy (
6.6
), then by Lemma
6.1
, for some
0<\theta<1
and some finite subset
E
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
such that
(0,0)\in E
and the function
f:\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined by
f(j_{1},j_{2}){=}x_{1}^{j_{1}}y_{1}^{j_{2}}{+}x_{2}^{j_{1}}y_{2}^{j_{2}}
, for all
(j_{1},j_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
satisfies the inequality

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(f(Z(1));\;\tau_{E}>1)\leqslant\theta f(j),\quad\forall j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+},

with
\tau_{E}=\inf\{n>0\penalty\ :\penalty\ Z(n)\in E\}
. By Lemma
6.2
, it follows that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the series

(7.47)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}g(j,k)f(k)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}g(j,k)\left(x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}}\right)

converges, and moreover, (see (
6.12
) in the proof of Lemma
6.2
), for

g_{E}(j,k)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=k;\;\tau_{E}\geqslant n),\quad j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E,\;k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+},

the following relation holds :

(7.48)

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g_{E}(j,k)f(k)\leqslant\frac{f(j)}{1-\theta}=\frac{x_{1}^{j_{1}}y_{1}^{j_{2}}+x_{2}^{j_{1}}y_{2}^{j_{2}}}{1-\theta}.

Since
(0,0)\in E
, from the last relation it follows in particular that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty)\leqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau_{E}<+\infty)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\min_{\ell\in E}f(\ell)\right)^{-1}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g_{E}(j,k)f(k)

(7.49)

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\min_{\ell\in E}f(\ell)\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{1-\theta}\left(x_{1}^{j_{1}}y_{1}^{j_{2}}+x_{2}^{j_{1}}y_{2}^{j_{2}}\right)

and consequently (
7.45
) is proved.

Remark finally that for any
j=(j_{1},0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(j,k)f(k)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g_{E}(j,k)f(k)+\sum_{\ell\in E\setminus\{(0,0)\}}g_{E}(j,\ell)\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(\ell,k)f(k),

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g_{E}(j,k)f(k)\penalty\ +\penalty\ {\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau_{E}<+\infty)\,\max_{\ell\in E}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(\ell,k)f(k).

Since the set
E
is finite and for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the series (
7.47
) converge, this last relation combined with
(
7.48
) and (
7.49
) prove (
7.46
).
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3), for any
0<y<y_{d}
there are two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfying (
6.6
) and such that for some
C>0
,

(7.50)

H_{j}(0,y)\leqslant C\left(x_{1}^{k_{1}}y_{1}^{k_{2}}+x_{2}^{k_{1}}y_{2}^{k_{2}}\right),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}.

Proof.

Indeed, if
0<y<y_{d}
, then, by the definition of the set
\Theta
, the point
(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}))
is in
\Theta
and, consequently, by Lemma
6.4
there are two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfying relations (
6.6
) and (
6.9
) with
(x,y)=(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}))
. By Lemma
7.7
, for these two points
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
, one gets (
7.46
) with
(x,y)=(0,y)
, and consequently (
7.50
) holds.
∎

7.8.2.
Proof of the first two assertions of Proposition
7.13

Consider first the case when one of the following assertions holds:

–

one of the cases (B0), (B1), (B3), (B4) holds;

–

(B2) and
x_{d}{<}x^{**}_{P}
hold;

–

(B2),
x_{d}{=}x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})){=}1
hold.

By Proposition
7.7
and the definition of
x_{d}
, we have always
x_{d}=x^{**}
and

(7.51)

\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))=1.

To prove Proposition
7.13
in each of the above cases, we first show that the function
\varkappa_{1}
is non-negative and harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
. Next we will prove that
\varkappa_{1}(j_{1},0)>0
for all
j_{1}>0
large enough, and using finally Lemma
2.1
, we will conclude that the function
\varkappa_{1}
is strictly positive on the set
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
.

Suppose first that either, one of the cases (B0), (B1), (B3), (B4) holds, or (B2) and
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
hold. Then, by Proposition
7.10
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

\lim_{x\to x_{d}}(x_{d}-x)H_{j}(x,0)=c\varkappa_{1}(j)

with
c>0
. Since
(x_{d}-x)H_{j}(x,0)\geqslant 0
for any real
x\in]0,x^{**}[
, it follows that
\varkappa_{1}(j)\geqslant 0
for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
.
When either one of the cases (B0), (B1), (B3), (B4) occurs or (B2) and
x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
hold, the function
\varkappa_{1}
is therefore non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
.

Suppose now that (B2),
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=1
hold. In this case, by Proposition
7.11
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

\lim_{x\to x_{d}}\sqrt{x_{d}-x}\,H_{j}(x,0)=c\,\varkappa_{1}(j)

with
c>0
. Since
\sqrt{x_{d}-x}\,H_{j}(x,0)\geqslant 0
for any real
x\in]0,x_{d}[
, it follows that
\varkappa_{1}(j)\geqslant 0
for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
. Hence, in this case, the function
\varkappa_{1}
is therefore also non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
.

Harmonicity property of the function
\varkappa_{1}
for the killed random walk. See Definition
1
.
For
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, by the definition of the functions
(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,y)
and
\varkappa_{1}:\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we have

\varkappa_{1}(j)=\begin{cases}x_{d}^{j_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{j_{2}}-{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau<+\infty)+(\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-1)H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x_{d}))&\text{if $j\not=(0,0)$},\\
\phi_{0}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-{\mathbb{P}}_{(0,0)}(\tau<+\infty)+(\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-1)H_{(0,0)}(0,Y_{1}(x_{d}))&\text{if $j=(0,0)$}.\end{cases}

Remark that for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{j}\left({\mathbb{P}}_{Z(1)}(\tau<+\infty);\;\tau>1\right)=

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau<+\infty)-\mu_{1}(-1,0)\mathbb{1}_{\{(0,1)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})-\mu_{2}(0,-1)\mathbb{1}_{\{(1,0)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle-\mu(-1,-1)\mathbb{1}_{\{(1,1)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})-\mu_{0}(0,0)\mathbb{1}_{\{(0,0)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})

and since the function
j\to H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x_{d}))
is potential for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
(see the properties of potential for the Markov chains functions in
[
25
]
),

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(H_{Z(1)}(0,Y_{1}(x_{d})),\;\tau>1)=H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x_{d}))-(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{j_{2}}\mathbb{1}_{\{j_{1}=0,j_{2}>0\}}(j_{1},j_{2}),

where for
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
we denote

\mathbb{1}_{\{k\}}(j)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $j=k$},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

By the definition of the function
Y_{1}
, we have
P(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))=1
, and because of (
7.51
),
\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))=1
. Hence, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{j}\bigl(x_{d}^{Z_{1}(1)}

\displaystyle(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{Z_{2}(1)};\;\tau>1\bigr)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\phi_{0}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-\mu_{0}(0,0)\mathbb{1}_{\{(0,0)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})&\text{if $j=(0,0)$},\\
x_{d}^{j_{1}}\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-\mu_{1}(-1,0)\mathbb{1}_{\{(0,1)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})&\text{if $j_{1}>0$ and $j_{2}=0$},\\
(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{j_{2}}\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-\mu_{2}(0,-1)\mathbb{1}_{\{(1,0)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})&\text{if $j_{1}=0$ and $j_{2}>0$},\\
{x_{d}^{j_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{j_{2}}}P(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-\mu(-1,-1)\mathbb{1}_{\{(1,1)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})&\text{if $j_{1}>0$, $j_{2}>0$},\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\phi_{0}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-\mu_{0}(0,0)\mathbb{1}_{\{(0,0)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})&\text{if $j=(0,0)$},\\
x_{d}^{j_{1}}-\mu_{1}(-1,0)\mathbb{1}_{\{(0,1)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})&\text{if $j_{1}>0$ and $j_{2}=0$},\\
(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{j_{2}}\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-\mu_{2}(0,-1)\mathbb{1}_{\{(1,0)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})&\text{if $j_{1}=0$ and $j_{2}>0$},\\
{x_{d}^{j_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{j_{2}}}-\mu(-1,-1)\mathbb{1}_{\{(1,1)\}}(j_{1},j_{2})&\text{if $j_{1}>0$, $j_{2}>0$},\end{cases}

With these relations, for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
j_{1}>0
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(\varkappa_{1}(Z(1)),\;\tau>1)

\displaystyle=x_{d}^{j_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{j_{2}}-{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau<+\infty)+(\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-1)H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x_{d})),

(7.52)

\displaystyle=\varkappa_{1}(j),

for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
j_{1}=0
and
j_{2}>0
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(\varkappa_{1}(Z(1))

\displaystyle={x_{d}^{j_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{j_{2}}}\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau<+\infty)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+(\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-1)\bigl(-{x_{d}^{j_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{j_{2}}}+H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x_{d}))\bigr)

(7.53)

\displaystyle=\varkappa_{1}(j).
