and for
j=(0,0)
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(\varkappa_{1}(Z(1))

\displaystyle=\phi_{0}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-{\mathbb{P}}_{(0,0)}(\tau<+\infty)+(\phi_{2}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))-1)H_{(0,0)}(0,Y_{1}(x_{d}))

(7.54)

\displaystyle=\varkappa_{1}(0,0).

Relations (
7.52
), (
7.53
) and (
7.54
) prove that function
\varkappa_{1}
is harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
.

To show that the function
\varkappa_{1}
is strictly positive at some point
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, we investigate an asymptotic behavior of this function as
j_{2}=0
and
j_{1}\to+\infty
. By Proposition
2.2
, in each of the cases (B0)-(B4), we have
0<Y_{1}(x_{d})<y_{d}
. Hence, by Lemma
7.8
, there are two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfying (
6.6
) for which (
7.50
) holds for
y=Y_{1}(x_{d})
and consequently, by Lemma
7.7
we obtain (
7.45
). Using these relations for
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
j_{2}=0
one gets

(7.55)

\left|\varkappa_{1}(j_{1},0)-x_{d}^{j_{1}}\right|\leqslant C_{1}\left(x_{1}^{j_{1}}+x_{2}^{j_{1}}\right),\quad\forall j=(j_{1},0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+},

with some do not depending on
j
constant
C_{1}>0
. Remark that
0<x_{2}<x_{1}
because the points
(x_{1},y_{1})
and
(x_{2},y_{2})
satisfy (
6.6
), and that
x_{1}<x_{d}
because
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
.
Hence, from the last relation it follows that

\varkappa_{1}(j_{1},0)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ x_{d}^{j_{1}}\quad\text{as $j_{1}\to\infty$},

and consequently, there is
N_{1}>0
such that

(7.56)

\varkappa_{1}(j_{1},0)>0\quad\text{ for any $j=(j_{1},0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}$ with $j_{1}\geqslant N_{1}$}.

Now we are ready to complete the proof of the first assertion of Proposition
7.13
: By Lemma
2.1
, there are
N_{0}>0
and a finite subset
E_{0}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
such that
(0,0)\not\in E_{0}
and for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
\|k\|>N_{0}
,

g(j,k)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=k,\;\tau_{0}>n)>0.

Hence, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
\|k\|>N_{0}
, there is
n_{j,k}\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that

{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n_{j,k})=k,\;\tau_{0}>n_{j,k})>0.

For any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
and
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, since the function
\varkappa_{1}
is non-negative and harmonic for the killed random walk, one has

\varkappa_{1}(j)={\mathbb{E}}_{j}(\varkappa_{1}(Z(n)),\;\tau_{0}>n)\geqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=k,\;\tau_{0}>n)\varkappa_{1}(k).

Using this relation together with (
7.56
), for
k=(k_{1},0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
k_{1}\geqslant\max\{N_{0},N_{1}\}
, one gets that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}

\varkappa(j)\geqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n_{j,k})=k,\;\tau_{0}>n_{j,k})\varkappa_{1}(k)>0,

and consequently, the function
\varkappa_{1}
is positive on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
.

The first assertion of Proposition
7.13
is therefore proved. To get the second assertion of this proposition, it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
.

7.8.3.
Proof of the third assertion of Proposition
7.13

Suppose that (B2) holds and let
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
and
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
. By the definition of the point
x_{d}
, we have here

x_{d}=x^{**}=x^{**}_{P},

and remark that in this case,

\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)=\left.\partial_{y}\left(\frac{L_{j}(x,y)+\left(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1\right)H_{j}(0,y)}{1-\phi_{1}(x,y)}\right)\right|_{(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))},\quad\;j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}.

To show that the function
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}
is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, we recall that in this cases, by Proposition
7.11
,

\lim_{x\to x_{d}}\sqrt{x_{d}-x}\,\frac{d}{dx}\,H_{j}(x,0)=c\,\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)

with
c>0
. Since for real
x\in]0,x^{**}_{P}[
, the function
x\to H_{j}(0,x)
is increasing on
]0,x^{**}_{P}[
,
it follows that
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)\geqslant 0
for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
.

Harmonicity property of the function
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}
for the killed random walk.
For
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, we have

(7.57)

\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)=\frac{\partial_{y}\phi_{1}((x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})))}{(1-\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})))^{2}}\varkappa_{1}(j)+\frac{1}{1-\phi_{1}((x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})))}\hat{\varkappa}_{1}(j)

with
\varkappa_{1}(j)
defined by (
2.35
) and

(7.58)

\displaystyle\hat{\varkappa}_{1}(j)=\partial_{y}L_{j}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))+

\displaystyle(\phi_{2}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))-1)\partial_{y}H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\partial_{y}\phi_{2}((x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})))H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})),

where

\partial_{y}L_{j}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))=\begin{cases}j_{2}\times(x^{**}_{P})^{j_{1}}(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))^{j_{2}-1}&\text{if $j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}$,}\\
\partial_{y}\phi_{0}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))&\text{if $j=(j_{1},j_{2})=(0,0)$}.\end{cases}

Remark now that for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, with the same arguments as in the proof of the first assertion of our proposition, one gets

(7.59)

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(\varkappa_{1}(Z(1)),\;\tau_{0}>1)=\varkappa_{1}(j)+(\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))-1)(x^{**}_{P})^{j_{1}}\mathbb{1}_{\{j_{1}>0,j_{2}=0\}}(j_{1},j_{2})

(here, an only difference with the proof of the first assertion of our proposition is that
\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))<1
). Moreover,
since the functions
j\to H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))
and
j\to\partial_{y}H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))
are potential for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
(see the properties of potential functions for Markov chains in Woess
[
25
]
), then for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, one has

(7.60)

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(H_{Z(1)}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})),\;\tau_{0}>1)=H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))-(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))^{j_{2}}\mathbb{1}_{\{j_{1}=0,j_{2}>0\}}(j_{1},j_{2})

and

(7.61)

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(\partial_{y}H_{Z(1)}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})),\;\tau_{0}>1)=\partial H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))-j_{2}\times(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))^{j_{2}-1}\mathbb{1}_{\{j_{1}=0,j_{2}>0\}}.

Finally, straightforward calculation shows that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(

\displaystyle\partial_{y}L_{Z(1)}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})),\;\tau_{0}>1)={\mathbb{E}}_{j}\bigl(Z_{2}(1)(x^{**}_{P})^{Z_{1}(1)}(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))^{Z_{2}(1)-1},\;\tau_{0}>1\bigr)

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))(x^{**}_{P})^{j_{1}}\mathbb{1}_{\{j_{1}>0,j_{2}=0\}}(j_{1},j_{2})

\displaystyle+\left(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})\partial_{y}P(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))+j_{2}P(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))\right)j_{2}(x^{**}_{P})^{j_{1}}(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))^{j_{2}-1}

\displaystyle+j_{2}(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))^{j_{2}-1}\bigl(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})\partial_{y}\phi_{2}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))+j_{2}\phi_{2}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))\bigr)\mathbb{1}_{\{j_{1}=0,j_{1}>0\}}(j_{1},j_{2}).

Since
\partial_{y}P(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}))=0
and
P(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}))=1
, from the last relation it follows that

(7.62)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(

\displaystyle\partial_{y}L_{Z(1)}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})),\;\tau_{0}>1)

\displaystyle=\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))(x^{**}_{P})^{j_{1}}\mathbb{1}_{\{j_{1}>0,j_{2}=0\}}(j_{1},j_{2})+j_{2}(x^{**}_{P})^{j_{1}}(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))^{j_{2}-1}

\displaystyle+j_{2}(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))^{j_{2}-1}\bigl(Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})\partial_{y}\phi_{2}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))+j_{2}\phi_{2}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))\bigr)\mathbb{1}_{\{j_{1}=0,j_{1}>0\}}(j_{1},j_{2}).

When combined together, relations (
7.57
) - (
7.61
) imply that
{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(Z(1),\;\tau_{0}>1)=\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)
for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, and hence, the function
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}
is harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
.

To show that the function
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}
is strictly positive on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}
, we investigate an asymptotical behavior of this function as
j_{2}=0
and
j_{1}\to+\infty
. Remark that for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}
with
j_{2}=0
,

\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)=C_{1}(x^{**}_{P})^{j_{1}}+C_{2}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty)+C_{3}H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))+C_{4}\partial_{y}H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))

with

(7.63)

C_{1}=\frac{\partial_{y}\phi_{1}((x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})))}{(1-\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})))^{2}}

and some
C_{2},C_{3},C_{4}\in{\mathbb{R}}
do not depending on
j_{1}
. When (B2) and
x^{**}=x^{**}_{P}
hold, we have

x_{d}=x^{**}=x^{**}_{P},\quad\text{and}\quad Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})<y_{d}=y^{**}\leqslant y^{**}_{P},

Hence by Lemma
7.8
, for
{\varepsilon}>0
such that
Y_{1}(x^{**})+{\varepsilon}<y_{d}
, there are two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfying (
6.6
) for which (
7.50
) holds with
y=Y_{1}(x^{**})+{\varepsilon}
, and by Lemma
7.7
we get (
7.45
). Using these relations for
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
j_{2}=0
we obtain

(7.64)

{\mathbb{P}}_{(j_{1},0)}(\tau_{0}<+\infty)\leqslant C_{5}\,\frac{x_{1}^{j_{1}}+x_{2}^{j_{1}}}{1-\theta},\quad\forall j=(j_{1},0)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+},

and

(7.65)

H_{(j_{1},0)}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})+{\varepsilon})\leqslant C_{6}(x_{1}^{j_{1}}+x_{2}^{j_{1}})

with some
C_{5}>0
and
C_{6}>0
do not depending on
j_{1}
. Since the function
y\to H_{j}(0,y)
is equal on the disk of its analyticity
B(0,y_{d})
to its power series with the positive coefficients, we have moreover

\partial_{y}H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))\leqslant C_{7}H_{j}(0,Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})+{\varepsilon}_{2})

with some do not depending on
j_{1}
constant
C_{7}>0
, and consequently, using (
7.8.3
), (
7.64
) and (
7.65
), we obtain

\left|\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j_{1},0)-C_{1}(x^{**}_{P})^{j_{1}}\right|\leqslant C_{9}(x_{1}^{j_{1}}+x_{2}^{j_{1}}),\quad\forall j_{1}>0,

with some do not depending on
j_{1}
constant
C_{8}>0
. Remark finally that the constant
C_{1}
defined by (
7.63
) is strictly positive because the function
y\to\phi_{1}(x^{**}_{P},y)
is convex and strictly increasing on
]0,+\infty[
. Hence,
with the same argument as in the proof of the first assertion of Proposition
7.13
we obtain

\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j_{1},0)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ C_{1}(x^{**}_{P})^{j_{1}}\quad\text{as $j_{1}\to+\infty$},

and consequently,
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}(j)>0
for all
j_{1}>0
large enough.

The function
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}
is therefore non-negative, harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
and non zero at the points
(j_{1},0)
for all
j_{1}>0
large enough. With this results and using exactly the same arguments as in the proof of the first assertion of our proposition, we conclude that the function
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}
is positive everywhere in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
.

7.8.4.
Proof of the last assertions of Proposition
7.13

Suppose now that (B2) holds, and let a point
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
be such that
\hat{x}<x_{d}
and
\hat{y}<y_{d}
. For such a point
(\hat{x},\hat{y})
, by Lemma
7.6
, the function
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}
is non-negative on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
.

The proof of the identity

{\mathbb{E}}_{j}(\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}(Z(1)),\;\tau_{0}>1)=\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}(j),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+},

is straightforward, it uses the arguments quite similar to those of the proofs of (
7.52
), (
7.53
) and (
7.54
). The function
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}
is therefore harmonic for the killed random walk
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
.

To prove that
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}(j)>0
for some
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
we consider the point
\hat{w}=w_{D}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=(u_{D}(\hat{x},\hat{y}),v_{D}(\hat{x},\hat{y}))
on the unit circle
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
defined by (
2.66
) with
x=\hat{x}
and
y=\hat{y}
, and we investigate an asymptotical behavior of
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}(j)
as
\|j\|\to+\infty
and
j/\|j\|\to\hat{w}
.

By the definitions of the functions
L_{j}
and
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}
, for
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}
,

\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}(j)=\hat{x}^{j_{1}}\hat{y}^{j_{2}}-{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\tau_{0}<+\infty)+\left(\phi_{1}(\hat{x},\hat{y})-1\right)H_{j}(\hat{x},0)+\left(\phi_{2}(\hat{x},\hat{y})-1\right)H_{j}(0,\hat{y})

Since we assume that
\hat{y}<y_{d}
, by Lemma
7.8
, there are two points
(x_{1},y_{1})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{2},y_{2})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
satisfying (
6.6
) for which (
7.50
) holds with
y=\hat{y}
, and by Lemma
7.7
we get (
7.45
). With the same arguments (it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
, we get that there are two
(x_{3},y_{3})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{1}}
and
(x_{4},y_{4})\in\overset{\circ}{D}\cap\overset{\circ}{D_{2}}
for which

H_{j}(\hat{x},0)\leqslant C^{\prime}\left(x_{3}^{k_{1}}y_{3}^{k_{2}}+x_{4}^{k_{1}}y_{4}^{k_{2}}\right),\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+},

with some do not depending on
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
constant
C^{\prime}>0
. Using the last relation together with (
7.45
) and (
7.50
), one gets

(7.66)

\bigl|\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}(j)-\hat{x}^{j_{1}}\hat{y}^{j_{2}}\bigr|\leqslant C_{1}\sum_{i=1}^{4}x_{i}^{j_{1}}y_{i}^{j_{2}}.

with some do not depending on
j
constant
C_{1}>0
. For
\hat{w}=w_{D}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=(u_{D}(\hat{x},\hat{y}),v_{D}(\hat{x},\hat{y}))
defined by relation (
2.66
) with
x=\hat{x}
and
y=\hat{y}
, the point
(\hat{x},\hat{y})
is an only point in the set
D
where the function
(x,y)\to x^{\hat{u}_{1}}y^{\hat{u_{2}}}
achieves its maximum over
D
. Since non of the points
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{4},y_{4})
is equal to
(\hat{x},\hat{y})
(recall that the points
(x_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(x_{4},y_{4})
belong to the interior of the set
D
and the point
(\hat{x},\hat{y})
belongs to the boundary of
D
), it follows that

\frac{1}{\hat{x}^{j_{1}}\hat{y}^{j_{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{4}x_{i}^{j_{1}}y_{i}^{j_{2}}\to 0\quad\text{as}\quad\|j\|\to+\infty\quad\text{and}\quad j/\|j\|\to\hat{u}

and consequently, using (
7.66
), we get

\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}(j)\sim\hat{x}^{j_{1}}\hat{y}^{j_{2}}\quad\text{as}\quad\|j\|\to+\infty\quad\text{and}\quad j/\|j\|\to\hat{u}.

This proves that
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}(j)>0
for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
\|j\|
large enough and
j\|j\|
closed enough to
\hat{u}
.

The function
\varkappa_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})}
is therefore non-negative, harmonic for
(Z_{\tau_{0}}(n))
, and non zero at some points
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
\|j\|>N_{0}
.
Hence, the similar arguments as in the proof of the first assertion of our proposition, we conclude that the function
\tilde{\varkappa}_{1}
is positive everywhere on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
.

7.9.
Proof of Theorem
2

Now we summarize the above results in orther to get Theorem
2
:

–

The first assertion of Theorem
2
follows from Proposition
7.10
and the first assertion of Proposition
7.13
.

–

The second assertion of Theorem
2
is a consequence of Proposition
7.11
and the third assertion of Proposition
7.13
.

–

Proposition
7.12
, Proposition
7.13
and the second assertion of Proposition
7.13
prove the assertions iii)- v) of Theorem
2
.

–

The last assertion of our theorem is proved by Lemma
7.6
and the last assertion of Proposition
7.13
.

8.
Asymptotics along the Axes

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
3
.

For
k_{2}{=}0
, the asymptotics (
2.56
)-(
2.62
) follows from Theorem
2
and the Tauberian-like theorem (see Corollary VI.1 of Flajolet and Sedgevick
[
10
]
) in a straightforward way. Hence, to complete the proof of our theorem, i.e. to get the asymptotics (
2.56
)-(
2.62
) for
k_{2}>0
, it is sufficient to show that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
,

(8.1)

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\frac{g(j,(n,k_{2}))}{g(j,(n,0))}=\nu_{1}(k_{2})>0,\quad\forall k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Before getting this result, let us notice that under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3), the function
y\to\psi_{1}(x_{d},y)/Q(x_{d},y)

is analytic at the origin
y=0
because the functions
y\to\psi_{1}(x_{d},y)
and
y\to Q(x_{d},y)
, see definitions (
2.25
) and (
2.24
), are analytic in a neighborhood of the closed disk
\overline{B}(0,Y_{2}(x_{d}))
and with the definition of the function
Q
(see (
2.24
))

Q(x_{d},0)={-}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}:\\
k_{2}=-1\end{subarray}}x_{d}^{k_{1}}\mu(k)<0.

For any given
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
, the quantity (
2.55
) is therefore well defined and equal to the
(n-1)
-th coefficient of the Taylor expansion of the function
y\to\psi_{1}(x_{d},y)/Q(x_{d},y)
in a neighborhood of the origin
y=0
:

\psi_{1}(x_{d},y)/Q(x_{d},y)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\nu_{1}(n)y^{n-1}.

To show that all coefficients
\nu_{1}(n),\,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
are positive, and to get (
8.1
) for
k_{2}>0
, we will need a probabilistic representation of the quantities
\nu_{1}(n),n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
, in terms of the invariant measure of the following Markov chain on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
:
Define the twisted positive measures
\tilde{\mu}
on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{1}
on
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
by letting

\tilde{\mu}(k_{1},k_{2})=x_{d}^{k_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{k_{2}}\mu(k_{1},k_{2}),\quad\forall(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},

\tilde{\mu}_{1}(k_{1},k_{2})=x_{d}^{k_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{k_{2}}\mu_{1}(k_{1},k_{2}),\quad\forall(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

With the definitions of the point
x_{d}
and the function
Y_{1}
, we have

\tilde{\mu}(\mathbb{Z}^{2})=P(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))=1\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{\mu}_{1}(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+})=\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))\leqslant 1,

and consequently, the twisted measure
\tilde{\mu}
is stochastic on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
and the twisted measure
\tilde{\mu}_{1}
is sub-stochastic on
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Consider now a twisted random walk
(\tilde{Z}^{1}(n)=(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{1}(n),\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n)))
on
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
with transition probabilities

{\mathbb{P}}_{(j_{1},j_{2})}\bigl(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}(1)=(k_{1},k_{2})\bigr)\penalty\ =\penalty\ \begin{cases}\tilde{\mu}(k-j)&\text{if $j_{2}>0$,}\\
\tilde{\mu}_{1}(k-j)&\text{if $j_{2}=0$.}\end{cases}

Since the transition probabilities of the twisted random walk
(\tilde{Z}^{1}(n)=(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{1}(n),\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n)))
are invariant with respect to the shifts on
(\ell,0)
for any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}
, its second component
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
is a Markov chain on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
(stochastic if
\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))=1
and sub-stochastic if
\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))<1
) with transition probabilities

{\mathbb{P}}_{j_{2}}\bigl(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(1)=k_{2}\bigr)\penalty\ =\penalty\ \begin{cases}\sum_{k_{1}=-1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mu}(k_{1},k_{2}-j_{2})&\text{if $j_{2}>0$,}\\
\\
\sum_{k_{1}=-1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mu}_{1}(k_{1},k_{2})&\text{if $j_{2}=0$.}\end{cases}

Recall that a non-negative measure
\pi_{1}
on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is invariant for
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
if, for any
\ell{\in}\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, the relation

(8.2)

\pi_{1}(\ell)=\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\pi_{1}(\ell^{\prime}){\mathbb{P}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n)=\ell)

holds. The following lemma relates the vector
\nu_{1}=(\nu_{1}(k_{2}),k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
with the unique (up to a multiplicative constant) invariant measure for
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
.

Lemma 8.1
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3),
\pi_{1}=(\pi_{1}(n)=\nu_{1}(n)(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{n},\,n\in{\mathbb{N}})
is a unique up to the multiplication by constants invariant measure of the Markov chain
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
.

Proof.

We prove this lemma in two steps: First we will show that the Markov chain
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
has an invariant measure
\pi_{1}=(\pi_{1}(n),\,n\in{\mathbb{N}})
with
\pi_{1}(0)=1
, and next we will prove that the generating function
f(y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\pi_{1}(n)(y/Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{n}
satisfies in a neighborhood of the point
y=0
in
\mathbb{C}
the identity

f(y)=\phi_{1}(x_{d},y)+P(x_{d},y)(f(y)-1)

and we will deduce from this identity that
\pi_{1}(n)=\nu_{1}(n)(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{n}
for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

To perform the first step of our proof, let us notice that under our hypotheses, the jumps of the Markov chain
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
are integrable and moreover, for any non-zero
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\ell}(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(1))

\displaystyle=\ell+\sum_{k_{2}=-1}^{\infty}k_{2}\sum_{k_{1}=-1}^{\infty}x_{d}^{k_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{k_{2}}\mu(k_{1},k_{2})=\ell+Y_{1}(x_{d})\partial_{y}P(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))

with
\partial_{y}P(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))\leqslant 0
. The Markov chain
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
is therefore recurrent if the measure
\tilde{\mu}_{1}
is stochastic, i.e. if
\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))=1
, and it is transient if
\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))<1
.

In the case when the Markov chain
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
is recurrent, it has a unique invariant measure
(\pi_{1}(\ell),\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
with
\pi_{1}(0)=1
and

\pi_{1}(\ell)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n)=\ell,\;\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(k)\not=0,\,\forall k<n),\quad\forall\ell>0.

Suppose now that the Markov chain
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
is transient, and let

\pi_{1}^{(k)}(\ell)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{k}(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n)=\ell),\quad k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

Then for any
k,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
k>\ell
, with a straightforward calculation one gets

(8.3)

\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\pi_{1}^{(k)}(\ell^{\prime}){\mathbb{P}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(1)=\ell)\penalty\ =\penalty\ \pi_{1}^{(k)}(\ell).

Remark moreover that because of Assumption (A2), by the strong Markov property,

{\pi_{1}^{(k)}(\ell)}/{\pi_{1}^{(k)}(0)}={\pi_{1}^{(\ell)}(\ell)}/{\pi_{1}^{(\ell)}(0)},\quad\forall k\geqslant\ell,

and
{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(1)=\ell)=0
for all
\ell^{\prime}>\ell+1
. Using (
8.3
), it follows that the measure
\pi_{1}=(\pi_{1}(\ell),\,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
with
\pi_{1}(0)=1
and

\pi_{1}(\ell)=\pi_{1}^{(\ell)}(\ell)/\pi_{1}^{(\ell)}(0),\quad\forall\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*},

is invariant for
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
. The first step of our proof is therefore completed.

Remark now that for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, using the identity

(8.4)

\sum_{\ell^{\prime}=0}^{\ell+n}\pi_{1}(\ell^{\prime}){\mathbb{P}}_{\ell^{\prime}}(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n)=\ell)=\pi_{1}(\ell)

with
\ell=0
, one gets

\pi_{1}(n)\leqslant\pi_{1}(0)\left({\mathbb{P}}_{n}(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n)=0)\right)^{-1}\leqslant\pi_{1}(0)\left(\sum_{k_{1}=-1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mu}(k_{1},-1)\right)^{-n}

with

\sum_{k_{1}=-1}^{\infty}\tilde{\mu}(k_{1},-1)=\sum_{k_{1}=-1}^{\infty}x_{d}^{k_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{-1}\mu(k_{1},-1)=-Q(x_{d},0)>0.

Hence, the generating function
f(y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\pi_{1}(n)(y/Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{n}
is analytic in a neighborhood of the origin
y=0
in
\mathbb{C}
, and by (
8.2
), for any non zero
y
, satisfies there the identity

f(y)=f(0)\Bigl(\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\tilde{\mu}_{1}(k_{1},k_{2})(y/Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{k_{2}}\Bigr)\\
+(f(y)-f(0))\Bigl(\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\mu}(k_{1},k_{2})(y/Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{k_{2}}\Bigr).

Since
f(0)=\pi_{1}(0)=1
and with the definition of the twisted measures
\tilde{\mu}
and
\tilde{\mu}_{1}
, for non-zero
y
, we have

\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}}\tilde{\mu}(k_{1},k_{2})\frac{y^{k_{2}}}{(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{k_{2}}}=P(x_{d},y)

and

\sum_{(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}}\tilde{\mu}_{1}(k_{1},k_{2})\frac{y^{k_{2}}}{(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{k_{2}}}=\phi_{1}(x_{d},y),

this implies that for any non-zero
y\in\mathbb{C}
with
|y|
small enough,

(f(y)-1)(1-P(x_{d},y))=\phi_{1}(x_{d},y)-1.

By the definition of the functions
(x,y)\to\psi_{1}(x,y)
and
(x,y)\to Q(x,y)
, from the last relation it follows that for any
y\in\mathbb{C}
with
|y|
small enough,

(f(y)-1)Q(x_{d},y)=y\psi_{1}(x_{d},y)

and consequently, since
Q(x_{d},0)\not=0
, for any
y\in\mathbb{C}
with
|y|
small enough, we have also

f(y)=1+\frac{y\psi_{1}(x_{d},y)}{Q(x_{d},y)}.

From the last relation, by the uniqueness of the coefficients of the Taylor expansion at the point
y=0
, it follows that

\pi_{1}(n)(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{-n}=\nu_{1}(n),\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

and consequently, the measure
\pi_{1}=(\nu_{1}(n)Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{n},\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
is the unique invariant measure of the Markov chain
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
with
\pi_{1}(0)=1
.
∎

As a straightforward consequence of Lemma
8.1
one gets the following property of the coefficients
\nu_{1}(n),n\in{\mathbb{N}}
:

Corollary 8.1
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3), all coefficients
\nu_{1}(n),\,n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, are positive and
(\nu_{1}(n),n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+})
is an only positive solution of the system

(8.5)

\sum_{\ell_{1}=1}^{\infty}\mu_{1}(\ell_{1},n)+\sum_{\ell_{2}=1}^{n+1}\nu_{1}(\ell_{2})\sum_{\ell_{1}=1}^{\infty}\mu(\ell_{1},n-\ell_{2})=\nu_{1}(n),\quad k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

Proof.

Indeed, since by Lemma
8.1
,

\nu_{1}(n)=\pi_{1}(n)(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{-n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, the system (
8.5
) is equivalent to (
8.2
). Under our hypotheses, the Markov chain
(\tilde{Z}^{(1)}_{2}(n))
is irreducible on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\pi_{1}(0)=1\not=0
. It follows that
\pi_{1}(n)>0
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and consequently also
\nu_{1}(n)=\pi_{1}(n)(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{-n}>0
for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.
∎

From this result it follows that to get (
8.1
), it is sufficient to show that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
k_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
, the sequence
\bigl(g(j,(n,k_{2}))/g(j,(n,0))\bigr)
converges and the limits
\lim_{n\to\infty}g(j,(n,k_{2}))/g(j,(n,0))\bigr)
,
k_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, satisfy the system of the equations (
8.5
). To prove the convergence of these sequences, the following preliminary results will be needed.

Corollary 8.2
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3), if one of the conditions (B0)-(B6) holds, then for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}

(8.6)

\lim_{n}g(j,(k_{1}+n,0))/g(j,(n,0))=1,\quad\forall k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

and there are constants
c_{j}>0
and
c^{\prime}_{j}>0
such that for any
k_{1}>0
,

(8.7)

c_{j}k_{1}^{\gamma}x_{d}^{-k_{1}}\leqslant g(j,(k_{1},0))\leqslant c^{\prime}_{j}k_{1}^{\gamma}x_{d}^{-k_{1}},

where

\gamma=\begin{cases}-1/2&\text{ if $x_{d}=x^{**}_{P}$ and either (B2) holds with $\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))=1$,}\\
\,&\text{\hskip 10.22217ptor (B5) holds with $\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))<1$,},\\
-3/2&\text{if (B2) holds with $x_{d}=x^{**}_{P}$ and $\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))<1$},\\
1&\text{if (B5) holds with $x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}$},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

This result is a straightforward consequence of the asymptotics (
2.56
)-(
2.62
) with
k_{2}=0
. These asymptotics
follow from Theorem
2
and a Tauberian-like theorem (see Corollary VI.1 of Flajolet and Sedgevick
[
10
]
) in a straightforward way.
∎

Lemma 8.2
.

Under the hypotheses (A1)-(A3), for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
and
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
there are three constants
c_{1}>0
,
c_{2}>0
and
N(k_{2})>0
, such that

(8.8)

c_{1}\,g(j,(k_{1},0))\leqslant g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))\leqslant c_{2}\,g(j,(k_{1},0)),

for any
k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
such that
k_{1}\geqslant N(k_{2})
.

Proof.

Remark that for any
j,k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
and
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
, by using the Markov property one gets

\displaystyle g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))

\displaystyle\geqslant\sum_{n=N}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=(k_{1},k_{2}),\,Z(N)=(k_{1},0),\;\tau_{0}>n)

(8.9)

\displaystyle\geqslant g(j,(k_{1},0)){\mathbb{P}}_{(k_{1},0)}(Z(N)=(k_{1},k_{2}),\;\tau_{0}>0).

Moreover, similar arguments as in the proof of Lemma
7.2
shows that for any
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, there is
N\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
and a sequence
\bigl(\ell_{1}^{(0)},\ell_{2}^{(0)}\bigr),\ldots,\bigl(\ell_{1}^{(N)},\ell_{2}^{(N)}\bigr)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that

\ell^{(0)}+\ldots+\ell^{(n)}\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+},\quad\forall n\in\{0,\ldots,N\},

\sum_{n=0}^{N}\ell^{(n)}=(0,k_{2})\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\mu_{1}\bigl(\ell^{(0)}\bigr)\prod_{n=1}^{N}\mu\bigl(\ell^{(n)}\bigr)>0.

Hence, using Assumption (A2) we conclude that for any
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
k_{1}>N
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{(k_{1},0)}\bigl(Z(n(k_{2}))=(k_{1},k_{2}),\;\tau_{0}>0\bigr)\geqslant\mu_{1}(\ell^{(0)})\prod_{n=1}^{N}\mu(\ell^{(n)})>0.

When combined with (
8.9
) the last relation proves the first inequality of (
8.8
) with

c_{1}=\mu_{1}(\ell^{(0)})\prod_{n=1}^{N}\mu(\ell^{(n)}).

The proof of the second inequality of (
8.8
) is quite similar.
∎

Now we are ready to complete proof of Theorem
3
. Throughout our proof, the starting point
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
will be given.

Since by Lemma
8.2
for any
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, the sequence
(g(j,(n,k_{2}))/g(j,(n,0)),\,n\geqslant N_{n})
is bounded below and above by some positive constants, to get (
8.1
) it is sufficient to show that for any subsequence
(N_{n})
of the sequence of non-zero natural numbers
(n)
, for which the sequence of functions

(8.10)

k_{2}\to f_{n}(k_{2})=g(j,(N_{n},k_{2}))/g(j,(N_{n},0))

converges point-wise in
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, one has

(8.11)

\lim_{n}f_{n}(k_{2})=\nu_{1}(k_{2}),\quad\forall k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

Suppose now that for a subsequence
(N_{n})
, the sequence of functions
(f_{n})
defined by (
8.10
) converges point-wise in
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and let
f_{\infty}=\lim_{n}f_{n}
. By Corollary
8.1
, to get (
8.11
) it is sufficient to show that the limit function
f_{\infty}
satisfies the system of equations

(8.12)

\sum_{\ell_{1}=1}^{\infty}\mu_{1}(\ell_{1},k_{2})+\sum_{\ell_{2}=1}^{n+1}f_{\infty}(\ell_{2})\sum_{\ell_{1}=1}^{\infty}\mu(\ell_{1},k_{2}-\ell_{2})=f_{\infty}(k_{2}),\quad k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

To get this result, we consider the sequence of functions

F_{n}:\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
defined for any
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
by

F_{n}(k_{1},k_{2})=\begin{cases}g(j,(k_{1}+N_{n},k_{2}))/g(j,(N_{n},0))&\text{if $k_{1}+N_{n}\geqslant 0$,}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Remark that by (
8.6
), the sequence of functions
(F_{n})
also converges point-wise in
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, and for any
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

(8.13)

\lim_{n}F_{n}(k_{1},k_{2})=\lim_{n}f_{n}(k_{2})=f_{\infty}(k_{2}).

Remark moreover that for any
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{j\}
,

\displaystyle g(j,(k_{1},k_{2}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}}g(j,(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})){\mathbb{P}}_{(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})}(Z(1)=(k_{1},k_{2}),\,\tau_{0}>1)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\ell_{2}=1}^{k_{2}+1}g(j,(0,\ell_{2}))\mu_{2}(k_{1},k_{2}-\ell_{2})+\sum_{\ell_{1}=1}^{k_{1}+1}g(j,(\ell_{1},0))\mu_{1}(k_{1}-\ell_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt+\sum_{\ell_{1}=1}^{k_{1}+1}\sum_{\ell_{2}=1}^{k_{2}+1}g(j,(\ell_{1},\ell_{2}))\mu(k_{1}-\ell_{1},k_{2}-\ell_{2}).

Using this relation with
k_{1}=N_{n}
, for any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that
N_{n}>j_{1}
, one gets

\displaystyle F_{n}(0,k_{2})=\sum_{\ell_{2}=1}^{k_{2}+1}F_{n}(-N_{n},\ell_{2})

\displaystyle\mu_{2}(N_{n},k_{2}-\ell_{2})+\sum_{\ell_{1}=-1}^{N_{n}}F_{n}(-\ell_{1},0))\mu_{1}(\ell_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\ell_{1}=-1}^{N_{n}}\sum_{\ell_{2}=1}^{k_{2}+1}F_{n}(-\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\mu(\ell_{1},k_{2}-\ell_{2})\quad\quad\forall k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},

and consequently, using (
8.13
) we will obtain (
8.12
) if we prove that for any
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\ell_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}+1\}
, the following relations hold

(8.14)

\lim_{n}F_{n}(-N_{n},\ell_{2})\mu_{2}(N_{n},k_{2}-\ell_{2})=0,

(8.15)

\lim_{n}\sum_{\ell_{1}=-1}^{N_{n}}F_{n}(-\ell_{1},0))\mu_{1}(\ell_{1},k_{2})=\sum_{\ell_{1}=-1}^{\infty}\lim_{n}F_{n}(-\ell_{1},0))\mu_{1}(\ell_{1},k_{2})

and

(8.16)

\lim_{n}\sum_{\ell_{1}=-1}^{N_{n}}F_{n}(-\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\mu(\ell_{1},k_{2}-\ell_{2})=\sum_{\ell_{1}=-1}^{\infty}\lim_{n}F_{n}(-\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\mu(\ell_{1},k_{2}-\ell_{2}).

To get (
8.14
) we remark that by Corollary
8.2
, there is a constant
C>0
such that for any
N_{n}
large enough,

F_{n}(-N_{n},\ell_{2})=g(j,(0,\ell_{2}))/g(j,(N_{n},0))\leqslant g(j,(0,\ell_{2}))C(N_{n})^{\gamma}x_{d}^{N_{n}}

and consequently, for any
{\varepsilon}>0
, there is a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any
N_{n}
large enough,

F_{n}(-N_{n},\ell_{2})\leqslant g(j,(0,\ell_{2}))C_{\varepsilon}(1+{\varepsilon})^{N_{n}}x_{d}^{N_{n}}.

Since by Assumption (A3)(ii), the generating function
\phi_{1}
is finite in a neighborhood of the point
(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))
, there is
{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{1}(n,k_{2})(1+{\varepsilon})^{n}x_{d}^{n}=0.

and consequently, for any
k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and
\ell_{2}\in\{1,\ldots,k_{2}+1\}
, (
8.14
) holds.

To get (
8.15
) we use the implicit function theorem and Corollary
8.2
. By Corollary
8.2
, there is a constant
C_{j}>0
such that for any
N_{n}>0
and
\ell_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
-1\leqslant\ell_{1}\leqslant N_{n}
,

(8.17)

F_{n}(-\ell_{1},0)=\frac{g(j,(N_{n}-\ell_{1},0))}{g(j,(N_{n},0))}\leqslant C_{1}\frac{(N_{n}-\ell_{1})^{\gamma}}{N_{n}^{\gamma}}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}.

In the case when
\gamma\geqslant 0
, it follows that for any
N_{n}>0
and
\ell_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
-1\leqslant\ell_{1}\leqslant N_{n}
,

F_{n}(-\ell_{1},0)\leqslant C_{1}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}},

and consequently, since
F_{n}(-\ell_{1},0)=0
for all
\ell_{1}\geqslant N_{n}
, and since under our hypotheses,

\sum_{\ell_{1}=-1}^{\infty}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}\mu_{1}(\ell_{1},k_{2})\leqslant(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2}}\sum_{\ell=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}}^{\infty}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{\ell_{2}}\mu_{1}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})=\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))<+\infty,

by the implicit function theorem one gets (
8.15
). In the case when
\gamma\geqslant 0
, (
8.15
) is therefore proved.

Suppose now that
\gamma<0
. In this case, using (
8.17
), one gets that for any
N_{n}>0
and
\ell_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
-1\leqslant\ell_{1}<N_{n}
,

F_{n}(-\ell_{1},0)\leqslant C_{1}\frac{N_{n}^{|\gamma|}}{(N_{n}-\ell_{1})^{|\gamma|}}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}\leqslant\begin{cases}C_{1}N_{n}^{|\gamma|}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}\leqslant C_{1}(2\ell_{1})^{|\gamma|}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}&\text{if $\ell_{1}>N_{n}/2$}\\
C_{1}2^{|\gamma|}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}&\text{if $\ell_{1}\leqslant N_{n}/2$}\end{cases}

and consequently, for any
{\varepsilon}>0
there is a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that
N_{n}>0
and
\ell_{1}\in\{-1,\ldots,N_{n}-1\}
, one has

(8.18)

F_{n}(-\ell_{1},0)\leqslant C_{\varepsilon}(1+{\varepsilon})^{\ell_{1}}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}.

Since
F_{n}(-\ell_{1},0)=0
for all
\ell_{1}\geqslant N_{n}
, and since under our hypotheses, for some
{\varepsilon}>0
small enough,

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1}=-1}^{\infty}(1+{\varepsilon})^{\ell_{1}}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}\mu_{1}(\ell_{1},k_{2})

\displaystyle\leqslant(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2}}\!\!\!\sum_{\ell=(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}_{+}}^{\infty}(1+{\varepsilon})^{\ell_{1}}x_{d}^{\ell_{1}}(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{\ell_{2}}\mu_{1}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})

(8.19)

\displaystyle\leqslant(Y_{1}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2}}\phi_{1}((1+{\varepsilon})x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))<+\infty,

by the implicit function theorem, it follows (
8.15
). Relation (
8.15
) is therefore proved.

To get (
8.16
) we use first Lemma
8.2
. By Lemma
8.2
, there are two constants
N(\ell_{2})
and

C_{1}>0
such that for any
N_{n}\geqslant 0
and
\ell_{1}\in\{-1,\ldots,N_{n}-N(\ell_{2})\}
,

F_{n}(-\ell_{1},\ell_{2})=\frac{g(j,(N_{n}-\ell_{1},\ell_{2}))}{g(j,(N_{n},0))}\leqslant C_{1}\frac{g(j,(N_{n}-\ell_{1},0))}{g(j,(N_{n},0))}

and for
\ell_{1}\in\{N_{n}-N(\ell_{2})+1,\ldots,N_{n}-1\}
,

F_{n}(-\ell_{1},\ell_{2})=\frac{g(j,(N_{n}-\ell_{1},\ell_{2}))}{g(j,(N_{n},0))}\frac{C_{2}}{g(j,(N_{n},0))}

with
C_{2}=\max\{g(j,(1,0)),\ldots,g(j,(N(\ell_{2}),0)\}
. Hence with the similar arguments as above one gets that for any
{\varepsilon}>0
there is a constant
C_{\varepsilon}>0
such that
N_{n}>0
and
\ell_{1}\in\{-1,\ldots,N_{n}-1\}
, (
8.18
) holds, and consequently, using (
8.19
) we conclude that (
8.16
) holds.

Theorem
3
is therefore proved.

9.
Asymptotics along Directions of
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorem
4
.

9.1.
Main ideas and the sketch of the proof

The main ideas of the proof of Theorem
4
are the following:

By using Theorem
1
, we first get an integral representation

(9.1)

g(j,k)=\\
\int_{|x|=\hat{x}}\int_{|y|=\hat{y}}\frac{L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{1}(x,y){-}P(x,y))H_{j}(x,0){+}(\phi_{2}(x,y){-}P(x,y))H_{j}(0,y)}{(2\pi i)^{2}\,x^{k_{1}+1}y^{k_{2}+1}(1{-}P(x,y))}dxdy

for any
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in{\Gamma}
with
\hat{x}<x_{d}
and
\hat{y}<y_{d}
. Next we show that the set
\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:x<x_{d},y<y_{d}\}
is non empty. By the definition of the set
{\Gamma}
, the set
\overset{\circ}{D}
is included to the set
{\Gamma}
, with this result we will be able to consider the integral representation (
9.1
) with
\hat{x}<x_{d}
,
\hat{y}<y_{d}
such that
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\overset{\circ}{D}
.

To prove the first tree assertions of Theorem
4
, the integral representation (
9.1
) is next modified in the following way: since the functions
(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,y)
and
(x,y)\to(1-P(x,y))^{-1}
are analytic in the polycircular set
\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D})
, and, by Theorem
1
,

–

the function
(x,y){\to}(\phi_{1}(x,y){-}P(x,y))H_{j}(x,0)
is analytic in the polycircular set
\{(x,y){\in}\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D}){:}|x|{<}x_{d}\}
,

–

the function
(x,y){\to}(\phi_{2}(x,y){-}P(x,y))H_{j}(0,y)
is analytic in the polycircular set
\{(x,y){\in}\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D}){:}|y|{<}y_{d}\}
,

and since the set
\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D})
does not contain zeros of the function
(x,y){\to}x^{k_{1}+1}y^{k_{2}+1}(1{-}P(x,y))
, we can write

(9.2)

g(j,k)=I_{0}(j,k)+I_{1}(j,k)+I_{2}(j,k)

with

(9.3)

I_{0}(j,k)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{|x|=\hat{x}_{0}}\int_{|y|=\hat{y}_{0}}\frac{L_{j}(x,y)}{x^{k_{1}+1}y^{k_{2}+1}(1-P(x,y))}dx\,dy,

(9.4)

I_{1}(j,k)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{|x|=\hat{x}_{1}}\int_{|y|=\hat{y}_{1}}\frac{(\phi_{1}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(x,0)}{x^{k_{1}+1}y^{k_{2}+1}(1-P(x,y))}dx\,dy

and

(9.5)

I_{2}(j,k)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{|x|=\hat{x}_{2}}\int_{|y|=\hat{y}_{2}}\frac{(\phi_{2}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(0,y)}{x^{k_{1}+1}y^{k_{2}+1}(1-P(x,y))}dx\,dy

for any
(\hat{x}_{0},\hat{y}_{0})\in\overset{\circ}{D}
,
(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1})\in\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:x<x_{d}\}
, and
(\hat{x}_{2},\hat{y}_{2})\in\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:y<y_{d}\}
.

To prove the first assertion of Theorem
4
, it is sufficient to get the asymptotic behavior (
2.75
) when
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
w_{k}=k/\|k\|\to w
for any
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
. In order to get this result, we identify the asymptotic behavior of
I_{1}(j,k)
by using the residue theorem (applied first for the integral with respect to
x
and next for the integral with respect to
y
), and using next
large deviation estimates of
I_{0}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)
we prove that the terms
I_{0}(j,k)
and
I_{3}(j,k)
are negligible with respect to
I_{1}(j,k)
.

The proof of the second assertion of Theorem
4
is exactly the same as the proof of the first assertion, it is sufficient to exchange the roles of the first and the second coordinates of the points
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
.

To prove the assertions (iii)-(v) of our theorem, we show that the term
I_{0}(j,k)
is negligible with respect to
I_{1}(j,k)+I_{2}(j,k)
and we identify (in the same way as in the proof of the first assertion of our theorem) the asymptotic behavior of each term
I_{1}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)
.

Finally, the last assertion of Theorem
4
, is obtained as a consequence of Proposition 1 of the paper
[
11
]
.

The proof of Theorem
4
is organized as follows:

The integral representations (
9.1
) and (
9.2
) of the Green function
g(j,k)
are obtained respectively in Lemma
9.1
and Corollary
9.1
of Section
9.2
.

Section
9.3
is devoted to the upper large deviation estimates for the integrals
I_{0}(j,k)
,
I_{1}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)
. These large deviation estimates will be used in order to identify the dominant terms of the sum
I_{0}(j,k){+}I_{0}(j,k){+}I_{0}(j,k)
of the right-hand side of (
9.2
).

In Section
9.4
we obtain exact asymptotics of
I_{1}(j,k)
(resp
I_{2}(j,k)
) as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
for those
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
for which
u>u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
(resp. for which
v>v_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})
). This is a subject of Lemma
9.6
and Lemma
9.5
below. Remark that we do not need the exact asymptotics of
I_{1}(j,k)
(resp
I_{2}(j,k)
) when
k/\|k\|\to w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
and
u\leqslant u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
(resp.
v\leqslant v_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})
): the large deviation asymptotics obtained in Section
9.3
will be in this case sufficient.

In Section
9.5
the proof of the first assertion of our theorem is completed. It will be proved there that for any
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
, the inequality
u>u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
holds, and consequently that the results of Section
9.4
provide the exact asymptotic for
I_{1}(j,k)
as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
. A comparison of the exact asymptotics for
I_{1}(j,k)
as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
with the asymptotics of
I_{2}(j,k)
and
I_{0}(j,k)
will show that the terms
I_{0}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)
are negligible with respect to
I_{1}(j,k)
.

The second assertion of Theorem
4
is obtained by using the arguments of the symmetry: to get this statement, the same arguments as in the proof of the first assertion can be applied if one exchanges the roles of
x
and
y
.

In Section
9.6
and
9.7
, we complete the proof of the assertions (iii)-(v) of Theorem
4
. It will be shown in these cases, the exact asymptotics of
I_{1}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)
are given by the results of Section
9.4
and that the term
I_{0}(j,k)
is negligible with respect to the terms
I_{1}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)
. The exact asymptotic of the Green function
g(j,k)
will be obtained by comparing the exact asymptotics of
I_{1}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)
.

9.2.
Integral representation of the coefficients
g(j,k)

Lemma 9.1
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A4), the set
\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:x<x_{d},\;y<y_{d}\}
is non empty and for any
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\overset{\circ}{D}
such that
\hat{x}<x_{d}
and
\hat{y}<y_{d}
, (
9.1
) holds.

Proof.

By the first assertion of Theorem
1
, the series (
2.28
) (and consequently also the series (
6.1
)) converge on the polycircular set
\{(x,y){\in}\Omega({\Gamma}):|x|{<}x_{d},|y|{<}y_{d}\}
. The function
(x,y){\to}H_{j}(x,y)
is therefore analytic in

\{(x,y){\in}\Omega({\Gamma}):|x|{<}x_{d},|y|{<}y_{d}\}
and for any
(\hat{x},\hat{y}){\in}{\Gamma}
with
\hat{x}{<}x_{d}
and
\hat{y}{<}y_{d}
, one has

(9.6)

g(j,k)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{|x|=\hat{x}}\int_{|y|=\hat{y}}\frac{H_{j}(x,y)}{x^{k_{1}+1}y^{k_{2}+1}}dx\,dy,\quad\forall j,k{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}

Remark now that under our assumptions, the set
\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:x<x_{d},y<y_{d}\}
is non empty:

–

when one of the assertions (B0)-(B2) is valid, this is a consequence of the first assertion of Lemma
6.4

–

when one of the assertions (B3) or (B4) holds, the set
\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:x<x_{d},y<y_{d}\}
is non empty because in this case and
x^{*}_{P}<x_{d}=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
,
y_{d}>Y_{1}(x_{d})
and by Lemma
4.1
, any point of the line segment
[(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d})),(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))]
, aside of the ends points
(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))
and
(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
belongs to the interior
\overset{\circ}{D}
of the set
D
;

–

similarly, when one of the assertions (B5) or (B6) holds, the set
\{(x,y){\in}\overset{\circ}{D}:x{<}x_{d},y{<}y_{d}\}
is non empty because
y^{*}_{P}{<}y_{d}{=}y^{**}{<}y^{**}_{P}
,
x_{d}>X_{1}(y_{d})
and by Lemma
4.1
, any point of line segment
[(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d}),(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})]
, aside of the end points
(X_{1}(y_{d}),y_{d})
and
(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})
belongs to the interior of the set
D
.

By the definition of the set
{\Gamma}
, the set
\overset{\circ}{D}
is included to
{\Gamma}
, it follows that (
9.6
) holds also for any
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\overset{\circ}{D}
with
\hat{x}<x_{d}
and
\hat{y}<y_{d}
.

Furthermore, by the second assertion of Theorem
1
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, on the set
\{(x,y){\in}\Omega({\Gamma}):\penalty\ |x|{<}x_{d},\;|y|{<}y_{d}\}
, the function
h_{j}
satisfies the identity (
2.30
). Since the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D}):x<x_{d},y<y_{d}\}
has an empty intersection with the sets
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:x=0\}
and
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ y=0\}
, it follows that on the set
\{(x,y){\in}\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D}):x{<}x_{d},y{<}y_{d}\}
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, the relation

\displaystyle(1-P(x,y))(H_{j}(x,y)-H_{j}(x,0)-

\displaystyle H_{j}(0,y))=Q(x,y)h_{j}(x,y)

\displaystyle=L_{j}(x,y)+\psi_{1}(x,y)h_{1j}(x)+\psi_{2}(x,y)h_{2j}(y)

\displaystyle=L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(x,0)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(0,y),

holds, or equivalently,

(1-P(x,y))H_{j}(x,y)=L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{1}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(x,0)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(0,y).

Since the function
(x,y)\to 1/(1-P(x,y))
is analytic in the set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ (|x|,|y|)\in\overset{\circ}{D}\}
, this implies that, on the set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ (|x|,|y|)\in\overset{\circ}{D},\;|x|<x_{d},\;|y|<y_{d}\}
, we have also the identity

H_{j}(x,y)=\frac{L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{1}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(x,0)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(0,y)}{1-P(x,y)}.

The last identity combined with (
9.6
) proves (
9.1
).
∎

Since the functions
(x,y)\to L_{j}(x,y)
and
(x,y)\to x^{-k_{1}-1}y^{k_{2}-1}(1-P(x,y))^{-1}
are analytic in the polycircular set
\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D})
, and by Theorem
1
,
the functions
(x,y)\to(\phi_{1}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(x,0)
and
(x,y)\to(\phi_{2}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(0,y)
are analytic respectively on
\{(x,y){\in}\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D}):|x|{<}x_{d}\}
and
\{(x,y)\in\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D}):\,|y|<y_{d}\}
, as a straightforward consequence of Lemma
9.1
we obtain

Corollary 9.1
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A4), for any
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
and
k{=}(k_{1},k_{2}){\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{(0,0)\}
,
relation (
9.2
) holds with
I_{0}(j,k)
,
I_{1}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)
defined respectively by (
9.3
), (
9.4
) and (
9.5
), for any
(\hat{x}_{0},\hat{y}_{0}){\in}\overset{\circ}{D}
,
(\hat{x}_{1},\hat{y}_{1}){\in}\{(x,y){\in}\overset{\circ}{D}:x{<}x_{d}\}
, and
(\hat{x}_{2},\hat{y}_{2}){\in}\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:y{<}y_{d}\}
.

9.3.
Large deviation estimates of the quantities
I_{0}(j,k)
,
I_{1}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)

Lemma 9.2
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A4), for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
,
and
j,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, as
\|k\|\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
,

(9.7)

\limsup_{k}\|k\|^{-1}\log\left|I_{0}(j,k)\right|\leqslant-\max_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in D}\Bigl(u\ln(\hat{x})+v\ln(\hat{y})\Bigr),

(9.8)

\limsup_{k}\|k\|^{-1}\log\left|I_{1}(j,k)\right|\leqslant-\max_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in D,\,\hat{x}\leqslant x_{d}}\!\Bigl(u\ln(\hat{x})+v\ln(\hat{y})\Bigr).

and

(9.9)

\limsup_{k}\|k\|^{-1}\log\left|I_{2}(j,k)\right|\leqslant-\max_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in D,\,\hat{y}\leqslant y_{d}}\!\Bigl(u\ln(\hat{x})+v\ln(\hat{y})\Bigr).

Proof.

Indeed, the definition of
I_{2}(j,k)
and Cauchy’s inequality give, for any
j,k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
and
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\overset{\circ}{D}
with
\hat{y}<y_{d}
,

|I_{2}(j,k)|\leqslant M(\hat{x},\hat{y})\hat{x}^{-k_{1}}\hat{y}^{-k_{2}},

with

M(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\max\{|(\phi_{2}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(0,y)(1-P(x,y))^{-1}|:\penalty\ (x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2},|x|=\hat{x},|y|=\hat{y}\}.

This proves that as
\|k\|\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w=(u,v)
,

\limsup_{k}\|k\|^{-1}\log\left|I_{2}(j,k)\right|\leqslant-\sup_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\overset{\circ}{D}:\hat{y}<y_{g}}\bigl(u\log(\hat{x})+v\log(\hat{y})\bigr)\\
=-\max_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in D,\,\hat{y}\leqslant y_{d}}\bigl(u\log(\hat{x})+v\log(\hat{y})\bigr)

where the second relation holds because the function
(x,y)\to u\ln x+v\ln y
is continuous on
D
. Relation (
9.9
) is therefore proved.
The proof of (
9.7
) and (
9.8
) is quite similar.
∎

9.4.
Exact asymptotic behavior of
I_{1}(jk)
as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty

To get the exact asymptotic of
I_{1}(jk)
as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty
we consider first the following preliminary results.

Lemma 9.3
.

Under the assumption (A1), for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
, the function

x\to u\ln(x)+v\ln(Y_{2}(x))

is strictly increasing in the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},x_{D}(w)]
and strictly decreasing in the line segment
[x_{D}(w),x^{**}_{P}]
.

Proof.

Indeed, recall that under our assumptions, the function
(\alpha,\beta)\to\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)=P(e^{\alpha},e^{\beta})
is strictly convex and finite in a neighborhood of the set
\tilde{D}=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:\penalty\ P(e^{\alpha},e^{\beta})\leqslant 1\}
, and that for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
, the point
(\alpha_{D}(w),\beta_{D}(w)=(\ln(x_{D}(w)),\ln(y_{D}(w)))
(see Definition
2.6.1
) is an only point on the boundary of the set
\tilde{D}
, where the function
(\alpha,\beta)\to u\alpha+v\beta
achieves its maximum over
\tilde{D}
.

Consider the line segment
[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{R}:\penalty\ \inf_{\beta\in\mathbb{R}}\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)\leqslant 1\}
. We have, see the proof of Lemma
4.1
,
\alpha^{*}_{P}{=}x\ln(x^{*}_{P})
,
\alpha^{**}_{P}{=}\ln(x^{**}_{P})
, and that for any
\alpha\in]\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}[
and
\beta_{2}(\alpha)=\ln(Y_{2}(e^{\alpha}))
, we have

\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta_{2}(\alpha))=1\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\beta}\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta_{2}(\alpha))>0.

Since the function
\tilde{P}
is strictly convex, by the implicit function theorem, it follows that the function
\beta_{2}
is also strictly convex on the line segment
[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]
.

Under our assumptions, the definition
2.6.1
of the mapping
w{\to}(\alpha_{D}(w),\beta_{D}(w)){=}(\ln(x_{D}(w)),\ln(y_{D}(w)))
gives that, if
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
,

\partial_{\beta}\tilde{P}(\alpha_{D}(w),\beta_{D}(w))\geqslant 0.

By the definition of the function
\alpha\to\beta_{2}(\alpha)=\ln(Y_{2}(e^{\alpha}))
, see the proof of Lemma
4.1
, it follows that,

\beta_{D}(w)=\beta_{2}(\alpha_{D}(w)),

and that
\alpha_{D}(w)
is an only point in the line segment
[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]
where the function

(9.10)

\alpha\to u\alpha+v\beta_{2}(\alpha)=u\alpha+v\ln(Y_{2}(e^{\alpha}))

achieves its maximum over
[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]
. Since the function
\beta_{2}
is strictly convex on
[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]
, the function (
9.10
) is also strictly convex on
[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha^{**}_{P}]
, and consequently, it is strictly increasing on the segment
[\alpha^{*}_{P},\alpha_{D}(w)]
and strictly decreasing on the line segment
[\alpha_{D}(w),\alpha^{**}_{P}]
. Since the function
x\to\ln(x)
is strictly increasing on
]0,+\infty[
, this proves that the function

x\to u\ln(x)+v\ln(Y_{2}(x))
is strictly increasing on the line segment
[x^{*}_{P},x_{D}(w)]
and strictly decreasing on the line segment
[x_{D}(w),x^{**}_{P}]
.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

If condition (A1) is satisfied and let, for some
x_{0}\in]x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}[
,
{\varepsilon}_{1}>0
and
{\varepsilon}_{2}>0
, a function
(x,y)\to F(x,y)
be analytic in the polycircular set

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ |x_{0}{-}|x||<{\varepsilon}_{1},|Y_{2}(x_{0}){-}|y||<{\varepsilon}_{2}\}

and do not vanish at the point
(x_{0},Y_{2}(x_{0}))
. Then for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
such that
x_{D}(w)>x_{0}
and
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\overset{\circ}{D}
such that
x_{0}-{\varepsilon}_{1}<\hat{x}<x_{0}
and
|Y_{2}(x_{0})-\hat{y}|<{\varepsilon}_{2}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
,

(9.11)

\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{|x|=\hat{x}}\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{F(x,y)}{x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}(x_{0}-x)(1-P(x,y))}\,dx\,dy\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \frac{C_{1}}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}

with

(9.12)

C_{1}=\left.\frac{F(x,y)}{\partial_{y}P(x,y)}\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{0},Y_{2}(x_{0}))}\not=0

and

(9.13)

\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{|x|=\hat{x}}\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{F(x,y)}{x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}(x_{0}-x)^{2}(1-P(x,y))}\,dx\,dy\\
\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \frac{k_{1}C_{1}}{x_{0}^{k_{1}+1}(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}-\frac{k_{2}{C}_{2}}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}+1}}

with
C_{1}
given by (
9.12
) and

(9.14)

C_{2}=\left.\frac{F(x,y)\partial_{x}P(x,y)}{\left(\partial_{y}P(x,y)\right)^{2}}\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{0},Y_{2}(x_{0}))}.

Proof.

Before proving this lemma, remark that under our assumptions, by Lemma
4.1
,
Y_{2}(x_{0})>Y_{1}(x_{0})
and consequently, without any restriction of generality, we can assume throughout our proof that

{\varepsilon}_{2}<Y_{2}(x_{0})-Y_{1}(x_{0}).

Because of Assumption (A1), the function
(x,y)\to 1-P(x,y)
is analytic in a neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of the set
\Omega(D)
and does
not vanishes in its interior
\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D})
. Hence, for any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
, the function

(x,y)\to F(x,y)(x_{0}-x)^{-m}x^{-k_{1}}y^{-k_{2}}(1-P(x,y))^{-1}

is analytic in the polycircular sets
\{(x,y)\in\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D}),\,x_{0}-{\varepsilon}_{1}<|x|<x_{0},\,|Y_{2}(x_{0})-y|<{\varepsilon}_{2}\}
and
\{(x,y)\in\Omega(\overset{\circ}{D}),\,x_{0}<|x|<x_{0}+{\varepsilon}_{1},\,|Y_{2}(x_{0})-y|<{\varepsilon}_{2}\}
, and consequently, the function

(\hat{x},\hat{y})\to J_{m,k}(\hat{x},\hat{y})=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{|x|=\hat{x}}\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{F(x,y)\,dx\,dy}{x^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}(x_{0}-x)^{m}(1-P(x,y))}

is constant on the set
A_{-}=\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:\penalty\ x_{0}-{\varepsilon}_{1}<x<x_{0},\,|Y_{2}(x_{0})-y|<{\varepsilon}_{2}\}
and on the set
A_{+}=\{(x,y)\in\overset{\circ}{D}:\penalty\ x_{0}<x<x_{0}+{\varepsilon}_{1},\,|Y_{2}(x_{0})-y|<{\varepsilon}_{2}\}
. We denote

\displaystyle J_{m}(k)

\displaystyle=J_{m,k}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\quad\text{ for $(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in A_{-}$},

\displaystyle\hat{J}_{m}(k)

\displaystyle=J_{m,k}(\hat{x},\hat{y})\quad\text{ for $(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in A_{+}$.}

Remark now that for any
\hat{y}\in]Y_{2}(x_{0})-{\varepsilon}_{2},Y_{2}(x_{0})[
and
\delta>0
small enough, the point
(x_{0}-\delta,\hat{y})
belongs to the set
A_{-}
, the point
(x_{0}+\delta,\hat{y})
belongs to the set
A_{+}
and by the residue theorem, for any
y\in\mathbb{C}
with
|y|=\hat{y}
,

\int_{|x|=x_{0}+\delta}\!\frac{F(x,y)\,dx}{x^{k_{1}}(x_{0}-x)(1-P(x,y))}=\int_{|x|=x_{0}-\delta}\,\frac{F(x,y)\,dx}{x^{k_{1}}(x_{0}-x)(1-P(x,y))}-2\pi i\frac{F(x_{0},y)}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}

and

\int_{|x|=x_{0}+\delta}\frac{F(x,y)\,dx}{x^{k_{1}}(x_{0}-x)^{2}(1-P(x,y))}=\int_{|x|=x_{0}-\delta}\,\frac{F(x,y)\,dx}{x^{k_{1}}(x_{0}-x)^{2}(1-P(x,y))}\\
+2\pi i\left(\frac{\partial_{x}F(x_{0},y)}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}-\frac{k_{1}F(x_{0},y)}{x_{0}^{k_{1}+1}(1-P(x_{0},y))}+\frac{F(x_{0},y)\partial_{x}P(x_{0},y)}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}(1-P(x_{0},y))^{2}}\right)

Hence, for any
\hat{y}\in]Y_{2}(x_{0})-{\varepsilon}_{2},Y_{2}(x_{0})[
,

(9.15)

J_{1}(k)=\hat{J}_{1}(k)+\frac{1}{2\pi i\,x_{0}^{k_{1}}}\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy

and

(9.16)

J_{2}(k)=\hat{J}_{2}(k)-\frac{1}{2\pi ix_{0}^{k_{1}}}\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{\partial_{x}F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy\\
+\frac{k_{1}}{(2\pi i)x_{0}^{k_{1}+1}}\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy-\frac{1}{2\pi ix_{0}^{k_{1}}}\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)\partial_{x}P(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))^{2}}dy.

Due to Assumption (A1)(ii), the function
y\to P(x_{0},y)
is analytic in a neighborhood of the closed annulus
\{y\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ Y_{1}(x_{0})\leqslant|y|\leqslant Y_{2}(x_{0})\}
, satisfies the inequality

|P(x_{0},y)|\leqslant P(x_{0},|y|)<1

on the annulus
\{y\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ Y_{1}(x_{0})<|y|<Y_{2}(x_{0})\}
, and because of Assumption (A1) i) (we use here Proposition P7.5 of
[
24
]
), for any
y\in\mathbb{C}
with
|y|=Y_{2}(x_{0})
,

|P(x_{0},y)|<P(x_{0},Y_{2}(x_{0}))=1\quad\text{whenever $y\not=Y_{2}(x_{0})$}.

Since by Lemma
4.1
,

\left.\partial_{y}P(x,y)\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{0},Y_{2}(x_{0}))}>0,

this proves that for some
\delta>0
, the point
Y_{2}(x_{0})
is an only and simple zero of the function
y\to P(x_{0},y)
in the annulus
\{y\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ Y_{1}(x_{0})<|y|<Y_{2}(x_{0})+\delta)
. Since we assumed that
Y_{2}(x_{0})-{\varepsilon}_{2}<Y_{1}(x_{0})
, and the functions
y\to F(x_{0},y)
,
y\to\partial_{x}F(x_{0},y)
,
y\to F(x_{0},y)
are analytic in the annulus
\{y\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ Y_{2}(x_{0})-{\varepsilon}_{2}<|y|<Y_{2}(x_{0})+{\varepsilon}_{2}\}
, this implies that for
\delta>0
small enough, the functions

y\to\frac{F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}\quad\text{and}\quad y\to\frac{\partial_{x}F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}

are analytic in the set
\{y\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ Y_{2}(x_{0})-{\varepsilon}_{2}<|y|<Y_{2}(x_{0})+\delta,\,y\not=Y_{2}(x_{0})\}
and have at the point
Y_{2}(x_{0})
, a simple pole with the residue equal respectively to

\frac{-C_{1}}{(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{-\tilde{C}_{1}}{(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}.

with
C_{1}
given by (
9.12
) and

\tilde{C}_{1}=\left.\frac{\partial_{x}F(x,y)}{\partial_{y}P(x,y)}\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{0},Y_{2}(x_{0}))}.

Similarly, the function

y\to\frac{F(x_{0},y)\partial_{x}P(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))^{2}}

is analytic in the set
\{y\in\mathbb{C}:\penalty\ Y_{2}(x_{0})-{\varepsilon}_{2}<|y|<Y_{2}(x_{0})+\delta,\,y\not=Y_{2}(x_{0})\}
and has at the point
Y_{2}(x_{0})
, a pole of the second order with the residue equal to

\frac{C}{(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}-\frac{k_{2}C_{2}}{(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}+1}}

with
C_{2}
given by (
9.14
) and some constant
C\in\mathbb{C}
does not depending on
k_{2}
. By the residue theorem, it follows that for any
Y_{2}(x_{0})-{\varepsilon}_{2}<\hat{y}<Y_{2}(x_{0})
and
Y_{2}(x_{0})<y_{\delta}<Y_{2}(x_{0})+\delta)

\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy=\frac{2\pi iC_{1}}{(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}+\int_{\|y\|=y_{\delta}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy,

\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{\partial_{x}F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy=\frac{2\pi i\tilde{C}_{1}}{(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}+\int_{\|y\|=y_{\delta}}\frac{\partial_{x}F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy

and

\int_{\|y\|=\hat{y}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)\partial_{x}P(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))^{2}}dy\penalty\ =\penalty\ -2\pi i\left(\frac{C}{(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}-\frac{k_{2}C_{2}}{(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}+1}}\right)+\int_{\|y\|=y_{\delta}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)\partial_{x}P(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))^{2}}dy

Using these relations at the right hand side of (
9.15
) and (
9.16
) we obtain

(9.17)

J_{1}(k)=\frac{C_{1}}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}+\frac{1}{(2\pi i)}\int_{\|y\|=y_{\delta}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy+\hat{J}_{1}(k)

and

(9.18)

\displaystyle J_{2}(k)

\displaystyle=\frac{k_{1}C_{1}}{x_{0}^{k_{1}+1}(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}-\frac{k_{2}C_{2}}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}+1}}+\frac{\tilde{C}}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}}\lx@nobreakspace

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi ix_{0}^{k_{1}}}\int_{\|y\|=y_{\delta}}\frac{\partial_{x}F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy+\frac{k_{1}}{(2\pi i)x_{0}^{k_{1}+1}}\int_{\|y\|=y_{\delta}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi ix_{0}^{k_{1}}}\int_{\|y\|=y_{\delta}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)\partial_{x}P(x_{0},y)}{y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))^{2}}dy+\hat{J}_{2}(k)

with some constant
\tilde{C}
does not depending on
k
. Remark now that by the Cauchy inequality, for
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w=(u,v)
,

\displaystyle\limsup_{k}\|k\|^{-1}\log\left|\frac{1}{(2\pi i)}\int_{\|y\|=y_{\delta}}\frac{F(x_{0},y)}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}y^{k_{2}}(1-P(x_{0},y))}dy\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant-\bigl(u\log(x_{0})+v\log(y_{\delta})\bigr)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt<-(u\ln(x_{0})+v\ln(Y_{2}(x_{0}))),

and with the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
9.2
,

\limsup_{k}\|k\|^{-1}\log\bigl|\hat{J}_{1}(k)\bigr|\leqslant-\sup_{x_{0}\leqslant\hat{x}\leqslant x_{0}+{\varepsilon}_{1}}\bigl(u\ln\hat{x}+v\ln(Y_{2}(\hat{x}))\bigr)<-(u\ln(x_{0})+v\ln(Y_{2}(x_{0})))

where the last relation holds because under our assumptions,
x^{*}_{P}<x_{0}<x_{D}(w)
and by Lemma
9.3
, the function
x\to u\ln x+v\ln(Y_{2}(x))
is strictly increasing on
[x^{*}_{P},x_{D}(w)]
. Since for
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, as
\|k\|\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
,

\lim\|k\|^{-1}\log\left|\frac{F(x_{0},Y_{2}(x_{0}))}{x_{0}^{k_{1}}(Y_{2}(x_{0}))^{k_{2}}\partial_{y}P(x_{0},Y_{2}(x_{0}))}\right|=-\bigl(u\log(x_{0})+v\log(Y_{2}(x_{0}))\bigr)

this proves that as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
, the second and the third terms in the right hand side of (
9.17
) are negligible with respect to the first one. Hence (
9.11
) is verified, and with the same arguments, from (
9.18
) one gets (
9.13
).
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
9.4
and Theorem
2
, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 9.5
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
with
u>u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
, the following assertions hold

i)   If either one of the assertions (B0), (B1), (B3),(B4) holds or (B2) holds with
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
, then for any
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
,

(9.19)

I_{1}(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ {c}_{1}\,a_{1}\,\varkappa_{1}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}-1}(Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2}-1}

where

(9.20)

c_{1}=\left.\frac{\phi_{1}(x,y)-1}{\partial_{y}P(x,y))}\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))}>0

and
a_{1}>0
is given by (
2.41
).

ii)   If (B5) holds with
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
, then for
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
,

(9.21)

I_{1}(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \frac{c_{1}\,a_{3}\varkappa_{2}(j)k_{1}}{x_{d}^{k_{1}+2}(Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{k_{2}+1}}+\frac{\tilde{c}_{1}\,a_{3}\varkappa_{2}(j)k_{2}}{x_{d}^{k_{1}+1}(Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{k_{2}+2}}

where
c_{1}>0
is given by (
9.20
),

\tilde{c}_{1}=\left.\frac{\partial_{x}P(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))}{\left(\partial_{y}P(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\right)^{2}}\right|_{(x,y)=(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))}

and
a_{3}>0
is given by (
2.47
).

iii)   If (B6) holds, then for
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
,

(9.22)

I_{1}(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ {c}_{1}\,a_{5}\,\varkappa_{2}(j)x_{d}^{-k_{1}-1}(Y_{2}(x_{d}))^{-k_{2}-1}

where
c_{1}>0
is given by (
9.20
) and
a_{5}>0
is given by (
2.53
).

Proof.

Indeed, when either, one of the conditions (B0), (B1), (B3) or (B4) is satisfied, or (B2) holds with
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
, by the first assertion of Theorem
2
and using Proposition
7.13
, one gets that for some
{\varepsilon}>0
, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,x_{d}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
and has a simple pole at the point
x_{d}
with the residue

-a_{1}\varkappa_{1}(j)=-\varkappa_{1}(j)\left(\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))\right)^{-1}<0.

Since the function
(x,y)\to\phi_{1}(x,y)-P(x,y)
is analytic in a neighborhood of the set
\Omega(D)
, this implies that the function
(x,y)\to F(x,y)=(x_{d}-x)(\phi_{1}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to a neighborhood of the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega(D):\,|x|<x_{d}+{\varepsilon}\}
by letting

F(x_{d},y)=(\phi(x_{d},y)-P(x_{d},y))a_{1}\varkappa_{1}(j).

When either, one of the conditions (B0), (B1), (B3) or (B4) is satisfied, or (B2) holds with
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
, one has always
x^{*}_{p}<x_{d}=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
, and consequently, by Lemma
4.1
,
Y_{2}(x_{d})>Y_{1}(x_{d})
and by Lemma
4.1
,
\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{1}(x_{d}))=1
. Since the function
y\to\phi_{1}(x_{d},y)
is strictly increasing, it follows that
\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))>1
. Since by Theorem
2
,
a_{1}>0
, we obtain therefore

\displaystyle F(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))

\displaystyle=(\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x))-P(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))a_{1}\varkappa_{1}(j)=(\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x))-1)a_{1}\varkappa_{1}(j)>0.

By Lemma
9.4
, it follows that for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
such that
x_{D}(u)>x_{d}
, and as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
, (
9.19
) holds with
c_{1}>0
given by (
9.20
).
Since for
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{2}_{+}
the inequality
x_{D}(u)>x_{d}
is equivalent to the inequality
u>u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
, the first assertion of our lemma is therefore proved.

Suppose now that (B5) holds with
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
. Then, by Theorem
2
and using Proposition
7.13
, one gets that for some
{\varepsilon}>0
, the function
x\to H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to the set
B(0,x_{d}+{\varepsilon}){\setminus}\{x_{d}\}
and has a pole of the second order at the point
x_{d}
with

\lim_{x\to x_{0}}(x_{d}-x)^{2}H_{j}(x,0)=a_{3}\varkappa_{2}(j)>0

where
a_{3}>0
is given by (
2.47
).
With the same arguments as above, it follows that the function
(x,y)\to F(x,y)=(x_{d}-x)^{2}(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(x,0)
can be extended as an analytic function to a neighborhood of the set
\{(x,y)\in\Omega(D):\,|x|<x_{d}+{\varepsilon}\}
by letting
F(x_{d},y)=(\phi_{1}(x_{d},y)-1)a_{3}\varkappa_{2}(j)
and that the extended function
F

satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
9.4
with
x_{0}=x_{d}
and
F(x_{0},Y_{2}(x_{0}))=a_{3}\varkappa_{2}(j)(\phi_{1}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))-1)>0
. Hence, using (
9.13
) we obtain (
9.21
) with
c_{1}>0
given by (
9.20
). The second assertion of our lemma is therefore also proved.

The proof of the third assertion of our lemma is exactly the same as the proof the first assertion, with an only difference that now, one should use the fifth assertion of Theorem
2
instead of the first one.
∎

Remark finally that if we exchange the roles of
x
and
y
, then with the same arguments as above we obtain the following result.

Lemma 9.6
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
with
v>v_{D}(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})
, the following assertions hold

i)

If either one of the assertions (B0), (B1), (B5),(B6) holds or (B2) holds with
y_{d}<y^{**}_{P}
, then for any
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
,

(9.23)

I_{2}(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ {c}_{2}\,b_{2}\varkappa_{2}(j)(X_{2}(y_{d}))^{-k_{1}-1}(y_{d})^{-k_{2}-1}

where

(9.24)

c_{2}=\left.(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)\left(\partial_{x}P(x,y)\right)^{-1}\right|_{(x,y)=(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})}>0

and

(9.25)

a_{2}=\left.\left(\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\right)^{-1}\right|_{y=y_{d}}

ii)

If (B3) holds with
y_{d}<y^{**}_{P}
, then for
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
,

(9.26)

I_{2}(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \frac{c_{2}\,b_{3}\varkappa_{1}(j)k_{2}}{(X_{2}(y_{d}))^{k_{1}+1}y_{d}^{k_{2}+2}}+\frac{\tilde{c}_{2}\,b_{3}\varkappa_{1}(j)k_{1}}{(X_{2}(y_{d}))^{k_{1}+2}{y_{d}}^{k_{2}+1}}

with
c_{2}>0
given by (
9.24
),

(9.27)

a_{3}^{\prime}=\left.(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)\left(\frac{d}{dy}\phi_{2}(X_{1}(y),y)\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\frac{d}{dy}X_{1}(y)\right)^{-1}\right|_{(x,y)=(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})}>0

and some
\tilde{c}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
do not depending on
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
.

iii)

If (B4) holds, then for
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
,

(9.28)

I_{2}(j,k)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ {c}_{2}\,a^{\prime}_{5}\varkappa_{1}(j)(X_{2}(y_{d}))^{-k_{1}-1}(y_{d})^{-k_{2}-1}

with
c_{2}
given by (
9.24
) and

(9.29)

a^{\prime}_{5}=\left.(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)\left((1-\phi_{2}(x,y))\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\frac{d}{dy}X_{1}(y)\right)^{-1}\right|_{(x,y)=(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})}>0

9.5.
Proof of the assertions i) and ii) of Theorem
4

For
I_{1}(j,k)
the exact asymptotics as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
were obtained only in the case where
u{>}u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
. In the following lemma, we show that this inequality always holds when
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
. The definitions
3
-
6
give that the set
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is empty if either, one of the cases (B5), or (B6) occurs or, (B2) and
x_{d}{=}x^{**}_{P}
hold, we can assume that either one of cases (B0), (B1), (B3) or (B4) occurs or (B2) and
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
hold.

Lemma 9.7
.

If conditions (A1)-(A3) hold and if either, one of cases (B0), (B1), (B3) or (B4) holds, or (B2) and
x_{d}{<}x^{**}_{P}
hold, then for any
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,

(9.30)

u>u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})).

Proof.

Suppose first that (B0) holds. Then by Proposition
2.2
and the definition of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see (
2.33
) and (
2.34
)),

(9.31)

X_{1}(y_{d})<x_{d}=x^{**}<X_{2}(y_{d}),\quad Y_{1}(x_{d})<y_{d}=y^{**}<Y_{2}(x_{d}).

In this case
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=\{w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:u>u_{c}\}
where
w_{c}=(u_{c},v_{c})
is the only point in
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
such that

(9.32)

u_{c}\ln(x_{d})+v_{c}\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d}))=u_{c}\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))+v_{c}\ln(y_{d}),

or equivalently, such that

(9.33)

(\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))-\ln(x_{d}))u_{c}=(\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d}))-\ln(y_{d}))v_{c}.

Since by (
9.31
) ,
X_{2}(y_{d})>x_{d}
and
Y_{2}(x_{d})>y_{d}
, the last relation implies that

u_{c}>0
and
v_{c}>0
, and consequently, if
u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\leqslant 0
, one gets

u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\leqslant u_{c}<u,\quad\forall\;w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}.

When (B0) holds and
u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\leqslant 0
, our lemma is therefore proved.

Consider now the case when (B0) holds and
u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))>0
. For
\hat{w}=(\hat{u},\hat{v})=(u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})),v_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})))
, by the definition of the mapping
w\to(x_{D}(w),y_{d}(w))
, the point
(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
is the only point in the set
D
where the function
(x,y)\to\hat{u}\ln(x)+\hat{v}\ln(y)
achieves its maximum over the set
D
. Since in the case (B0),
(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\not=(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})
, it follows that for
\hat{w}=(\hat{u},\hat{v})=(u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})),v_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})))
,

\hat{u}\ln(x_{d})+\hat{v}\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d}))>\hat{u}\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))+\hat{v}\ln(y_{d})

or equivalently that

(\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))-\ln(x_{d}))\hat{u}<(\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d}))-\ln(y_{d}))\hat{v}.

Since
X_{2}(y_{d})>x_{d}
and
Y_{2}(x_{d})>y_{d}
, and since we assumed that
\hat{u}=u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))>0
, the last inequality shows that
\hat{v}=v_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))>0
and consequently,
\hat{v}=\sqrt{1-\hat{u}^{2}}
and

(\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))-\ln(x_{d}))\hat{u}<(\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d}))-\ln(y_{d}))\sqrt{1-\hat{u}^{2}}.

Finally, we have already proved that
u_{c}>0
and
v_{c}>0
, and in this case, relation (
9.33
) is equivalent to

(\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))-\ln(x_{d})){u}_{c}=(\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d}))-\ln(y_{d}))\sqrt{1-{u}_{c}^{2}}.

Since the function
u\to(\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))-\ln(x_{d})){u}
is strictly increasing and the function
u\to(\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d}))-\ln(y_{d}))\sqrt{1-{u}_{c}^{2}}
is decreasing on the segment
[0,1]
, the last two relations show that
u_{c}>\hat{u}=u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
, and consequently using the definition of
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
one gets
u>u_{c}>u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
for all
u\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
. If (B0) holds, Lemma
9.7
is therefore proved.

When either, (B1) holds, or (B2) and
x_{d}{<}x^{**}_{P}
hold, relation (
9.30
) follows directly from the definition of the set
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
(see Definition
4
and Definition
5
).

Consider now the case when (B3) holds. With definition
6
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=\{w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:\penalty\ u>0\}
and by Proposition
2.2
and the definition of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see (
2.33
) and (
2.34
)), one has
(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
. By Lemma
4.1
and the definition of the mapping
(x,y)\to w_{D}(x,y)
, it follows that

u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))=\frac{x_{d}\partial_{x}P(x_{d},y_{d})}{\sqrt{(x_{d}\partial_{x}P(x_{d},y_{d}))^{2}+(y_{d}\partial_{y}P(x_{d},y_{d}))^{2}}}\leqslant 0.

and consequently, for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
, (
9.30
) holds.

Suppose now that (B4) holds. Then, with the Definition
6
,
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
and by Proposition
2.2
and the definition of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see (
2.33
) and (
2.34
)) and using (
2.9
) one gets

(9.34)

X_{1}(y_{d})=x_{d}=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P},\quad y^{*}_{P}<y^{*}\leqslant 1<y_{d}=Y_{2}(x_{d})<y^{**}\leqslant y^{**}_{P},\quad(x_{d},y_{d})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12}.

By Lemma
4.1
and the definition of the mapping
(x,y)\to w_{D}(x,y)
, it follows that for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,

u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})=u_{D}(x_{d},y_{d})=u_{D}(X_{1}(x_{d}),y_{d})=\frac{x_{d}\partial_{x}P(x_{d},y_{d})}{\sqrt{(x_{d}\partial_{x}P(x_{d},y_{d}))^{2}+(y_{d}\partial_{y}P(x_{d},y_{d}))^{2}}}<0\leqslant u,

and consequently, (
9.30
) holds.
∎

As a straightforward consequence of Lemma
9.5
and Lemma
9.7
, one gets

Corollary 9.2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
4
, for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
w\in{\mathcal{W}}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
, (
9.19
) holds.

Now we will show that for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}
, as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
, the terms
I_{0}(j,k)
and
I_{2}(j,k)
are negligible with respect to
I_{1}(j,k)
. For this we will use the large deviation estimates for
I_{0}(k,j)
, and

–

the large deviation estimates of
I_{2}(j,k)
when

\limsup_{k}\frac{1}{\|k\|}\ln I_{2}(j,k)<\lim\frac{1}{\|k\|}\ln I_{1}(j,k),

–

the exact asymptotic of
I_{2}(j,k)
when

\limsup_{k}\frac{1}{\|k\|}\ln I_{2}(j,k)=\lim\frac{1}{\|k\|}\ln I_{1}(j,k).

To compare the limit
\lim_{k}\ln I_{1}(j,k)/\|k\|
with the large deviation estimates for
\limsup_{k}\ln I_{0}(j,k)/\|k\|
and
\limsup_{k}\ln I_{2}(j,k)/\|k\|
, the following lemma will be useful.

Lemma 9.8
.

If conditions (A1)-(A3) hold and let
w{=}(u,v){\in}{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
, if one of the assertions holds,

–

one of the conditions (B0), (B1) or (B3) holds,

–

(B2) and
x^{**}{<}x^{**}_{P}
hold,

–

(B4) holds and
u>0
,

then

(9.35)

\max_{(x,y)\in D,\,{y}\leqslant y_{d}}\!\Bigl(u\ln({x})+v\ln({y})\Bigr)>u\ln(x_{d})+v\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d})).

Proof.

Consider first the case when (B0) holds. We have, see the proof of Lemma
9.8
,
X_{2}(y_{d})>x_{d}
and
Y_{2}(x_{d})>y_{d}
, the point
(w_{c}=(u_{c},v_{c})\in{\mathbb{S}}^{2}_{+}
satisfies (
9.33
) and
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=\{w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:u>u_{c}\}
. For
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
with
u>u_{c}
(and consequently also with
v<v_{c}
), it follows that

(\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))-\ln(x_{d}))u>(\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d}))-\ln(y_{d}))v

or equivalently,

(9.36)

u\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))+v\ln(y_{d})>u\ln(x_{d})+v\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d})).

Since the point
(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})
belongs to the set
\{(x,y)\in D:\penalty\ y\leqslant y_{d}\}
, this proves that for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
with
u>u_{c}
,

(9.37)

\max_{({x},{y})\in D,\,{y}\leqslant y_{d}}\!\Bigl(u\ln({x})+v\ln({y})\Bigr)\geqslant u\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))+v\ln(y_{d})>u\ln(x_{d})+v\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d})),

and consequently, (
9.35
) holds.

Consider now the case when (B1) holds, we have the relation

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=\{w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:\penalty\ u>u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\},

and, by Proposition
2.2
and the definition of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see (
2.33
) and (
2.34
)),

(9.38)

X_{1}(y_{d})<x_{d}=x^{**}=X_{2}(y_{d}),\quad Y_{1}(x_{d})<y_{d}=y^{**}=Y_{2}(x_{d})

and
(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))=(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
. Hence, for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,

(9.39)

x_{D}(w)>x_{d}=X_{2}(y_{d})\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad y_{D}(w)<y_{d}=Y_{2}(x_{d}).

The last relations show that the point
(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
belongs to the set
\{(x,y)\in D:\penalty\ y\leqslant y_{d}\}
and is not equal to
(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
. Since the point
(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
is an only point in
D
where the function
(x,y)\to u\ln(x)+x\ln(y)
achieves its maximum over
D
, it follows that

(9.40)

\max_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in D,\,\hat{y}\leqslant y_{d}}\!\Bigl(u\ln(\hat{x})+v\ln(\hat{y})\Bigr)=u\ln(x_{D}(w))+v\ln(y_{D}(w))>u\ln(x_{d})+v\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d})),

and consequently, when the assertion (B1) holds, relation (
9.35
) is also proved.

Consider now the case when (B2) and
x_{d}<x^{**}_{P}
hold. By Proposition
2.2
and the definition of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see (
2.33
) and (
2.34
)),

(9.41)

x_{d}=x^{**}>X_{2}(y_{d}),\quad y_{d}=y^{**}>Y_{2}(x_{d}),\quad(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d})),\,(X_{2}(y_{d}),y_{d})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22},

and with the definition of
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=\{w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:\penalty\ u>u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\}.

Hence, in this case, for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,

x_{D}(w)>x_{d}>X_{2}(y_{d})\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad y_{D}(w)<Y_{2}(x_{d})<y_{d}.

These relations show that the point
(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
belongs to the set
\{(x,y)\in D:\penalty\ y\leqslant y_{d}\}
and is not equal to
(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
, and consequently, using exactly the same arguments as in the previous case, one gets (
9.35
).

Suppose now that (B3) and
y_{d}<y^{**}_{P}
hold. By Proposition
2.2
and the definition of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see (
2.33
) and (
2.34
)), we have

(9.42)

X_{1}(y_{d})=x_{d}=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P},\quad y_{d}=y^{**}=Y_{2}(x_{d}),\quad(x_{d},y_{d})=(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12}

and

{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=\{w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:\penalty\ u>0\}.

Since we assume that
y_{d}<y^{**}_{P}
and by (
2.9
),
y^{**}>y^{*}\geqslant y^{*}_{P}
, by Lemma
4.1
and the definition of the mapping
(x,y)\to w_{D}(x,y)=(u_{D}(x,y),v_{D}(x,y))
, from (
9.42
) it follows that

(9.43)

x_{d}=X_{1}(y_{d})<X_{2}(y_{d})\quad\text{and}\quad u_{D}(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))=u_{D}(x_{d},y_{d})<0.

Hence, in this case, for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,

(9.44)

u\ln(x_{d})+x\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d}))=u\ln(x_{d})+v\ln(y_{d})<u\ln(X_{2}(y_{d}))+v\ln(y_{d})\\
\leqslant\max_{({x},{y})\in D,\,{y}\leqslant y_{d}}\bigl(u\ln({x})+v\ln({y})\bigr)

and consequently, (
9.35
) holds.

Suppose now that (B3) holds and
y_{d}=y^{**}_{P}
. Then
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}=\{w=(u,v)\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}:\penalty\ u>0\}
and (
9.42
) holds, but now, instead of (
9.43
) one has

x_{d}=X_{1}(y_{d})=X_{2}(y_{d}),\quad y_{d}=y^{**}=y^{**}_{P}\quad\text{and}\quad\quad w_{D}(x_{d},y_{d})=(0,1).

Hence, in this case, for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
,

(9.45)

x_{D}(w)>x_{d}\quad\;\text{and}\;\quad y_{D}(w)<y_{d}.

These relations show that the point
(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
belongs to the set
\{(x,y)\in D:\penalty\ y\leqslant y_{d}\}
and is not equal to
(x_{d},Y_{2}(x_{d}))
and consequently, using exactly the same arguments as in the case (B2), one gets (
9.35
).

Consider finally the case when (B4) holds. In this case,
{\mathcal{W}}_{1}={\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
and by Proposition
2.2
and the definition of the points
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
(see (
2.33
) and (
2.34
))

(9.46)

X_{1}(y_{d})=x_{d}=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P},\quad y^{*}\leqslant 1<y_{d}=Y_{2}(x_{d})<y^{**}\leqslant y^{**}_{P},\quad(x_{d},y_{d})\in{\mathcal{S}}_{12}.

Hence, in this case, (
9.43
) holds and consequently, using exactly the same arguments as above, one gets (
9.35
) for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
with
u>0
.
∎

Now we are ready to complete the proof of the first assertion of Theorem
4
: Since the right hand side of (
2.75
) does not depend on
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
, it is sufficient to show that for any
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
and
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
, (
2.75
) holds as
\min\{k_{1},k_{2}\}\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
.

Consider first the case when one of the following assertion holds

–

one of the conditions (B0), (B1) or (B3) holds

–

(B2) and
x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
hold,

–

(B4) holds and
u>0
.

By using Lemma
9.2
and Lemma
9.8
, as
\|k\|\to+\infty
and
k/\|k\|\to w
, one gets

\displaystyle\limsup_{k}\|k\|^{-1}\log\left|I_{0}(j,k)+I_{2}(j,k)\right|

\displaystyle\leqslant-\max_{(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in D,\,\hat{y}\leqslant y_{d}}\Bigl(u\ln(\hat{x})+v\ln(\hat{y})\Bigr)

(9.47)

\displaystyle<-u\ln(x_{d})+v\ln(Y_{2}(x_{d})).

By Corollary

, for any

and

, as

and

,

(9.48)

and the definition of the point
