9.8.
Proof of the assertion vi) of Theorem
4

To prove the last assertion of Theorem
4
it is sufficient to show that for any
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
, (
2.84
) holds uniformly with respect to
w_{m}=m/\|m\|
in some neighborhood of
w
. For this we use Proposition 1 of
[
11
]
. In our setting, this result gives the following lemma.

Lemma 9.9
.

Suppose that the Assumption (A1) is satisfied and let
w=(u,v){\in}{\mathbb{S}}^{1}_{+}
and
{\varepsilon}{>}0
and a function
(x,y){\to}F(x,y)
be analytic in the polycircular set

(9.63)

\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ \bigl||x|-x_{D}(w)\bigr|<{\varepsilon},\;\bigl||y|-y_{D}(w)\bigr|<{\varepsilon}\},

and not vanishing at the point
(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
.
Then the integrals

(9.64)

I(m)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{|x|=\hat{x}}\int_{|y|=\hat{y}}\frac{F(x,y)}{x^{m_{1}+1}y^{m_{2}+1}(1-P(x,y))}\,dxdy,\quad m=(m_{1},m_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+},

are well defined and does not depend on the point
(\hat{x},\hat{y})
on the set

(9.65)

\{(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\overset{\circ}{D}:\penalty\ |\hat{x}-x_{D}(w)|<{\varepsilon},\quad|\hat{y}-y_{D}(w)|<{\varepsilon}\},

and as
\|m\|\to+\infty
, uniformly with respect to
w_{m}=m/\|m\|
in some neighborhood of
w
,

(9.66)

I(m)\penalty\ \sim\penalty\ \frac{F(x_{D}(w_{k}),y_{D}(w_{k}))\sqrt{w_{k}^{\perp}\cdot{\mathcal{Q}}(w_{k})w_{k}^{\perp}}}{\bigl(2\pi\|k\|)^{1/2}\|{\mathfrak{m}}(w_{k})\|^{-1}(x_{D}(w_{k}))^{k_{1}}(y_{D}(w_{k}))^{k_{2}}}

By Corollary
9.1
, for any
(\hat{x},\hat{y})\in\overset{\circ}{D}
, with
\hat{x}<x_{d}
and
\hat{y}<y_{d}
, we have

(9.67)

g(j,k)=\frac{1}{(2\pi i)^{2}}\int_{|x|=\hat{x}}\int_{|y|=\hat{y}}\frac{F_{j}(x,y)}{x^{k_{1}+1}y^{k_{2}+1}(1-P(x,y))}\,dxdy.

with

F_{j}(x,y)=L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{1}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(x,0)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-P(x,y))H_{j}(0,y).

Remark moreover that the set of directions
{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
is non-empty if and only if (B2) holds, and that in this case, for any
w=(u,v)\in{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
, one has

(9.68)

x_{D}(w)<x_{d},\quad\quad y_{D}(w)<y_{d}.

By Theorem
1
, it follows that for some neighborhood
V(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
of the point
(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the function
(x,y)\to F_{j}(x,y)=L_{j}(x,y)+(\phi_{1}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(x,0)+(\phi_{2}(x,y)-1)H_{j}(0,y)
is analytic in the polycircular set
\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{C}^{2}:\penalty\ (|x|,|y|)\in V(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))\}
, and by Proposition
7.13
,

F_{j}(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))=\varkappa_{(x_{D}(w),y_{D}(w))}(j)>0,\quad\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}.

For any
w{\in}{\mathcal{W}}_{0}
and
j{\in}\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{{\setminus}}E_{0}
, the conditions of Lemma
9.9
are therefore satisfied with
F{=}F_{j}
, and consequently relation (
2.84
) holds uniformly with respect to
w_{k}{=}k/\|k\|
in some neighborhood of
w
.

Appendix A
Relations with Positive Recurrence and Transience Conditions

This section discusses the conditions (B0)-(B7) defining our classification with transience and positive recurrence conditions of of these random walks. As we will see, the regions of our classification are in fact not defined in terms of positive recurrence or transience. Transience and positive recurrence are possible in several regions.

Throughout this section,
\mu_{0}
,
\mu_{1}
or
\mu_{2}
are assumed to be stochastic, i.e. probability distributions.
Before getting these results, note that Assumption (A1)‘(ii) is equivalent to the usual Cramer’s condition for the distribution of the jumps of
(S(n))
.

(\alpha,\beta)\to\tilde{P}(\alpha,\beta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}P(e^{\alpha},e^{\beta})=\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})}\mu(k)e^{\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2}}.

It is satisfied if and only if the function
\tilde{P}
is finite in a neighborhood of the set
\tilde{D}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\{(\alpha,\beta){\in}\mathbb{R}^{2}:\penalty\ P(e^{\alpha},e^{\beta}){\leqslant}1\}
. Similarly, Assumption (A3) (ii) is satisfied if and only if the generating functions

\tilde{\phi}_{i}(\alpha,\beta)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{\rm def.}}}{{=}}\phi_{1}(e^{\alpha},e^{\beta})=\sum_{k=(k_{1},k_{2})}\mu_{i}(k)e^{\alpha k_{1}+\beta k_{2}},\quad i{\in}\{0,1,2\},

are finite in a neighborhood of
\tilde{D}
. Since
(0,0){\in}\tilde{D}
, all jumps of the random walk
(Z(n))
are in particular integrable.

Remark furthermore that because of Assumptions (A1) (ii) and (A3) (iv), the mean jumps

(M_{1},M_{2})=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}j\mu(j),\;(M^{1}_{1},M^{1}_{2})=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}j\mu_{1}(j)\quad and\quad(M^{2}_{1},M^{2}_{2})=\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}j\mu_{1}(j)

are non zero,
M^{1}_{2}{>}0
and
M^{2}_{1}{>}0
. Moreover, since with Assumption (A1) (iii), the sets
D{\cap}D_{1}
and
D{\cap}D_{2}
have a non-empty interior, one has also
M_{1}M^{1}_{2}{\not=}M_{2}M^{1}_{1}
and
M_{2}M^{2}_{1}{\not=}M_{1}M^{2}_{2}
.

Under our assumptions, the necessary and sufficient conditions of positive recurrence and transience for the Markov chain
(Z(n))
, are given in the next proposition. See Theorem 3.3.1 of
[
7
]
for example.

Proposition A.1
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3) then, the following assertions hold for the Markov chain
(Z(n))
on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{2}
.

(1)

Positive recurrence. If and only if one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(R0)

M_{1}{<}0
,
M_{2}{<}0
,
M_{1}M^{1}_{2}{<}M_{2}M^{1}_{1}
and
M_{2}M^{2}_{1}{<}M_{1}M^{2}_{2}
;

(R1)

M_{2}{<}0
,
M_{1}{\geqslant}0
and
M_{1}M^{1}_{2}{<}M_{2}M^{1}_{1}
;

(R2)

M_{1}{<}0
,
M_{2}{\geqslant}0
and
M_{2}M^{2}_{1}{<}M_{1}M^{2}_{2}
.

(2)

Transience. If one of the following conditions is satisfied:

(T0)

M_{1}{>}0
and
M_{2}{>}0
;

(T1)

M_{2}{<}0
and
M_{1}M^{1}_{2}{>}M_{2}M^{1}_{1}
;

(T2)

M_{1}{<}0
and
M_{2}M^{2}_{1}{>}M_{1}M^{2}_{2}
;

The proposition below studies the relation between conditions of transience and positive recurrence for the Markov chain
(Z(n))
and the location of the points defining the regions (B0)-(B7). We formulate the conditions (R0)-(R2) and (T0)-(T3) in terms of the location of the points
(x^{*},Y_{i}(x^{*}))
,
(X_{i}(y^{*}),y^{*})
, and
(x^{**},Y_{i}(x^{**}))
,
(X_{i}(y^{**}),y^{**})
,
i{\in}\{1,2\}
.
In Proposition
A.4
, the relation with the location of the dominant singularities
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
is analyzed.

We first establish a technical lemma.

Lemma A.1
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), then

(i)

M_{2}{<}0
if and only if
(1,1){\in}{\mathcal{S}}_{11}{\cup}{\mathcal{S}}_{21}
and
x^{*}_{P}{<}1{<}x^{**}_{P}
.

In this case,
Y_{1}(1){=}1
and

(A.1)

1=\begin{cases}x^{*}&\text{ if }M_{1}M^{1}_{2}<M_{2}M^{1}_{1},\\
x^{**}&\text{ if }M_{1}M^{1}_{2}>M_{2}M^{1}_{1}.\end{cases}

(ii)

M_{1}{<}0
if and only if
(1,1){\in}{\mathcal{S}}_{11}{\cup}{\mathcal{S}}_{12}
and
y^{*}_{P}{<}1{<}y^{**}_{P}
.

In this case,
X_{1}(1){=}1
and

(A.2)

1=\begin{cases}y^{*}&\text{ if }M_{2}M^{2}_{1}<M_{1}M^{2}_{2},\\
y^{**}&\text{ if }M_{2}M^{2}_{1}>M_{1}M^{2}_{2}.\end{cases}

Proof.

To prove
(i)
, it is sufficient to remark that

M_{2}{=}\partial_{y}P(1,1)
and that, according to the definition of the curves
S_{ij}
,
i
,
j{\in}\{1,2\}
,

{\mathcal{S}}_{11}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{21}=\{(x,y)\in\partial D:\penalty\ \partial_{y}P(x,y)\leqslant 0\}

and that by Lemma
4.1
, the points
(x^{*}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{*}_{P})){=}(x^{*}_{P},Y_{2}(x^{*}_{P}))
and
(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})){=}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))
are the only points on the boundary
\partial D
of the set
D
satisfying the relation
\partial_{y}P(x,y){=}0
.

Moreover, by relations (
2.15
) and (
2.17
), we have
{\mathcal{S}}_{11}{\cup}{\mathcal{S}}_{21}{=}\{(x,y)\in\partial D:\penalty\ y{=}Y_{1}(x)\}
.
Hence, when
M_{2}{<}0
, one has
Y_{1}(1){=}1
and therefore
\phi_{1}(1,Y_{1}(1)){=}\phi_{1}(1,1)=1
. By Corollary
4.1
, it follows that the point
1
is an end point of the line segment
[x^{*},x^{**}]
, and, consequently, with relations (
2.9
) and (
2.10
), we conclude that one and only one of the following cases occurs:

–

x^{*}_{P}<x^{*}=1<x^{**}\leqslant x^{**}_{P}
;

–

x^{*}_{P}\leqslant x^{*}<1=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}
.

By the implicit function theorem we have, for any
x{\in}]x^{*}_{P},x^{**}_{P}[
,

\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))=\left.\partial_{x}\phi_{1}(x,y)+\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(x,y)\partial_{x}P(x,y)/\partial_{y}P(x,y)\right|_{(y=Y_{1}(x))},

hence, when
M_{2}{<}0
, the relation

\displaystyle\left.\frac{d}{dx}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))\right|_{x=1}

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\phi_{1}(1,1)+\partial_{y}\phi_{1}(1,1)\partial_{x}P(1,1)/\partial_{y}P(1,1)\penalty\ =\penalty\ M^{1}_{1}-M^{1}_{2}M_{1}/M_{2},

holds, and, consequently, in a neighborhood of
x{=}1
, the function
x{\to}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}x))
is

–

non-decreasing if
M^{1}_{1}{-}M^{1}_{2}M_{1}/M_{2}>0
,

–

non-increasing if
M^{1}_{1}{-}M^{1}_{2}M_{1}/M_{2}<0
.

By Corollary
4.1
, we have the relation
\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)){<}1
, for all
x{\in}]x^{*},x^{**}[
, and
\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x)){>}1
, for
x{\in}[x^{*}_{P},x^{*}[\,\cup\,]x^{**},x^{**}_{P}]
. Hence, when
x^{*}_{P}{<}x^{*}{=}1{<}x^{**}{\leqslant}x^{**}_{P}
, the function
x{\to}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is non-decreasing in a neighborhood of
x{=}1
and
M^{1}_{1}M_{2}{-}M^{1}_{2}M_{1}{>}0
, and, when
x^{*}_{P}{\leqslant}x^{*}{<}1{=}x^{**}{<}x^{**}_{P}
, the function
x{\to}\phi_{1}(x,Y_{1}(x))
is non-decreasing in a neighborhood of
x{=}1
and
M^{1}_{1}M_{2}{-}M^{1}_{2}M_{1}{<}0
.

The first assertion of our lemma is proved. The second assertion is symmetrical by exchanging the roles of
x
and
y
.
∎

Proposition A.2
.

Under Assumptions (A1)-(A3), then

(i)

(R0) holds if and only if
(x^{*},Y_{1}(x^{*})){=}(X_{1}(y^{*}),y^{*}){=}(1,1)
,
x^{*}_{P}{<}1
and
y^{*}_{P}{<}1
;

(ii)

(R1)
holds if
"
and only if

(x^{*},Y_{1}(x^{*})){=}(1,1)
and
x^{*}_{P}{<}1
;

(iii)

(R2)
holds if
"
and only if

(X_{1}(y^{*}),y^{*}){=}(1,1)
and
y^{*}_{P}{<}1
.

(iv)

(T0)
holds if
"
and only if

(1,1){\in}{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
,
1{<}x^{**}
and
1{<}y^{**}
.

In this case,
Y_{2}(x^{**}){<}1
and
X_{2}(y^{**}){<}1
.

(v)

(T1) holds if and only if
(1,1){=}(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**}))
and
1{<}x^{**}_{P}
.

(vi)

(T2)
holds if
"
and only if

(1,1){=}(X_{1}(y^{**}),y^{**})
and
1{<}y^{**}_{P}
.

Proof.

The assertions (i)-(iii) and (v)-(vi) follow directly from Lemma
A.1
.

We have only to establish (iv). By using again the relations
M_{1}{=}\partial_{x}P(1,1)
and
M_{2}{=}\partial_{y}P(1,1)
, when
(1,1){\in}{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
, by relations (
2.9
), (
2.10
) and (
2.18
), and with Lemma
4.1
, we get the identities
(1,1){=}(1,Y_{2}(1)){=}(X_{2}(1),1)
and the relations

(A.3)

x^{*}_{P}<X_{1}(y^{**}_{P})\leqslant 1\leqslant x^{**}\leqslant x^{**}_{P}\quad\text{and}\quad y^{*}_{P}<Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P})\leqslant 1\leqslant y^{**}\leqslant y^{**}_{P},

and

(A.4)

\displaystyle M_{1}{>}0

\displaystyle\;\Leftrightarrow\;(1,1)\not=(X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P})\;\Leftrightarrow\;1<y^{**}_{P},

(A.5)

\displaystyle M_{2}{>}0

\displaystyle\;\Leftrightarrow\;(1,1){\not=}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))\quad\Leftrightarrow\;1<x^{**}_{P}.

Hence, when
(1,1){\in}{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
, we have
1{<}x^{**}
and
1{<}y^{**}
, the condition (T0) holds.

Conversely, if (T0) holds, then, according to the definition of the curve
{\mathcal{S}}_{22}
,

(A.6)

(1,1)=(1,Y_{2}(1))=(X_{2}(1),1)\in{\mathcal{S}}_{22}

and, by using relations (
A.4
) and (
A.5
), we get

1<x^{**}_{P},\quad\text{and}\quad 1<y^{**}_{P}.

By relation (
A.3
), we have always
1{\leqslant}x^{**}
and, if we assume that
x^{**}{=}1
, then, by relations (
A.6
) and (
A.3
), we obtain
x^{*}_{P}{<}1{=}x^{**}{<}x^{**}_{P}
. Consequently, by Lemma
4.1
and Corollary
4.1
, we have
Y_{1}(1){<}Y_{2}(1)
and
\phi_{1}(1,Y_{1}(1)){=}1
.

Since under our assumptions (A3) (ii) and (A3) (v), the function
y{\to}\phi_{1}(1,y)
is strictly increasing on the line segment
[0,Y_{1}(1)]
, these relations imply that
\phi_{2}(1,Y_{2}(1)){>}1
and, consequently,
(1,Y_{1}(1)){\not=}(1,1)
. Since this last relation contradicts relation (
A.6
), we conclude that
x^{**}{>}1
.

By exchanging the roles of
x
and
y
, the same arguments prove also that when (T0) holds, the relation
y^{**}{>}1
holds. By relation (
2.18
), we have

{\mathcal{S}}_{22}{=}\{(x,Y_{2}(x)):\penalty\ x{\in}[X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]\}{=}\{(X_{2}(y),y):\penalty\ y{\in}[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]\},

and, by Lemma
4.1
, the functions

X_{2}:[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]{\to}[X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]\text{ and }Y_{2}:[X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),x^{**}_{P}]{\to}[Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P}]

are strictly decreasing. It follows that
Y_{2}(x^{**}){<}Y_{2}(1){=}1
holds and, similarly,
X_{2}(y^{**}){<}X_{2}(1){=}1
. The assertion (iv) of Proposition
A.2
is proved.
∎

The following statement give relations between the conditions (B0)-(B7) and the conditions (R0)-(R2) and (T0)-(T2).

Proposition A.3
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3), then

(1)

both conditions (T1) and (T2) hold if and only if (B7) holds;

(2)

(B2) holds if (T0) holds.

(3)

If either (B3) or (B4) holds, then

–

(T2) is not possible;

–

(T1) holds if and only if
x^{**}{=}1
, and in this case,
Y_{1}(1){=}1
;

–

either (R0) or (R1) or (R2) holds whenever
x^{**}{>}1
.

(4)

If either (B5) or (B6) holds, then

–

(T1) is not possible;

–

(T2) holds if and only if
y^{**}{=}1
, and in this case,
X_{1}(1){=}1
;

–

either (R0) or (R1) or (R2) holds whenever
y^{**}{>}1
.

Proof.

The first and the second assertions of this statement follow from directly Proposition
2.2
and Proposition
A.2
.

If either (B3) or (B4) holds, by Proposition
2.2
,
1{<}Y_{2}(x^{**}){\leqslant}y^{**}
holds, and, consequently, by Proposition
A.2
, the case (T2) is not possible.

Still under the condition that either (B3) or (B4) holds. By Proposition
2.2
and with relations (
2.9
) and (
2.10
), we have
1{\leqslant}x^{**}{<}x^{**}_{P}
. Therefore, by Proposition
A.2
, (T1) holds if and only if
(1,1){=}(x^{**},Y_{1}(x^{**}))
holds. Since
P(1,1){=}1
, by Lemma
4.1
, either
Y_{1}(1){=}1
or
Y_{2}(1){=}1
holds, since, in our case, we have
1{<}Y_{2}(x^{**})
by Proposition
2.2
, it follows that when either (B3) or (B4) holds, the condition (T1) is satisfied if and only if
x^{**}{=}1
.

Under the condition that either (B3) or (B4) holds and that
x^{**}{>}1
holds. We have that either
M_{2}{\leqslant}0
or
M_{1}{\leqslant}0
, because otherwise (T0) and, consequently, (B2) holds. By Proposition
2.2
and with relation (
2.9
), we get the relations
1{<}Y_{2}(x^{**}){\leqslant}y^{**}{\leqslant}y^{**}_{P}
and
1{<}x^{**}{\leqslant}x^{**}_{P}
, and, consequently,
(1,1){\not=}(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))
and
(1,1){\not=}(X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P})
. Since the point
(x,y)=(x^{**}_{P},Y_{1}(x^{**}_{P}))
, resp.
(x,y){=}(X_{1}(y^{**}_{P}),y^{**}_{P})
) is the only point on the boundary
\partial D
of
D
for which
\partial_{x}P(x,y)>0
and
\partial_{y}P(x,y)=0
, resp.
\partial_{x}P(x,y){=}0
and
\partial_{y}P(x,y){>}0
) and, since
M_{1}{=}\partial_{x}P(1,1)
and
M_{2}{=}\partial_{y}P(1,1)
, we conclude therefore that, either
M_{1}{<}0
or
M_{2}{<}0
. If
M_{2}{<}0
, then, by Lemma
A.1
, and the assumption
x^{**}{>}1
, we get
x^{*}{=}1
and
M_{1}M^{1}_{2}{<}M_{2}M^{1}_{1}
. Similarly, if
M_{1}{<}0
, by Lemma
A.1
and since, by Proposition
2.2
,
1{<}Y_{2}(x^{**}){\leqslant}y^{**}
holds, we obtain
y^{*}{=}1
and
M_{2}M^{2}_{1}{<}M_{1}M^{2}_{2}
. We conclude that in the case when either (B3) or (B4) holds and
x^{**}{>}1
, one of the conditions (R0), (R1) or (R2) is satisfied.

The third assertion of Proposition
A.3
is therefore also proved. The last assertion follows by exchanging the roles of
x
and
y
.
∎

We can now establish the relations with the locations of the dominant singularities
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
.

Proposition A.4
.

Under the assumptions (A1)-(A3) the following assertions hold:

(i)

If the Markov chain
(Z(n))
is positive recurrent then
x_{d}{>}1
and
y_{d}{>}1
.

(ii)

If (T0) is satisfied then (B2),
x_{d}{=}x^{**}{>}1
and
y_{d}{=}y^{**}{>}1
hold.

(iii)

If (T1) is satisfied then

–

in the cases (B0)-(B2), relations
x_{d}{=}x^{**}{=}1{<}x^{**}_{P}
and
y_{d}{=}y^{**}{>}Y_{1}(1){=}1
hold;

–

in the cases (B3) and (B4), relations
x_{d}{=}x^{**}{=}1{<}x^{**}_{P}
and
y_{d}{=}Y_{2}(1){>}1
holds;

–

(B5) and (B6) do not hold.

(iv)

If (T2) is satisfied then

–

in the cases (B0)-(B2), relations
y_{d}{=}y^{**}{=}1{<}y^{**}_{P}
and
x_{d}{=}x^{**}{>}X_{1}(1){=}1
hold;

–

and (B3) and (B4) do not hold;

–

in the cases (B5) and (B6), relations
y_{d}{=}y^{**}{=}1{<}y^{**}_{P}
and
x_{d}{=}X_{2}(1){>}1
hold.

Proof.

Consider first the case when one of the cases (B0)-(B2) occurs. Then by Proposition
2.2
and according to the definition of
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
,

(A.7)

x_{d}=x^{**}>X_{1}(y^{**})\quad\text{and}\quad y_{d}=y^{**}>Y_{1}(x^{**}).

Hence, if we suppose moreover that (R0) holds, by Lemma
A.1
and using (
2.9
), we obtain

1=x^{*}<x^{**}=x_{d}\quad\text{and}\quad 1=y^{*}<y^{**}=y_{d}.

If we suppose that (R1) holds, then by Proposition
A.2
and using (
2.9
), and (
A.7
),

1=x^{*}<x^{**}=x_{d},\quad\text{and}\quad 1=Y_{1}(x^{*})<y_{d}.

And similarly, if we suppose that (R2) holds, then by Proposition
A.2
and using (
2.9
), and (
A.7
),

1=y^{*}<y^{**}=y_{d},\quad\text{and}\quad 1=X_{1}(y^{*})<x_{d}.

In the case when one of the cases (B0)-(B2) holds, the first assertion of out statement is therefore proved.

Suppose now that either (B3) or (B4) holds. In this case, by Proposition
2.2
and according to the definition of
x_{d}
and
y_{d}
, we have

(A.8)

x_{d}=x^{**}<x^{**}_{P}\quad\text{and}\quad y_{d}=Y_{2}(x^{**})>1.

Moreover, by relation (
2.10
), the inequality
1\leqslant x^{**}
always holds, and consequently, in this case, by Proposition
A.3
,
one of the conditions (R0)-(R2) is satisfied if and only if
1<x^{**}=x_{d}
. Hence, when either (B3) or (B4) holds, the first assertion of Proposition
A.4
is also proved. The same arguments (it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
) prove the first assertion of our proposition in the case when either (B5) or (B6) holds.

Furthermore, if the condition (T0) is satisfied, then by Proposition
A.3
, (B2) holds and consequently, by Proposition
A.2
and using (
A.7
) one gets
x_{d}=x^{**}>1
and
y_{d}=y^{**}>1
. The second assertion of Proposition
A.4
is therefore also proved.

Suppose now that the condition (T1) is satisfied. Then by Proposition
A.3
, the cases (B5) and (B6) are impossible, and by Proposition
A.2
,

(A.9)

x^{**}=1\quad\text{ and }\quad Y_{1}(x^{**})=1.

Moreover, if one of the cases (B0)-(B2) holds, then by (
A.7
), one gets
y_{d}=y^{**}>Y_{1}(x^{**})=1
, and if either (B3) or (B4) holds, then by (
A.8
) and using the second relation of (
A.9
), we obtain
y_{d}=Y_{2}(x^{**})>Y_{1}(x^{**})=1
. The third assertion of Proposition
A.4
is therefore also proved. The proof of fourth assertion is the same, it is sufficient to exchange the roles of
x
and
y
.
∎

Appendix B
Irreducibility Properties of the killed Markov Chain

Proof of Lemma
2.1

Since by the assumption (A1), the homogeneous random walk
(S(n))
with transition probabilities
{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(S(1)=k)=\mu(k-j)
is irreducible on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, there is a sequence of points
\ell^{k}(1)=(\ell_{1}^{k}(1),\ell_{2}^{k}(1)),\ldots\ell^{k}(N_{k})=(\ell_{1}^{k}(N_{k}),\ell_{2}^{k}(N_{k}))\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
\ell^{k}(1)+\cdots+\ell^{k}(N_{k})=k
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(S(N_{k})=k)\geqslant\mu(\ell_{1}^{k})\times\cdots\times\mu(\ell^{k}(N_{k}))>0
.

Let
N_{1}=1+\max\{N_{(-1,0)},N_{(1,0)},N_{0,-1)},N_{(0,1)}\}
. Then because of Assumption (A2), for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
j_{1},j_{2}\geqslant N_{1}
and
k\in\{(-1,0),(1,0),(0,-1),(0,1)\}
the sequence of points
m^{k}(1)=j+\ell^{k}(1),\ldots,m^{k}(N_{k})=j+\ell^{k}(1)+\cdots+\ell^{k}(N_{k})
does not exit from the set
({\mathbb{N}}^{*})^{2}
, and consequently

{\mathbb{P}}_{j}\bigl(Z(n)=j+k,\;\text{for some $n<\tau$}\bigr)\geqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{j}\bigl(Z(n)=m^{k}(n),\quad\forall n\in\{1,\ldots,N_{k}\}\bigr)>0.

This proves that

(B.1)

g(j,k)\geqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(Z(n)=k,\;\text{for some $n<\tau$})>0\quad\text{whenever \; $j_{1},j_{2},k_{1},k_{2}\geqslant N_{1}$}.

Consider now a random walk
(\hat{S}(n))
on
\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}
with transition probabilities

{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\hat{S}(1)=k)=\begin{cases}\mu(k-j)&\text{ for all $j=(j_{1},j_{2}),k\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}$ with $j_{2}>0$,}\\
\mu_{1}(k-j)&\text{ for all $j=(j_{1},j_{2}),k\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}$ with $j_{2}=0$.}\end{cases}

Remark that under our hypotheses (see Assumptions (A1) and (A3) (v) and (vi)) such a random walk is irreducible on
\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}
, and consequently, for any
j_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that
(k_{1},k_{2}+j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}
, there is a sequence of points
\ell^{j_{2},k}(1)=(\ell_{1}^{j_{2},k}(1),\ell_{2}^{j_{2},k}(1)),\ldots\ell^{j_{2},k}(N_{j_{2},k})=(\ell_{1}^{j_{2},k}(N_{j_{2},k}),\ell_{2}^{j_{2},k}(N_{j_{2},k}))\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}
such that for any
n\in\{1,\ldots,N_{j_{2},k}\}
,

(0,j_{2})+\ell^{j_{2},k}(1)+\cdots+\ell^{j_{2},k}(n)\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}},

and

{\mathbb{P}}_{(0,j_{2})}\bigl(\hat{S}(N_{j_{2},k})=(0,j_{2})+k\bigr)={\mathbb{P}}_{(0,j_{2})}\bigl(\hat{S}(n)-\hat{S}(n-1)=\ell^{j_{2},k}(n),\;\forall n\in\{1,\ldots,N_{j_{2},k}\}\bigr)>0.

Letting

N_{2}=1+\max\{N_{j_{2},k}:\penalty\ \;j_{2}\leqslant N_{1},\,(k_{1},k_{2})\in\{(-1,0),(1,0),(0,-1),(0,1)\},\;(k_{1},k_{2}+j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}\}

and using Assumptions (A2) and (A3) (iv), we obtain that for any
j=(j_{1},j_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
j_{1}\geqslant N_{2}
, and any
k=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\{(-1,0),(1,0),(0,-1),(0,1)\}
such that
j+k\in\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbb{N}}
, the sequence of points
m^{j,k}(1)=j+\ell^{j_{2},k}(1),\ldots,m^{j,k}(N_{j_{2},k})=j+\ell^{j_{2},k}(1)+\cdots+\ell^{j_{2},k}(N_{j_{2},k})=(\ell_{1}^{j_{2},k}(N_{j_{2},k}),\ell_{2}^{j_{2},k}(N_{j_{2},k}))
does not exist from the set
{\mathbb{N}}^{*}\times{\mathbb{N}}
, and consequently,

{\mathbb{P}}_{j}\bigl(Z(n)=j+k\;\text{for some $n<\tau$}\bigr)\geqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{j}\bigl(\hat{Z}(n)=m^{j_{2},k}(n)\;\forall n\in\{1,\ldots,N_{j_{2},k}\}\bigr)>0.

This proves that

(B.2)

g(j,k)>0\quad\text{whenever \, $j_{2}\leqslant N_{1},k_{2}\leqslant N_{1}+1$ and $j_{1},k_{1}\geqslant N_{2}$},

and with exactly the same arguments (it is sufficient to exchange the roles of the first and the second coordinates of the points in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
), one gets that for some
N_{3}>0
,

(B.3)

g(j,k)>0\quad\text{whenever \, $j_{1}\leqslant N_{1},k_{1}\leqslant N_{1}+1$ and $j_{2},k_{2}\geqslant N_{3}$}.

When combined together, relations (
B.1
), (
B.2
) and (
B.3
) show that for some
N_{0}\geqslant 0
,

(B.4)

g(j,k)>0\quad\text{for all \; $j,k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}$ with $\|j\|\geqslant N_{0}$ and $\|k\|\geqslant N_{0}$}.

Remark now that if
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
is such that
g(\ell,j_{0})>0
for some
j_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
\|j_{0}\|\geqslant N_{0}
, then also
{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}(Z(n)=j_{0},\;\tau>n)>0
for some
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and consequently, by (
B.4
) and using the inequality

(B.5)

g(\ell,k)\geqslant\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}\setminus\{0\}}{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}(Z(n)=j,\;\tau>n)g(j,k)\penalty\ \geqslant\penalty\ {\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}(Z(n)=j_{0},\;\tau>n)g(j_{0},k)

we obtain

g(\ell,k)\geqslant{\mathbb{P}}_{\ell}(Z(n)=j,\;\tau>n)g(j,k)>0\quad\text{for all $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}$ with $\|k\|\geqslant N_{0}$}.

Hence, for any
\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
we have either
g(\ell,k)>0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
\|k\|\geqslant N_{0}
, or
g(\ell,k)=0
also for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
\|k\|\geqslant N_{0}
, and in the last case, because of (
B.4
),
\|\ell\|<N_{0}
. Letting therefore
E_{0}=\{\ell\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}:\penalty\ g(\ell,k)=0\;\text{for all $k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}$ with $\|k\|\geqslant N_{0}$}\}
we obtain a finite subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
satisfying (
2.32
). Remark moreover that this set satisfies also (
2.31
) because if suppose that
g(\ell,j)>0
for some
\ell\in E_{0}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}E_{0}
, then using again the inequality (
B.5
) and the same arguments as above we would get
g(\ell,k)>0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
with
\|k\|\geqslant N_{0}
. Now, to complete the proof of our lemma, it is sufficient to notice that
g(0,k)>0
for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}{\setminus}\{0\}
, because under our assumptions, the random walk
(Z(n))
is irreducible on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}_{+}
, and consequently, the point
0=(0,0)
does not belong to the set
E_{0}
.
